  [G1.2] Tax status stated in the report is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report (page 20, Annual Allowance section) states: 'You have not yet triggered the MPAA, and you do not have a lower annual allowance.' MPAA triggered = false. Income tax band, annual allowance figure as applied to this client, and territorial profile are absent from the extracted model. The extraction notes these as not present in the document rather than extraction failures.
    File:     The supporting evidence files (provider policy documents and fact find) do not contain an explicit statement of the client's income tax band, territorial profile, or a fact-find field confirming MPAA status. The Aviva policy info (P4) shows pension input periods for 2020/2021 through 2025/2026 with £0 paid in recent years (except £756.14 in 2020/2021), with no indication of flexible access having been taken. The Standard Life document (P1) states the plan facilitates drawdown but does not confirm whether drawdown has been taken. The Fact Find (P17) records 'Highest rate of income tax paid (%) 0' for one client profile shown, which would suggest a basic or nil rate taxpayer, but this entry appears to be for the spouse (Kim) whose occupation and income details appear on that page. No document in the pack positively confirms or denies MPAA trigger status from a provider or fact-find source. The report's general Annual Allowance section (P19) states the allowance is £60,000 for 2024/2025 as a legislative statement, not a client-specific assertion.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts MPAA has not been triggered, but no supporting document (provider letter, fact-find field, or pension history) in the pack positively corroborates this — the claim is unsupported by external evidence.
      - The report is silent on income tax band and territorial profile; no fact-find entry unambiguously records John's tax band, meaning the tax treatment described for drawdown (marginal rate taxation) and contribution relief cannot be verified against a recorded tax position.
      - The Fact Find (P17) records 'Highest rate of income tax paid (%) 0' but this appears in a section relating to the spouse's employment details, creating ambiguity about whose tax rate it records.
    The MPAA assertion is a client-specific tax status claim that depends on whether the client has ever flexibly accessed a pension. No provider document in the pack confirms that no flexible access has occurred; the Aviva policy shows no recent contributions (P4) but does not address flexible access history, and the Standard Life document notes the plan facilitates drawdown without stating whether it has been used. The assertion therefore has no corroborating evidence in categories A, B, C or E and rests solely on the report's own statement — an [I] source cannot corroborate itself. The income tax band is not recorded anywhere in the pack for John: the fact-find entry showing 0% tax appears to relate to a different client profile context (P17, page 5-6, which records spouse details). Without a confirmed tax band, the report's statements about marginal rate taxation on drawdown withdrawals and contribution relief cannot be verified, which is relevant because the recommendation involves crystallising pension funds. The mid-growth rate label discrepancy (2.94% narrative vs 2.95% appendix) noted in the extraction is a separate finding already captured elsewhere; it does not affect this tax status assessment. The guard on generic legislative statements applies to the £60,000 annual allowance figure in P19 — that is not a client-specific assertion and is not flagged. The guard on Scottish vs rest-of-UK bands is relevant: territorial profile is absent, so a band comparison cannot be made, but the absence itself is the concern given that tax treatment is relied upon in the recommendation. Overall: the MPAA claim is unsupported by external evidence, and the income tax position is unrecorded, both of which matter to the recommendation (drawdown taxation, contribution relief). This is a Potential Concern.

    File:     Fact find [P5] shows JS's occupation as 'HGV Driver', employer as 'CDH Recruitment' (an agency). [P11] shows net basic monthly income of £1,200.00 for JS. The cashflow baseline [P3] shows JS employment income of £16,640 per annum. The report itself [P15] states JS receives £300 per week as an HGV driver. Kim's intended retirement age is stated as 66 in the report [P6/I], but no corroborating fact-find entry for Kim's retirement age is visible in the retrieved passages. No fact-find entry for Kim's employment details is present in the retrieved passages.

      - The report states the agency employer; the fact find [P5] names the employer as 'CDH Recruitment'. These are consistent in substance (CDH Recruitment is an agency) but the report does not name the employer, so no contradiction arises.

Three different implied annualised figures appear: £15,600 (report narrative), £14,400 (fact find monthly), £16,640 (cashflow model).
    There are three materially inconsistent figures for JS's employment income across the case file. The suitability report narrative states £300 per week net (£15,600 per annum). The fact find [P13] records net basic monthly income of £1,200.00 (£14,400 per annum, or £276.92 per week). The cashflow model [P3] uses £16,640 per annum (£320 per week). None of these three figures agree. Employment income underpins affordability and the cashflow projections that support the recommendation, so this is a material data inconsistency. The guard notes that a later file note may supersede fact-find data, but no dated file note is present in the pack to explain the variance — the three sources appear contemporaneous and unreconciled. Kim's intended retirement age of 66 is stated in the report but is unsupported by any category B or E evidence in the retrieved passages; the assertion rests solely on the report itself. While the value-match for '66' finds no match in the passages (nearest is 65 in [P14] which relates to Kim's current age per [P5]), the absence of corroborating fact-find evidence means the claim is unsupported. These are genuine data inconsistency and missing evidence concerns.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   C1 (John): employment income £300/week net as HGV driver; additional income £20/week from Sea Cadets volunteer role; State Pension £230/week (in receipt). C2 (Kim): employment income £1,100/month net; State Pension £230/week (projected). Cashflow modeller uses John's State Pension £11,973 p.a.; Kim's State Pension £11,973 p.a. from age 67. Household expenditure £1,700/month. Cashflow modeller retirement expenditure 'approximately £1,700' (context indicates monthly). Net assets £6,000 cash; no liabilities.
    File:     Fact find [P10]: Kim's basic income £1,200/month net (not £1,100); State Pension for Kim £230/month (not £230/week). Fact find total net monthly income £1,430 [P1]. Fact find total monthly expenditure £1,718 [P1] (not £1,700). Savings £6,000 [P13]. Cashflow tool [P11] shows Kim's State Pension age as 66. Report [P9/I] repeats £11,973 p.a. for Kim from age 67. Intelliflo baseline scenario [P12] shows age 67 in 2027 row consistent with Kim age 67 receiving state pension.
    Discrepancies:
      - Kim's employment income: report states £1,100/month net [P17], fact find states £1,200/month net [P10] — a £100/month difference exceeding 5% tolerance.
      - State Pension frequency for Kim in fact find: fact find records Kim's State Pension as £230/month [P10], while report states £230/week — these are materially different amounts (£230/month vs ~£998/month annualised at 52 weeks).
      - Household expenditure: report states £1,700/month; fact find calculated total is £1,718/month [P1] — a £18/month difference (approx 1%, within 5% rounding tolerance).
      - Fact find total net monthly income shown as £1,430 [P1], which at face value appears inconsistent with John (£300/week ≈ £1,300/month + £20/week ≈ £87/month) + Kim (£1,200/month per fact find) + Kim's state pension (£230/month per fact find) but the £1,430 figure may reflect only current employed incomes recorded at time of fact find rather than all sources.
      - Kim's State Pension age: report page 3 states age 66 [P17], cashflow modeller states age 67 [P9]; intelliflo proposed scenario [P11] shows State Pension Age 66 for Kim, creating a three-way conflict.
      - Cashflow modeller retirement expenditure: report quotes 'approximately £1,700' without specifying annual or monthly [P9]; if read as annual this conflicts materially with the £1,700/month objective stated elsewhere — extraction note resolves this as monthly but the ambiguity exists in the document.
    Two material discrepancies stand out. First, Kim's employment income: the report states £1,100/month net [P17] but the fact find records £1,200/month net [P10]. This is a £100/month difference (approximately 9%), exceeding the 5% tolerance. The gross-vs-net guard does not apply because both figures are stated as net. This is a data inconsistency between the suitability report and the supporting fact find — a finding on its own. Second, Kim's State Pension as recorded in the fact find appears as £230/month [P10], whereas the report states £230/week. These are materially different values (£230/month is roughly £53/week; £230/week is roughly £998/month). However, context suggests the fact find may have entered the weekly figure against a 'Monthly' frequency label in error, or the £230.00 monthly entry could relate to a different field. The fact find income table [P10] shows a 'State Pension / State Pension / Monthly / £0.00 / £230.00' row — the £230 in the net column under monthly frequency directly contradicts the report's £230/week assertion in value-when-annualised terms. This is a data quality/inconsistency issue between the two documents. The household expenditure discrepancy (£1,700 report vs £1,718 fact find) falls within the 5% rounding tolerance and is not a finding. The Kim State Pension age inconsistency (age 66 in narrative vs age 67 in cashflow) is flagged in the extraction's internal inconsistencies and is a separate internal inconsistency already identified. The cashflow total net monthly income of £1,430 in the fact find [P1] is also noteworthy: if Kim earns £1,200/month (fact find) and John earns £300/week (≈£1,300/month) + £20/week (≈£87/month), the combined income would be much higher than £1,430 — this figure appears to capture only Kim's income and John's current employed income without state pension, suggesting the £1,430 is a partial figure. This does not directly contradict the report but the fact find's own income total of £1,430 appears inconsistent with Kim's stated £1,200/month alone being below it, possibly because John's weekly income was not entered into the fact find in a way that was captured in the total. The primary finding is Kim's employment income mismatch (£1,100 report vs £1,200 fact find) and the State Pension frequency/amount discrepancy.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Client 1 (JS): in receipt of full State Pension of £230 per week (currently in payment). Client 2 (Kim/KS): will receive full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age. Page 3 states Kim will retire at age 66 (implying State Pension age 66). Cashflow assumptions on page 14 state Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67. JS's £230/week annualises to £11,960/year (£230 × 52); cashflow states £11,973 per annum for JS.
    File:     The intelliflo planning baseline scenario [P1] records STATE PENSION AGE as 66 for both JS and KS. The suitability report [P9] states Kim will retire at 66. The suitability report cashflow assumptions [P5] state 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67' — age 67, not 66. The fact find [P15] contains a State Pension Entitlement section but shows no figures for state pension amount or state pension age for either client. No external state pension forecast (e.g. BR19 or HMRC check) is present in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - Kim's State Pension age is stated as 66 in the narrative (page 3) and in the intelliflo baseline scenario [P1], but as 67 in the cashflow assumptions (page 14/[P5]) — a direct internal contradiction.
      - JS's £230/week state pension annualises to £11,960 per year (£230 × 52), but the cashflow assumptions state £11,973 per annum — a £13 difference that is not explained by rounding alone.
      - The fact find [P15] contains a State Pension Entitlement section with no figures populated — no corroborating evidence of amount or entitlement for either client from an independent source.
      - No BR19 or HMRC state pension forecast document is present in the file to corroborate the £230/week figure for either client.
    Two material issues arise. First, there is a direct internal contradiction in the report itself: page 3 states Kim will retire at age 66 and reach State Pension age at 66, while page 14 cashflow assumptions use age 67 as the point at which Kim receives her state pension. The intelliflo baseline scenario [P1] also records STATE PENSION AGE as 66 for KS, corroborating the page 3 figure and making the cashflow assumption of 67 an inconsistency that affects the modelling. The guard notes that state pension age and retirement age are different things, but here the report itself conflates them on page 3 ('intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully' — the same sentence that informs the state pension timing), and the cashflow tool separately records SPA as 66 in its own input screen. The divergence between page 3/cashflow tool (66) and cashflow narrative (67) is a genuine data inconsistency, not a retirement-age/SPA conflation guard situation. Second, the £230/week figure for JS annualises to £11,960 (£230 × 52), yet the cashflow uses £11,973 — a £13 shortfall that is not explained by rounding and represents a minor arithmetic inconsistency within the report's own figures. Third, and more broadly, the fact find's State Pension Entitlement section [P15] is blank, and no BR19 or HMRC forecast is on file. The report asserts full entitlement at £230/week for both clients without any corroborating external evidence. These combine to produce a Potential Concern: one internal contradiction that affects cashflow modelling (Kim's SPA 66 vs 67), one minor arithmetic mismatch on annual conversion, and an absence of any independent corroboration of the entitlement amounts.

Standard Life policy value (P15) confirms plan number D2301334000 and holder John Sullivan. Standard Life investment details (P16) confirm fund 'SL At Retirement Universal (PP10 Year) Pension Fd' at 100%. People's Pension policy info (P2–P10) confirms the policy number 6197175/PP and active contributions. The fact find (P1) records an Aviva Platform personal pension (AV2936864-001) with a value of £116,555.45 as at 23/07/2026, and a People's Pension (6197175/PP). No fact-find entry for Scottish Widows ZU4225575, Zurich P10026-719-001/DL or Standard Life D2301334000 is visible in the retrieved passages. No provider statement for Scottish Widows ZU4225575 appears in the evidence pack. The derived figures compute the five plan values sum to £116,998.47, not £110,000 or £106,000.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states combined pension value 'approximately £110,000' (page 3); the sum of the five individual plan values stated in the same report is £116,998.47 per derived figures; neither matches £110,000.
      - The report states pension funds 'approximately £106,000' (page 4 capacity for loss section); this differs from both the £110,000 figure on page 3 and the £116,998.47 arithmetic sum of the five plans.
      - The recommended solution table total transfer value of £110,185 (page 9) does not match either the page 3 narrative (£110,000), the page 4 narrative (£106,000), or the arithmetic sum of all five plans (£116,998.47), nor the sum of the three transferring plans (£110,185.60 rounds to £110,186 not £110,185).
      - Zurich charge on page 5 is stated as '0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin'; the switch charges table on page 11 states Zurich current annual recurring charge as 0.93% — these are materially inconsistent and cannot both be the correct total charge.
      - Standard Life charge on page 5 is stated as '0.52% fund charge'; the switch charges table on page 11 states Standard Life current annual recurring charge as 0.18% — these are directly contradictory.
      - The derived figures confirm EA5 (Standard Life) annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's (£3,002) — indicating the charge row for EA5 is computed on the wrong plan.
      - The derived figures confirm the EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% likewise implies Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's — the comparison resting on EA5 impact is unreliable.
      - The fact find (P1) records an Aviva Platform pension AV2936864-001 with value £116,555.45 as at 23/07/2026; the report's Aviva arrangement is SP50163762 with value £3,744.36 — different policy numbers and materially different values; no reconciliation is provided.
      - No provider statement for Scottish Widows ZU4225575 appears in the evidence pack — the reported value of £211.32 has no corroborating document.
      - The fact find retrieved passages show no entries for Scottish Widows ZU4225575, Zurich P10026-719-001/DL or Standard Life D2301334000 — three of the five arrangements are not listed in the visible fact-find pension schedule.
      - Standard Life transfer value per provider statement (P14) is £3,002.02; the report states £3,002 (£0.02 difference, minor rounding).
      - Kim's State Pension age: page 3 states retirement at 66; cashflow assumptions state state pension from age 67 — internally inconsistent within the report.
    Multiple material discrepancies are present. First, the three narrative figures for total pension value (£110,000 page 3; £106,000 page 4; £116,998.47 arithmetic sum; £110,185 recommended solution table) are all different and none reconciles with the others — this is a self-contradiction in a figure that drives the recommendation. Second, the Zurich and Standard Life charges are stated differently in two places within the same report (page 5 table vs page 11 switch table), with the values effectively swapped between the two providers, which is a material internal inconsistency bearing on the cost comparison. Third, the derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows are computed against Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's, making any cost comparison for Standard Life unreliable. Fourth, the fact find records an Aviva Platform policy AV2936864-001 valued at £116,555.45 (dated 23/07/2026) which does not match the Aviva policy SP50163762 (£3,744.36) in the report — these may be different plans, but no reconciliation is provided and the higher-value Aviva Platform plan is not accounted for in the report's existing arrangements. Fifth, no provider statement for Scottish Widows ZU4225575 exists in the pack, so the £211.32 value is unsupported by evidence. Sixth, three of the five arrangements (Scottish Widows, Zurich, Standard Life) do not appear in the retrieved fact-find pension schedule, meaning the fact-find does not visibly corroborate their inclusion. The Standard Life £3,002.02 vs £3,002.00 difference is within rounding tolerance and is not a finding. The fact-find Aviva Platform plan (AV2936864-001, £116,555.45) could represent the new recommended plan rather than an existing arrangement, but its presence in the fact find with no cross-reference in the report is unexplained. The guards on as-at date differences apply to individual plan values (Aviva £3,744.36 corroborated by P22; Zurich £103,439.24 corroborated by P18; Standard Life £3,002 corroborated by P14/P15) — these individual values are confirmed by provider statements. However, the aggregated totals and charge figures within the report itself are internally contradictory, which is a finding independent of any external evidence.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report contains multiple internally inconsistent figures: (1) Zurich charge stated as 0.18% + £2.12/month on page 5 but 0.93% total on page 11; (2) Standard Life charge stated as 0.52% on page 5 but 0.18% on page 11; (3) combined pension value stated as ~£110,000 on page 3, ~£106,000 on page 4, and individual plan values sum to £116,998.47 (per derived figures) or £117,998.47 per extraction; (4) Kim's State Pension age stated as 66 on page 3 but 67 in cashflow assumptions on page 14; (5) Standard Life narrative (page 8) says existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than new solution but page 11 table shows new solution is 0.26% more expensive; (6) mid-growth rate stated as 2.94% on page 11 but 2.95% on page 18; (7) volatility chart caption on page 13 swaps Aviva/Zurich letter assignments versus performance chart caption; (8) total transfer value shown as £110,185 on page 9 but individual values sum to £110,185.60; (9) EA5 charge rows in the charges table appear to be computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's.
    File:     This requirement compares the report against itself. No external supporting documents were searched. The derived figures confirm: the 5 arrangements sum to £116,998.47 (not ~£110,000 or ~£106,000); EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002); EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% likewise implies Zurich's fund value; NP1 ongoing charge of £484.81 at 0.44% implies ~£110,184 (consistent with transfer total, not full fund total).
    Discrepancies:
      - Zurich (EA4) charge: 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin (page 5) vs 0.93% total annual recurring charge (page 11)
      - Standard Life (EA5) charge: 0.52% fund charge (page 5) vs 0.18% current annual recurring charge (page 11)
      - Combined pension value: ~£110,000 (page 3) vs ~£106,000 (page 4) vs £116,998.47 sum of individual plan values (derived) vs £110,185 transfer total (page 9)
      - Kim's State Pension age: 66 (page 3 retirement narrative) vs 67 (page 14 cashflow assumptions)
      - Standard Life cost comparison direction: existing plan 0.08% more expensive than recommended (page 8 narrative) vs new solution 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life existing (page 11 table) — directions are contradictory
      - Mid-growth rate: 2.94% (page 11 narrative) vs 2.95% (page 18 appendix table)
      - Performance chart caption: A=Aviva, D=Zurich (page 13) vs volatility chart caption: D=Aviva, A=Zurich (page 13) — letter assignments swapped
      - Total transfer value: £110,185 (page 9 table) vs £110,185.60 (sum of EA3+EA4+EA5 individual values)
      - EA5 charge rows computed on ~£103,439 (Zurich's fund value) rather than £3,002 (Standard Life's fund value) — wrong base used for EA5 charge arithmetic
    This requirement assesses internal consistency of the report against itself; no external evidence is needed or relevant. Multiple material inconsistencies are confirmed. The most serious are: (1) Zurich's charge is stated two ways that cannot both be correct as a total — 0.18%+£2.12/month vs 0.93%; the derived figures confirm 0.93% is arithmetically consistent with Zurich's fund value (£961.98 ÷ £103,439 ≈ 0.93%), meaning the page 5 figure of 0.18% understates the total charge used in the switch analysis. (2) Standard Life's charge is also contradictory — 0.52% on page 5 vs 0.18% on page 11 — and the derived figures show EA5's charge rows (£186.19 at 0.18% and £268.94 at 0.26%) imply a fund value of ~£103,439, i.e. Zurich's value not Standard Life's £3,002, meaning the EA5 charge comparison is computed on the wrong plan's fund. This directly undermines the cost-benefit analysis for the Standard Life switch. (3) The combined pension value is inconsistent across three internal references (~£110,000, ~£106,000, and £116,998.47 sum of individual plans) — none agree. (4) Kim's State Pension age (66 vs 67) is inconsistent between narrative and cashflow model inputs, affecting projected retirement income. (5) The Standard Life cost comparison direction is contradicted between narrative and table — the narrative says the existing plan costs more, the table shows the new solution costs more. These are not date-driven discrepancies (no guard applies) and they are not rounding differences (directions differ, fund bases differ). The 2.94%/2.95% mid-growth rate difference and the £110,185/£110,185.60 rounding are low-severity presentation issues. The Aviva/Zurich chart label swap is a moderate labelling error. The material charge inconsistencies, the wrong fund base for EA5, the pension total inconsistency and the Kim SPA discrepancy are all high-severity findings that affect figures driving the recommendation and cashflow model.

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the following income figures: Alan earns £300/week net (employment), £20/week net (Sea Cadets voluntary role), and receives full State Pension of £230/week. Kim earns approximately £1,100/month net. Kim's projected State Pension is £230/week. For cashflow modelling (page 14), Alan's State Pension is £11,973 per annum and Kim's State Pension is £11,973 per annum from age 67. The report also states Kim will retire at age 66 (page 3) but the cashflow assumes she receives State Pension from age 67 (page 14). The report does not provide a total monthly income figure, nor does it convert income streams to a common frequency within the narrative.
    File:     The fact find (B) records Kim's income as: Basic Income (employed, CDH Recruitment) Monthly Net £1,200.00; State Pension Monthly Net £230.00. Total Net Monthly Income shown as £1,430.00. Alan's employment income is shown as £300/week net (corroborated). The £20/week Sea Cadets income does not appear in the fact find income section. The fact find records State Pension as £230.00 monthly (not weekly). There is no gross income figure in the fact find for Kim; Net Basic Monthly Income is £1,200.00 (vs report's £1,100/month). The annual equivalent of £230/week State Pension is £11,960 (230 x 52), but the cashflow uses £11,973/annum — a £13 difference that is within rounding tolerance. No fact-find figure of £11,973 p.a. is evidenced. The fact find total monthly income of £1,430 implies Alan's income contributes £230/month (i.e. £230 monthly State Pension for Alan only) and Kim's £1,200, which is inconsistent with the report's stated £300/week for Alan plus £20/week Sea Cadets.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Kim earns £1,100/month net; fact find records Kim's Net Basic Monthly Income as £1,200.00 — a difference of £100/month.
      - Report states £20/week income from Sea Cadets voluntary role for Alan; no corresponding entry appears in the fact find income section.
      - Report states Alan's State Pension is £230/week; fact find income table records State Pension as £230.00 monthly — frequency mismatch (£230/week = £11,960/year vs £230/month = £2,760/year).
      - Report's cashflow uses £11,973 per annum for both Alan and Kim's State Pension; £230/week annualises to £11,960 at 52 weeks — £13 difference (within tolerance, but the cashflow figure is not directly traceable to any fact-find entry).
      - Report states Kim will reach State Pension age and retire at 66 (page 3); cashflow assumptions state Kim receives State Pension from age 67 (page 14) — direct internal inconsistency.
      - Fact find total net monthly income is £1,430; if Alan earns £300/week (£1,300/month equivalent at 52/12) plus £20/week Sea Cadets (£87/month) plus Kim £1,100/month, the combined monthly total would be approximately £2,487 — far exceeding the fact find's £1,430, indicating the fact find captures only Kim's income or the figures are inconsistent.
      - Report's combined pension value is stated as 'approximately £110,000' (page 3) and 'approximately £106,000' (page 4); derived sum of five individual plan values is £116,998.47 (or £117,998.47 per extraction) — none of the three figures agree.
      - Zurich charge shown as 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin (page 5 existing arrangements table) vs 0.93% total current annual recurring charge (page 11 switch charges table) — materially inconsistent.
      - Standard Life charge shown as 0.52% fund charge (page 5) vs 0.18% current annual recurring charge (page 11) — directly contradictory.
      - EA5 (Standard Life) annual charge arithmetic: £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's value not Standard Life's (£3,002) — the charge row appears computed on the wrong plan.
      - EA5 impact: £268.94 at 0.26% also implies Zurich's fund value (~£103,439), not Standard Life's — same wrong-plan error.
      - Performance chart caption labels A=Aviva and D=Zurich; volatility chart caption immediately below swaps these to D=Aviva and A=Zurich — direct internal labelling contradiction.
      - Critical yield mid-growth rate stated as 2.94% in page 11 narrative vs 2.95% in page 18 appendix table.
      - Standard Life replacement analysis narrative (page 8) states existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than recommended solution; switch charges table (page 11) shows new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life existing — contradiction in direction of cost comparison.
    Multiple material discrepancies exist across frequency reconciliation and internal consistency.
    
    1. Kim's income: The report states £1,100/month net; the fact find records £1,200/month net — a £100/month difference that is outside the £1 tolerance and not explained by rounding. This is a direct contradiction between the report and supporting evidence.
    
    2. Sea Cadets income (£20/week): Not present in the fact find. The report asserts this income with no supporting document in the file. This is unsupported.
    
    3. State Pension frequency conflict: The fact find records Alan's State Pension as £230.00 monthly, while the report states £230/week. £230/month = £2,760/year; £230/week = £11,960/year. The cashflow uses £11,973/year — consistent with the weekly figure and within £13 rounding tolerance of 52 x £230. However, the fact find's monthly £230 figure would annualise to only £2,760, creating a significant discrepancy. The fact find figure appears to be an error in frequency labelling (it is likely weekly data entered as monthly), but as recorded it contradicts the report.
    
    4. Kim's State Pension age: Page 3 states age 66; cashflow page 14 states age 67. This is a direct internal inconsistency with no external guard to explain it. It affects retirement planning projections.
    
    5. Combined pension values: Three different figures appear — £110,000 (page 3), £106,000 (page 4), and the derived sum of £116,998.47 from individual plan values. None reconcile.
    
    6. Charge inconsistencies (Zurich and Standard Life): The existing arrangements table and the switch charges table give contradictory percentage charges for both Zurich (0.18%+fixed vs 0.93%) and Standard Life (0.52% vs 0.18%). The EA5 charge arithmetic is computed on Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's — confirming a wrong-plan error that undermines the cost comparison.
    
    7. Standard Life narrative vs table: The narrative says the existing plan is more expensive; the table shows the opposite direction. This is a material internal contradiction.
    
    8. Chart labelling: The performance and volatility chart captions swap the letter assignments for Aviva and Zurich — a direct internal inconsistency.
    
    9. Critical yield rate: 2.94% vs 2.95% — a 0.01pp difference within low severity rounding tolerance.
    
    The weekly/monthly guard does not explain items 1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 7 or 8. Items 3 and 9 are partially explained by the rounding guard (item 9 fully, item 3 partially — the weekly vs monthly labelling in the fact find remains a genuine discrepancy even if the annual cashflow figure rounds correctly from the weekly rate). The total monthly income figure in the fact find (£1,430) is also inconsistent with the sum of incomes the report describes for both clients, though the fact find may only be capturing one client's income — this ambiguity is noted but the Kim income mismatch stands independently.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   Cashflow was modelled to age 100. Assumptions: general expenditure inflation 2%, savings inflation 2%, state pension inflation 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions growth 5%, surplus income saved 0%. John earns £300/week as HGV driver and £20/week from sea cadets; Kim earns £1,100/month. John state pension £11,973 p.a.; Kim state pension £11,973 p.a. from age 67. Retirement expenditure approximately £1,700/month. Cashflow includes Aviva transfer £3,744, Zurich transfer £103,439, Standard Life £3,002. Narrative states Kim plans to retire at 66 (State Pension age 66 per page 3), but cashflow assumption states Kim receives state pension from age 67. Critical yield table mid-growth rate labelled 2.94% in page 11 narrative and 2.95% in page 18 appendix table. Combined pension value stated as approximately £110,000 on page 3 and approximately £106,000 on page 4; sum of five individual plans is £116,998.47 (derived); recommended solution table shows £110,185 total transfer value.
    File:     Intelliflo planning file (proposed scenario) [P7] shows Kim's STATE PENSION AGE as 66 and JS's STATE PENSION AGE as 66. The cashflow modelling file [P1] states mid growth rate as 2.95% and inflation assumptions match report (2%, 2.5%). [P14] baseline scenario lists DC pension total opening value of £116,997 (People's Pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211, Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002). [P22] corroborates Kim's income £1,100/month, state pension figures, £1,700 retirement expenditure, and cashflow inputs of £3,744, £103,439, £3,002. [P7] shows New Aviva pension £110,185 in proposed scenario. Pension switch report [P15/P16] uses mid growth rate 2.95% consistently in the switch comparison tables. The intelliflo proposed scenario file [P7] shows Kim's state pension age as 66, contradicting the cashflow assumption in the report narrative that Kim receives state pension from age 67.
    Discrepancies:
      - Kim's State Pension age: report narrative (page 3) states age 66; cashflow assumptions (page 14) state she receives state pension from age 67; intelliflo planning file [P7] records Kim's STATE PENSION AGE as 66 — the cashflow assumption of age 67 contradicts both the narrative and the planning file.
      - Mid-growth rate label: report narrative (page 11) states 2.94%; critical yield appendix (page 18) states 2.95%; pension switch report [P1/P15/P16] consistently uses 2.95% — the 2.94% in the narrative is inconsistent with the modelling tool output.
      - Combined pension value: page 3 narrative states approximately £110,000; page 4 states approximately £106,000; sum of five individual plan values per derived figures is £116,998.47; intelliflo baseline [P14] shows DC pension total of £116,997; recommended solution table shows £110,185 (transfer value of switching plans only) — four different figures, none reconciling with another.
      - Cashflow modelling inputs include Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002 but do not explicitly include People's Pension £6,601.55 (retained) — ambiguous whether intentional or omission, and the total modelled differs from any stated combined figure.
      - John's income of £300/week (HGV) and £20/week (sea cadets) are stated in report but not numerically corroborated in the retrieved cashflow planning file passages.
    Multiple internal inconsistencies are confirmed by the evidence pack. First and most materially, Kim's State Pension age is stated as 66 in the narrative and confirmed as 66 in the intelliflo planning file [P7], but the cashflow assumptions on page 14 of the report use age 67. This is a direct contradiction within the report that is also contradicted by the supporting planning file — it is not explained by any guard and affects the cashflow output. Second, the mid-growth rate used in the modelling is 2.95% (confirmed by [P1] and the pension switch report), but the suitability report narrative on page 11 states 2.94%. This is a minor data quality issue (1bp) but is a genuine internal inconsistency. Third, the combined pension value is cited as approximately £110,000 on page 3 and approximately £106,000 on page 4, while the intelliflo baseline file [P14] shows a DC pension total of £116,997 and the derived sum is £116,998.47 — neither narrative approximation is close to the evidenced figure of ~£117,000. The £110,185 figure in the recommended solution table represents only the three transferring plans, not all five, which is a different concept but the report does not make this distinction clear in its narrative approximations. The People's Pension omission from cashflow inputs may be intentional (ongoing workplace scheme) per the ambiguity noted, but no explicit statement in the report confirms this; however this is categorised as ambiguous rather than a clear error. The Kim state pension age discrepancy and the pension total discrepancies are material findings that affect the cashflow output and the description of the client's financial position respectively. The mid-growth rate discrepancy is minor. The guards regarding illustration growth rates and older model runs do not apply here as these are report-to-report and report-to-planning-file contradictions on client facts, not provider-prescribed rates.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7, G2.8). The report states objective 1 (lifestyle/expenditure) as the highest priority, but the fact find records objectives in an order that leads with consolidation/organisation ('just needs advice helping him organise his pensions') before mentioning the retirement income target. The explicit priority ranking 1-2-3 in the report is not replicated or confirmed in the fact find. The report states the client wants to 'retire in 2 years' (objective 2), but the cashflow tool (F) shows John's retirement event at age 71 in 2027 — which is approximately 2 years from the advice date — while Kim retires at age 66 in 2026 (approximately 1 year). The report does not distinguish between the two clients' retirement timelines in the objectives section. The fact find records monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00, while the report rounds this to 'approximately £1,700 net per month' for both the lifestyle objective and the retirement income target. This is a minor rounding difference but the same £1,700 figure is used for two distinct purposes (current expenditure maintenance and retirement income target) without distinguishing them. 6 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states three client-stated objectives in priority order: (1) continue to afford lifestyle through lifetime — maintain household expenditure of approximately £1,700 net per month; (2) retire in 2 years with a household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) consolidate pension arrangements into a more streamlined structure appropriately configured for retirement. All three are marked as client-stated.
    File:     The fact find (B) records under Retirement Planning: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The fact find also records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00 and a retirement planning income target of £1,700.00 per month. The file note (C) records the client is not reliant on pension funds and wishes them to have best growth potential over a 10-year period. The cashflow tool (F) records a retirement event for John at age 71 in 2027 and Kim at age 66 in 2026. The client declaration in the fact find confirms objectives are those identified and prioritised in the document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states objective 1 (lifestyle/expenditure) as the highest priority, but the fact find records objectives in an order that leads with consolidation/organisation ('just needs advice helping him organise his pensions') before mentioning the retirement income target. The explicit priority ranking 1-2-3 in the report is not replicated or confirmed in the fact find.
      - The report states the client wants to 'retire in 2 years' (objective 2), but the cashflow tool (F) shows John's retirement event at age 71 in 2027 — which is approximately 2 years from the advice date — while Kim retires at age 66 in 2026 (approximately 1 year). The report does not distinguish between the two clients' retirement timelines in the objectives section.
      - The fact find records monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00, while the report rounds this to 'approximately £1,700 net per month' for both the lifestyle objective and the retirement income target. This is a minor rounding difference but the same £1,700 figure is used for two distinct purposes (current expenditure maintenance and retirement income target) without distinguishing them.
      - The file note (C) records the client confirmed he is 'not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period' — a 10-year investment horizon — which does not appear as a stated objective or planning assumption in the report's objectives section, and sits in some tension with a 2-year retirement horizon.
    The three objectives stated in the report are substantively traceable to the fact find evidence. The fact find records the consolidation goal, the 2-year retirement timeline, and the £1,700 net per month income target explicitly. The client declaration confirms the objectives were identified and prioritised in the fact find document. The paraphrase guard applies: the report's wording ('continue to afford their lifestyle through their lifetime') is a reasonable restatement of the expenditure maintenance need evidenced by the £1,718 monthly expenditure figure in the fact find. The priority ranking 1-2-3 assigned in the report is not explicitly ordered in the fact find, but the goals themselves are not competing in a material way that would require a different priority — this is a low-concern gap. The 10-year investment horizon mentioned in the file note (C) is not reflected in the report's objectives section; this creates a mild tension with the 2-year retirement horizon but the file note context suggests this refers to the pension fund growth aspiration rather than a separate retirement objective. The cashflow tool confirms retirement events broadly consistent with 'within 2 years.' The rounding of £1,718 to £1,700 is immaterial. The main residual concern is that the explicit priority order (1-2-3) in the report lacks corroboration in the fact find — the fact find does not rank the objectives — though the goals themselves are evidenced. This is a low-severity gap rather than a material concern. No vulnerability indicators in the evidence bear on this objectives/goals check in a way that affects the assessment.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   John's intended retirement age is 66 (per /parties/clients[]/employments[]/intendedRetirementAge). One objective has a term of 2 years (/objectives[]/termYears). Time horizons are classified as LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm (/objectives[]/timeHorizon). Life expectancy basis: ONS life expectancy for a 69-year-old male is age 86; for a 65-year-old female is age 88. No targetDate or targetAge fields are present in the extracted model for any objective. Kim's intended retirement age is stated in the narrative as 66 (page 3). The cashflow on page 14 assumes Kim receives state pension from age 67.
    File:     [P10] (intelliflo proposed scenario) records John (07/06/1956, age 69) with STATE PENSION AGE 66 and Kim (07/05/1960, age 65) with STATE PENSION AGE 66, with Kim's 'Retire' event at age 66 in 2026 and John's 'Retire' event at age 71 in 2027. [P16] (intelliflo baseline scenario) records the same retirement events: Kim retires age 66 in 2026, John retires age 71 in 2027. [P11] (risk profile report) records an investment period of 2 years and investment of £106,000. [P1] (pension switch report) uses a retirement age of 75 for the Aviva plan illustration. The extraction ambiguity note records an internal inconsistency: page 3 states Kim retires at 66; page 14 cashflow states Kim receives state pension from age 67. John's state pension age is recorded as 66 in [P10]. The cashflow planning tool shows John retiring at age 71 in 2027, not at the age 66 stated as his intended retirement age in the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states John's intended retirement age as 66, but the intelliflo planning tool (both baseline [P16] and proposed [P10]) records John's 'Retire' event at age 71 in 2027, not age 66. This is a 5-year difference, exceeding the 2-year tolerance.
      - The suitability report's page 3 narrative states Kim intends to retire at age 66, which is consistent with the intelliflo tool, but page 14 of the same report states Kim receives state pension income from age 67 — an internal inconsistency of 1 year within the report itself.
      - The pension switch report [P1] uses retirement age 75 for its projections, which differs materially from the client's stated retirement intention of age 66 (John) or 66 (Kim).
      - One objective is assigned a 2-year term, which is classified as ShortTerm and is corroborated by the risk profile report [P11]. However, no targetDate or targetAge is linked to this 2-year objective, making it unclear which goal it serves or why 2 years is the relevant horizon when John is already 69 and the stated retirement age is 66 (which is already past for a 69-year-old).
      - John is currently aged 69 yet his intended retirement age is stated as 66 — this implies he has already passed his stated retirement age, making the retirement target date effectively in the past rather than a future planning horizon. No explanation or updated target is provided.
      - The life expectancy basis references a 69-year-old male and a 65-year-old female, which is consistent with John and Kim's current ages, but the planning horizon to age 86/88 used in the cashflow stress tests is not explicitly reconciled with any of the three time horizon labels (LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm) assigned to the objectives.
    Several material discrepancies arise from this comparison. First, and most significant: John's stated intended retirement age of 66 is inconsistent with the intelliflo planning tool in both the baseline [P16] and proposed [P10] scenarios, which record his 'Retire' event at age 71 in 2027. At John's current age of 69, a retirement age of 66 is already in the past — the report does not acknowledge this or explain any updated target. The 5-year gap between the report's stated retirement age (66) and the planning tool's modelled retirement age (71) far exceeds the 2-year tolerance. This is a genuine mismatch, not explained by the guard distinguishing plan contractual defaults from client intentions (the planning tool reflects adviser-input client intentions, not a product default). The guard about the switch report using its own retirement age (75) does explain why the switch report projections use age 75 — that is a product illustration default, not a client target — and that difference is therefore not a finding in its own right. Second, there is an internal inconsistency within the report itself regarding Kim's state pension age: page 3 states she retires at 66 while page 14 cashflow inputs use age 67 for her state pension receipt. This has been flagged as an ambiguity in the extraction metadata. The intelliflo tool records Kim's state pension age as 66 [P10], supporting the page 3 narrative and making the page 14 figure of 67 the anomalous entry. Third, the 2-year investment term and ShortTerm horizon classification are internally consistent with the risk profile report [P11], but with John already aged 69 and his stated retirement age of 66 having passed, the basis for this 2-year horizon is unexplained. Fourth, no targetDate or targetAge is captured for any objective, so it is impossible to verify that each objective is being planned to the correct date. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics have been identified in the evidence that materially affect this specific check — the absence of a vulnerability assessment (/vulnerability/C1/assessment is noted as NotPresentInDocument) is a matter for CHK-010. Overall, the primary concern is that the report's stated retirement age for John (66) conflicts with the planning tool's modelled retirement age (71) by 5 years, and John at age 69 has already passed 66. This is a material inconsistency that undermines the planning horizon justification and is a Potential Concern at High severity.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Five foreseeable life changes are asserted: (1) John expects to retire within the next two years (expected date 2027-10-14), ceasing HGV driver and Sea Cadets income. (2) Kim intends to continue working until age 66, then retire fully, ceasing employment income. (3) Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230/week when she reaches State Pension age — the canonical model notes this is modelled as age 67 in the cashflow on page 14, while the narrative on page 3 implies age 66 as Kim's retirement/State Pension age. (4) From 6 April 2027, unused DC pensions and death benefits will be included in the taxable estate for IHT. (5) People's Pension contributions (John £1,193.33 p.a., employer £897.13 p.a.) will cease at John's retirement — inferred from the report's statement that contributions will continue until retirement.
    File:     Fact Find (B): John's objectives recorded as organising pensions, likely retiring in the next 2 years, needing £1,700 net household income per month; no explicit income changes expected noted in the fact find income changes section (answer: No). Kim's income listed as Basic Income £1,200/month from CDH Recruitment and State Pension £230/month (though this entry appears in her record). Cashflow planning tool (F — proposed and baseline scenarios): Kim's 'Retire' event is modelled at age 66 in 2026; John's 'Retire' event at age 71 in 2027. Kim's State Pension age is shown as 66 in the planning tool heads-of-household section. The cashflow assumptions in the suitability report (page 14, passage P10) state 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67'. No documentary evidence in categories B, C or F explicitly records Kim's State Pension age as 67 — the planning tool records it as 66. The IHT legislative change (6 April 2027) is a general legislative change, not a client-specific fact-find item. People's Pension contribution cessation at retirement is supported by the report's own statement about the workplace scheme.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report's canonical model states Kim's State Pension age is modelled as 67 in the cashflow (page 14), but the planning tool (P8, P1) records Kim's State Pension age as 66, and the suitability report narrative on page 3 (P6) states Kim will retire at 66 with State Pension at State Pension age — creating an internal inconsistency between page 3 and page 14 of the report, and a discrepancy between the page 14 cashflow assumption (age 67) and the planning tool (age 66).
      - The planning tool (P8) records Kim's 'Retire' event at age 66 in 2026, consistent with the page 3 narrative; John's 'Retire' event is modelled at age 71 in 2027 in the proposed scenario — the report states John expects to retire within the next two years (from the advice date of September 2025, implying approximately 2027), so John's retirement at age 71 in 2027 is broadly consistent, but the planning tool proposed scenario records retirement at 71 rather than approximately 69-70 as the 'within 2 years' narrative implies given John is currently 69.
      - The fact find income changes section records 'No' expected changes to income for the clients, which is superficially inconsistent with the stated foreseeable retirement and income cessation events, though this may reflect the fact find question relating to near-term income changes rather than longer-term planning events.
      - The IHT legislative change (6 April 2027) is a general legislative change, not a client-disclosed foreseeable personal life change — the guard notes these are not client-specific foreseeable changes.
    The most material discrepancy is the inconsistency around Kim's State Pension age. The suitability report on page 3 (P6) states Kim will receive her full State Pension when she reaches State Pension age, alongside a retirement at age 66. The cashflow assumptions on page 14 (P10) state Kim receives state pension from age 67. The planning tool files (P1, P8) record Kim's State Pension age as 66 in both baseline and proposed scenarios. This is an internal inconsistency within the suitability report (page 3 implies 66, page 14 states 67) that is also contradicted by the planning tool evidence (which uses 66). The canonical model acknowledges this ambiguity but the report's own cashflow assumption of age 67 is unsupported by — and contradicted by — the planning tool. This matters because Kim's State Pension income timing affects the cashflow projections underpinning the retirement income objective. The guard regarding legislative changes applies to the IHT point: the April 2027 pension IHT change is a universal legislative development, not a client-specific disclosed foreseeable change, so its inclusion in the report as a foreseeable change is noted but does not constitute a client-evidence gap. John's retirement within two years is supported by the fact find (P9) and the planning tool (P8) models his retirement at age 71 in 2027, which is consistent with 'within two years' from the September 2025 advice date. Kim's retirement at age 66 is supported by the planning tool and the report narrative. The income changes 'No' answer in the fact find is a minor inconsistency but is explained by the fact find question likely capturing short-term changes rather than planned retirement events already captured elsewhere in the objectives and planning tool. The People's Pension contribution cessation is an inferred change supported by the report's own statement and is internally consistent. The State Pension age discrepancy (66 in planning tool and page 3 vs 67 in page 14 cashflow assumptions) is a data inconsistency that is not explained by any guard and represents a material concern because it affects the cashflow projections supporting the recommendation.   [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states three objectives in priority order (ranks 1, 2, 3): (1) ensuring continued affordability of lifestyle through lifetime, (2) retiring in 2 years with a household income of £1,700 net per month, (3) clearer understanding and streamlined management of pension arrangements. The recommended new pension consolidates three plans into an Aviva platform pension invested in balanced risk-5 funds. The critical yield analysis uses age 75 as the retirement/maturity age. Kim's retirement is stated as age 66 (2 years from advice). No target dates or target amounts are extracted from the model for the objectives. Kim's state pension age is stated as 66 on page 3 but the cashflow assumptions use age 67 for Kim's state pension receipt.
    File:     The fact find (P21, category B) confirms client financial planning objectives were identified and prioritised, with a signed declaration. The cashflow modelling (P11, category F) records Kim's state pension age as 66 and her retirement event at age 66 in 2026, and John's retirement at age 71 in 2027. The baseline cashflow scenario (P15, F) shows the household income objective is modelled with pension drawdown. The pension switch report (P20, P22, P24, category G) uses age 75 as the maturity/projection age throughout its critical yield and switch analysis. The suitability report itself (P8, I) uses age 75 as the retirement age for all critical yield projections. The file notes (P10, C) confirm the client's main priority is to consolidate, get a better handle on pensions, and be ready for retirement. The cashflow model (P11) records Kim's state pension age as 66 with a retirement event in 2026, but the suitability report cashflow assumptions (P8, I) state Kim receives state pension from age 67. No target amounts for the £1,700/month income objective are evidenced in the non-report documents beyond the cashflow modelling.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states Kim retires at age 66 (page 3) but the cashflow assumptions within the same report state Kim receives state pension from age 67 (page 14) — these are internally inconsistent and the cashflow model in P11 shows Kim's state pension age as 66, contradicting the age 67 used in the report's own cashflow assumptions.
      - The critical yield analysis uses age 75 as the maturity age (P8), which is 9 years beyond Kim's stated retirement age of 66 and significantly beyond John's retirement at age 71 — the investment term used to justify the strategy (to age 75) does not match the stated retirement goal horizon (2 years/age 66 for Kim).
      - No target dates or target amounts are present in the extracted canonical model for any of the three objectives, making it impossible to verify whether the recommended strategy can deliver the stated retirement income objective of £1,700 net per month by the stated date.
      - The priority ranking (1, 2, 3) is present in the report but no evidence of client-authored prioritisation beyond the generic fact find declaration (P21) is in the file — the objectives as worded in the report are not independently evidenced in a client-completed document.
    Two structural concerns arise. First, the investment term used to justify the strategy and the risk profile is to age 75 (as evidenced in the critical yield appendix at P8 and the pension switch report at P20/P22/P24), but Kim's stated retirement objective is in 2 years at age 66. This is a direct tension: the strategy is calibrated to a 6-year growth horizon (to age 75) while the income need crystallises in approximately 2 years. The report does not explain how drawdown will be managed in the intervening period or why the age-75 horizon is appropriate given the near-term income requirement. This is not a locked-capital scenario (the Aviva platform pension allows drawdown), but the growth assumptions and critical yield assessment are framed around a horizon that substantially outlasts the stated goal horizon, which is the structural conflict the guard directs assessment towards. Second, the Kim state pension age discrepancy (66 per page 3 and P11, 67 per the report's cashflow assumptions at P8) means the income modelling may be built on an incorrect assumption, affecting whether the £1,700/month objective is actually achievable on the stated timeline. The file notes at P10 corroborate the consolidation objective in client-authored terms ('The main priority for the client is to consolidate, get a better handle on his pensions and be ready for retirement'), providing some independent evidencing of objective 3. The income objective of £1,700/month is modelled in the cashflow tool (P11/P15) but the state pension age inconsistency undermines confidence in those projections. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics have been identified in the retrieved evidence that bear materially on the objectives or time-horizon alignment assessment here. The age-75 maturity horizon versus the 2-year retirement goal is a Potential Concern at high severity because the investment term justifying the risk profile (and critical yield) contradicts the stated goal horizon in a meaningful way, even though drawdown access is available.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report identifies three objectives: OBJ1 (unstated label but linked only to the sixth recommendation), OBJ2 and OBJ3 (linked to most recommendations). The recommended solution is a single Aviva Pension Portfolio (NP1) consolidating three pension transfers totalling £110,185, with no regular contributions. The planning horizon used for projections is age 75 (C1's retirement age). The cashflow modelling runs to age 100 and shows no projected shortfall. The retirement income target is £1,700 per month net in retirement. Kim (C2) is stated to retire at 66 on page 3 but the cashflow assumptions use age 67 for her state pension. The report states the state pension income will cover bills in retirement, allowing the pension to keep growing for large ad hoc spends. The recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc and CT Universal MAP Balanced C, each 50%) are described as aligned with retirement objectives. Flexi-access drawdown is identified as the preferred access mechanism at retirement. The client's age is 69 (C1, DOB 7 June 1956) at the time of advice, giving approximately 6 years to the stated retirement/projection age of 75.
    File:     The fact find (B) records a monthly household expenditure of £1,718 and total net monthly income of £1,430, producing a monthly deficit of -£288. No foreseeable expenditure changes are recorded. The intelliflo planning proposed scenario (F) records employment income of £16,640 (C1) and £13,200 (C2), total regular income of £41,813 combined, and regular expenditure of £24,001 in the first year of plan. The cashflow baseline (F) models liquid assets running to age 100 with no red bars and uses a dual age axis (C1/C2). The policy research document (G) records 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement'. The suitability report (I) states on page 14 that C2 receives state pension from age 67, but page 3 states Kim retires at 66. The Zurich policy illustration (E) uses a retirement date of 7 June 2031 (age 75), consistent with NP1 projections. The existing plan documents (E) confirm the existing arrangements are accessible from age 55/57 and support drawdown. The Key Features document (H) confirms the Aviva Pension Portfolio supports flexi-access drawdown and access from normal minimum pension age.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states Kim (C2) intends to retire at 66 (page 3) but the cashflow assumptions in the same report use state pension income from age 67 (page 14) — an internal inconsistency in the report itself that affects the income timeline underpinning the suitability assessment.
      - The objective labels (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) are used throughout the recommendations linkage but the substantive content of each named objective is not independently evidenced in any category B, C or F document — only the income target of £1,700 net per month appears in the policy research (G) document, which is a practitioner research note rather than a client-authored needs statement.
      - The cashflow modelling (F) excludes the People's Pension (EA1, £6,601.55) from the asset projection inputs (only Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439 and Standard Life £3,002 are listed), meaning the modelled asset base may understate total pension wealth, potentially affecting the sufficiency assessment underpinning the recommendation.
      - The fact find (B) records a current monthly deficit of -£288 (income £1,430, expenditure £1,718) for C1. The suitability report does not address how ongoing living costs are met during the accumulation phase given this deficit, nor does it address this as a foreseeable financial pressure bearing on the recommendation.
      - The six recommendations each link to OBJ2 and/or OBJ3 (and one to OBJ1) but no recommendation narrative specifically names what OBJ1, OBJ2 and OBJ3 represent in client-specific terms beyond the generic retirement objective framing in the report.
    The report's primary stated goal is retirement income of £1,700 net per month, supported by the policy research document [P1] and the cashflow modelling [P10]. The recommendation of a single consolidated pension with FAD access is consistent with the stated goal of flexible retirement income and the client's age of 69 with a 6-year horizon to age 75. The Aviva Pension Portfolio supports FAD and the access mechanism is appropriate for a client already past the minimum pension age — the guard on access mechanism rather than wrapper name applies here and no access restriction concern arises.
    
    However, three concerns remain after the guards. First, the Kim (C2) state pension age inconsistency (66 vs 67) is an internal inconsistency flagged in the canonical model's own ambiguities and confirmed by the passages [P10]. This affects the income projection in the cashflow model and therefore the income sufficiency conclusion that underpins the recommendation. The state pension income is described as covering bills in retirement [P10], so whether it arrives at 66 or 67 materially affects the one-year gap analysis. This is a Data inconsistency that weakens the cashflow sufficiency finding.
    
    Second, the objectives OBJ1, OBJ2 and OBJ3 are linked to recommendations throughout the report but their substantive content is only partially evidenced. The £1,700 income target appears in the policy research note [P1], which is a practitioner document rather than a client-authored or client-confirmed needs statement (category B or C). No category B or C passage sets out the objectives, priorities or time horizon in the client's own words or as confirmed by the client. The linkage of all pension-switch recommendations to OBJ2 and OBJ3 without specifying what distinguishes those objectives from each other in this context is consistent with boilerplate objective linkage rather than client-specific justification. This meets the 'Weak justification' pattern.
    
    Third, the People's Pension (EA1) exclusion from cashflow modelling inputs [P10] means the cashflow sufficiency conclusion rests on an incomplete asset base. Whether intentional or not, the report does not explain the omission, and the ambiguity noted in the canonical model is unresolved. Given that the cashflow conclusion ('no shortfall projected') is used to support the recommendation's suitability, this is a Missing component in the evidence base.
    
    The current monthly deficit of -£288 recorded in the fact find [P24] is not addressed in the suitability report as a foreseeable financial pressure. While the report covers the investment term to age 75, it does not address how C1 manages current outgoings during the accumulation phase — a gap in the foreseeable changes analysis.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence (the canonical model notes /vulnerability/C1/assessment is absent from the document). No vulnerability-related adjustment is therefore required here, though the absence of a recorded vulnerability assessment is noted.
    
    Overall: the recommendation's broad direction (pension consolidation for retirement income via FAD) is consistent with the stated goal, but the objective linkage lacks client-authored evidential support, the cashflow model contains an internal inconsistency on Kim's state pension age and excludes a retained pension, and the current income deficit is unaddressed. These are material enough to constitute a Potential Concern.

  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts three client-stated objectives: (1) continue to afford lifestyle through lifetime — maintain household expenditure of approximately £1,700 net per month; (2) retire in 2 years with a household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) consolidate pension arrangements into a more streamlined structure appropriately configured for retirement. Cashflow assumptions include: general expenditure inflation 2%, savings inflation 2%, state pension inflation 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions growth 5%, 0% surplus saved, cashflow run to age 100, John income £300/week HGV + £20/week Sea Cadets, Kim income £1,100/month, John state pension £11,973 p.a., Kim state pension £11,973 p.a. from age 67. The report also states Kim intends to retire fully at 66 (page 3), but the cashflow assumption places Kim's state pension commencement at age 67 — an internal inconsistency flagged in the canonical model. An emergency fund of £6,000 cash savings is noted in the fact find.
    File:     The fact find (B) records the retirement planning objective: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' Regular income target of £1,700.00 per month is recorded. Calculated total monthly household expenditure is £1,718.00, with no expected changes in expenditure in the foreseeable future. The fact find records a cash savings account of £6,000. Kim's retirement age is stated as 66 in the report's current situation section but age 67 in the cashflow assumptions — this inconsistency is present across the report itself and is flagged in the canonical model ambiguities. No independent evidence from category B or C directly records Kim's state pension age as either 66 or 67.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states Kim will retire and reach State Pension age at 66 (page 3) but the cashflow assumptions use age 67 for Kim's state pension commencement (page 14) — these are inconsistent within the report and no supporting document resolves which is correct.
      - The policy research document (G) leaves the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field blank for every arrangement (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Standard Life), meaning no recommendation is explicitly linked to a named objective in the supporting file.
      - The report states monthly household expenditure target of approximately £1,700 net per month; the fact find records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00 — a minor difference of £18, with the report rounding down.
      - The fact find records total net monthly income of £1,430 against total monthly expenditure of £1,718, producing a monthly shortfall of -£288 at the time of advice; the report presents the £1,700/month target as achievable in retirement without explicitly addressing this current shortfall in the objectives section.
    The three core objectives are supported by evidence in the file. Objective 1 (maintain £1,700/month lifestyle) is corroborated by the fact find's recorded regular income target of £1,700 and total monthly household expenditure of £1,718. Objective 2 (retire in 2 years with £1,700/month household income) is directly corroborated by the fact find's goals section. Objective 3 (consolidate pensions) is corroborated by the fact find's goals description and the file notes discussion of alternative options and consolidation rationale. The income assumptions used in the cashflow (John £300/week HGV, £20/week Sea Cadets, Kim £1,100/month) are corroborated by the file notes capacity for loss section. The £6,000 cash savings/emergency fund is recorded in the fact find. So far, no issue. However, two concerns remain. First, the report contains an internal inconsistency on Kim's retirement/state pension age (66 on page 3, 67 in cashflow on page 14). This is not merely a rounding or summarisation difference — age 66 versus age 67 for state pension commencement is a material planning assumption that affects the cashflow model and the income gap analysis. No document in the file resolves this; it is a data inconsistency within the report that no external evidence corroborates or corrects. The guard about inferred objectives confirmed at meetings does not apply here as this is an internal figure conflict. Second, the policy research document (G) leaves blank the field 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' for every single arrangement. While the report's narrative does link consolidation and cost as reasons, the working document connecting each recommendation to a specific objective is unpopulated, which means the recommendations are linked to objectives only by narrative boilerplate rather than by documented, specific linkage at the recommendation level. The £18 rounding difference between the report's '£1,700' and the fact find's £1,718 is adequately explained by rounding and does not constitute a finding. The current monthly shortfall (-£288) reflects the pre-retirement position and is not inconsistent with a post-retirement income target — it may reflect that income will change at retirement — so this is not a standalone concern on this requirement. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that would materially alter the assessment of objectives alignment. The Kim state pension age inconsistency is the more material finding, as it affects the cashflow planning horizon and income sufficiency analysis.
