  [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the client wants to retire in 2 years with a household income of £1,700 net per month. The cashflow modelling uses annual retirement expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month as the target. Cashflow assumptions include: John earns £300/week as HGV driver, £20/week from sea cadets, Kim earns £1,100/month; John receives state pension of £11,973 p.a.; Kim receives state pension of £11,973 p.a. from age 67; expenditure inflation 2%; cashflow runs to age 100; surplus income 0% saved. The report also states Kim plans to retire at age 66 (page 3) but the cashflow assumptions use age 67 for Kim's state pension commencement (page 14). Retirement age used for pension projections is 75.
    File:     The fact find (P2) records: 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The policy research document (P1) records: 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement.' The fact find (P4) shows calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00, current net monthly income of £1,430.00, and a monthly disposable income of -£288.00. The fact find (P3) records Kim's income as £1,200/month (basic) and £230/month (state pension), totalling £1,430. The intelliflo cashflow baseline (P22, P20) runs to age 100 and shows liquid assets and pension assets over the plan. The pension switch report (P6, P7, P13) uses retirement age 75 for projections with mid growth rate 2.95%. Kim's state pension age is stated as 66 on page 3 of the report but the cashflow uses age 67.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Kim's income as £1,100 per month in cashflow assumptions (P24), but the fact find (P3) shows Kim's basic income as £1,200 per month.
      - Report states Kim plans to retire at age 66 (page 3), but cashflow assumptions state Kim's state pension commences from age 67 (P24) — internal inconsistency also reflected in the extraction ambiguities.
      - The report's cashflow uses annual retirement expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month as the target; the fact find records current monthly household expenditure of £1,718 (P4), which is broadly consistent but the current income is only £1,430/month, meaning there is already a pre-retirement shortfall. The report does not explicitly address how the £1,700 target will be met from pension income alone post-retirement.
      - The report states John's Sea Cadets income as £20 per week in the cashflow assumptions; this figure is not matched in the retrieved passages (arithmetic check flags it as unmatched).
    The core retirement income target of £1,700 net per month is well-evidenced: both the fact find [P2] and the policy research document [P1] record this figure, matching the report's assertion exactly. This is corroborated external evidence, not merely internal repetition.
    
    However, two material discrepancies require attention:
    
    1. Kim's employment income in the cashflow: The report states £1,100 per month [P24] but the fact find records £1,200 per month basic income [P3]. This is a data inconsistency between the report and its source document. The difference of £100/month is not trivial in a cashflow running to age 100 and affects whether the model accurately represents the household's position.
    
    2. Kim's state pension age: The report states on page 3 that Kim plans to retire at 66 and will reach state pension age at 66, but the cashflow assumptions use age 67 for Kim's state pension commencement [P24]. This internal inconsistency (also flagged in the extraction) means the cashflow modelling may understate income in the year between age 66 and 67, or alternatively the page 3 narrative misrepresents when state pension begins. Either way, the figure used in the model does not match the narrative statement of when Kim reaches state pension age, and the report does not resolve this tension.
    
    3. The £20/week sea cadets income is used in the cashflow but is not corroborated by any category B or C document in the retrieved passages. The arithmetic check also fails to match it. This is an income assumption in the model without evidential support in the file.
    
    4. The fact find shows current household expenditure of £1,718/month against current income of £1,430/month — a shortfall of £288/month before retirement. The report states the £1,700 target will be met and that the cashflow shows no shortfall, but does not explain how post-retirement income will exceed pre-retirement income given that both state pensions together (£11,973 x2 = £23,946 p.a. = ~£1,995/month combined gross) would need to cover the target net. While the cashflow tool may have modelled this correctly, the report itself does not make the linkage clear.
    
    The Kim income discrepancy (£1,100 vs £1,200) and the Kim state pension age discrepancy (66 vs 67) are the most material findings. Neither is explained by the guards (these are not today's-money vs inflated figures, nor DB/DC base differences). The sea cadets income lack of evidence is a secondary concern. These collectively constitute a Potential Concern of moderate severity: the retirement income target itself is well evidenced, but the model inputs used to assess whether it is achievable contain inconsistencies with the source documents.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   CFL rated 'Medium' for the client (John). The basis given is: (1) John receives full State Pension ~£230/week, earns ~£300/week net as HGV driver, and ~£20/week from Sea Cadets; (2) Kim contributes £1,100/month from employment and will also receive full State Pension ~£230/week at SPA; (3) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of ~£1,700/month; (4) savings of ~£6,000; (5) no outstanding liabilities; (6) pension funds totalling approximately £106,000 form the basis of retirement provision; (7) John confirmed any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living. The report states CFL is separately assessed from ATR.
    File:     The risk profile document [P20] records a capacity for loss section but contains 'No additional notes were recorded' — no financial reasoning, income, expenditure or savings figures appear in that section. The cashflow/financial planning documents [P3][P4][P6] show total pension assets of £116,997 (baseline) and liquid assets of £6,000, with consistent in-year surpluses throughout the plan. No independent source document (fact-find, client questionnaire, income verification) in the evidence pack corroborates the income figures or the household expenditure figure of ~£1,700/month independently of the suitability report. The pension fund total stated as ~£106,000 in the CFL section is internally inconsistent: page 3 of the report states ~£110,000, the sum of five individual plan values is £116,998.47 (per derived figures), the cashflow baseline shows £116,997 in pensions [P3], and the recommended solution table shows £110,185 [P4].     Discrepancies:
      - The report's CFL section states pension funds total 'approximately £106,000'; the derived sum of five individual plan values is £116,998.47; the cashflow baseline shows £116,997 in pensions; page 3 of the report states 'approximately £110,000'; the recommended solution table shows £110,185 — none of these four figures agree.
      - The risk profile tool records 'No additional notes were recorded' in the capacity for loss section [P20], meaning no independent CFL reasoning, income data or expenditure data is documented on file outside the suitability report itself.

    The CFL rating of 'Medium' rests on a financial narrative in the suitability report that is not independently corroborated in the evidence pack. The risk profile document, which is the primary external assessment tool, records no notes at all in its capacity for loss section [P20]. The £106,000 figure does match the investment amount entered into the risk profile tool [P17], suggesting the report may have copied the risk tool input figure rather than the actual fund value into the CFL narrative, but this is unexplained and constitutes a data inconsistency material to the CFL basis.
    
    The guard against treating CFL as merely a restatement of ATR is relevant: the report does state CFL is separately assessed (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr: true) and provides financial reasoning beyond simply echoing the ATR. However, because the underlying financial data is uncorroborated and the risk profile CFL section is blank, the separate assessment is not evidenced on file.

    No vulnerability indicators are identified in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report addresses tolerance for loss only via a general risk warning: 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value.' No specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary amount is stated. The report notes the questionnaire gave a rating of 4 but the agreed rating was raised to 5 (Balanced), with the client confirming awareness that this is higher than the questionnaire result and that he is not reliant on his pension funds. The report models a stress test showing an immediate market crash of -30% combined with high inflation (6%) results in no shortfall. The path /objectives[]/needLevelRisk is absent from the canonical model, indicating the report does not state a need-level risk figure.
    File:     The Risk Profile Report [P19] records a natural ATR questionnaire result of 4 (Cautious Balanced) completed on 18/09/2025. [P14] records the agreed risk level as 5 (Balanced) following an interactive discussion considering natural ATR, capacity for loss and required return, with no additional notes recorded for either the investment target discussion or the agreed risk level section. The SIPP Research Report [P1][P5] confirms the agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced). [P11] records the Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses for John Sullivan on 18/09/2025 but the passage is truncated and no specific loss tolerance percentage or amount is visible in the retrieved text. The stress test in [P23] (suitability report, category I) models a -30% immediate market crash with no resulting shortfall. No passage in category D, B, G or H quantifies a maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount, nor is a need-level risk figure evidenced anywhere in the retrieved passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report raises the agreed risk rating from the questionnaire result of 4 to 5. The Risk Profile Report [P19][P14] confirms this occurred and records an agreed level of 5, but the 'Agreed risk level' section in [P4] states 'No additional notes were recorded' — no written justification for the upward override is documented in the risk profile tool beyond the client's stated preference and non-reliance on pension funds as recorded in the suitability report itself.
      - Tolerance for loss is addressed only via a general qualitative warning in the report; no maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary amount is stated anywhere in the report or in the supporting evidence.
      - The path /objectives[]/needLevelRisk is absent from the canonical model; no need-level risk figure appears in any retrieved passage, so the report's implicit claim that the agreed risk level is appropriate to the client's needs cannot be corroborated by external evidence.
      - The stress test models a -30% loss scenario but this appears only in the suitability report (category I) and is not corroborated by an independent document; the cashflow modelling output [P10] does not explicitly label a -30% crash scenario.
    The core issue here is two-fold. First, the upward override from ATR score 4 to agreed rating 5 is acknowledged by both the report and the supporting risk profile documentation, but the justification recorded in the risk profile tool itself is absent — [P4] explicitly states 'No additional notes were recorded' for both the investment target and agreed risk level sections. The suitability report (an [I] source) provides the rationale (client not reliant on pension funds, wishes for best growth potential over 10 years), but the report cannot corroborate itself. The external file therefore does not evidence the justification for the upward override beyond the bare fact that an agreed level of 5 was reached. This is a control gap: the override is recorded but not independently justified in the case file documentation. Second, tolerance for loss is never quantified. The report relies on a general risk warning and a qualitative confirmation that underperformance would not affect daily living. The stress test (-30% crash) appears only in the suitability report itself. No category D, B, G or H document quantifies a maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount. The guard acknowledges that many firms do not quantify tolerance for loss separately and that absence is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction — but the severity guidance flags that tolerance for loss not quantified where a large-loss scenario is modelled is a Low concern, and here a -30% scenario is modelled (in the report). The need-level risk path is absent, meaning the report does not assert a need-level risk figure, so no comparison on that point is possible; this is report silence rather than extraction failure per the extraction metadata. The vulnerability overlay: the extraction notes /vulnerability/C1/assessment as NotPresentInDocument, so no vulnerability factors are on file that would materially affect this assessment. Taken together: the override carries no independent written justification in the file, and tolerance for loss is unquantified despite a large-loss stress test being modelled. These are Potential Concerns.

This showed that if your plan sustained an immediate loss of -30% and the increased inflation continued, there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used."

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model records an investment term of 10 years for the pension recommendation. The objectives array records three time horizons: LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm — suggesting the pension objective is LongTerm and two others (likely relating to shorter-term goals) are ShortTerm.
    File:     The risk profile document [P3] shows the investment period used in the risk-level projections as 2 years, with an investment amount of £106,000. The SIPP research report [P9] table of contents references an agreed risk level of 5. The cashflow modelling in [P5] shows retirement events for Kim at age 66 in 2026 (i.e. approximately 1 year from the report date of September 2025) and for John at age 71 in 2027. The pension switch report [P12] uses a retirement age of 75 for Standard Life with a time to retirement of 5 years and 6 months. No document in the file explicitly states a 10-year investment term for the pension recommendation or links a 10-year horizon to the risk-5 profile.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts an investment term of 10 years, but the risk profile document [P3] used an investment period of 2 years in the projections that determined the agreed risk level of 5.
      - The risk profile projection used £106,000 over 2 years, not a 10-year term, yet this was the basis for agreeing a risk level of 5 Balanced — the time horizon underpinning the risk agreement does not match the 10-year term stated in the report.
      - The cashflow modelling [P5] shows Kim retiring at age 66 in 2026 (approximately 1 year away) and John at 71 in 2027, both materially shorter than 10 years.
      - The pension switch report [P12] uses a retirement age of 75 for the Standard Life plan with a time to retirement of 5 years 6 months, not 10 years.
      - No document in the pack explicitly evidences or corroborates a 10-year investment term as the basis for the risk profile or strategy.
    The report asserts a 10-year investment term which is recorded in the canonical model. The figure '10' is corroborated numerically in [P9] (the table of contents references the agreed risk level context), but the corroboration of the number 10 in [P9] appears to be a page number reference in the table of contents ('Combined cumulative investment performance table 10'), not a statement of a 10-year investment term. The risk profile document [P3] is the document that directly underpins the agreed risk level: it states an investment period of 2 years and a projection sum of £106,000 — materially inconsistent with a stated 10-year term. The 2-year period in the risk profile report is the horizon used to agree the risk level of 5 Balanced, not 10 years. No passage in the file links the 10-year term to the agreed risk rating or the recommended strategy. The guard notes that money left invested through retirement can legitimately have a longer horizon, but the report must say so — there is no such explanation in the evidence. The discrepancy between the 2-year projection period used to agree the risk level and the 10-year term asserted in the report is a material inconsistency: the time horizon used to support the risk profile in the underlying risk profiling tool is 2 years, while the suitability report claims 10 years. This is unsupported and unexplained. There is no vulnerability overlay information that materially affects this finding (the extraction notes the vulnerability assessment path was absent from the document).

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   Target risk rating 5 (Balanced). Two funds: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.18%; CT Universal MAP Balanced C at 50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.29%. Total allocation 100%. Strategy type: BespokePortfolio. Asset allocation breakdown absent from extracted model.
    File:     Risk profile report [P22] records natural ATR questionnaire score of 4 (Cautious Balanced) and an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) following interactive discussion. SIPP research report [P10] and [P8] confirm agreed risk level 5 Balanced. Research report [P16] states 'The client's agreed risk level is 5. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' Fund table in [P8] lists CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%). Suitability report fund table [P11] matches. KIID [P3] rates HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio as category 4 on its own 1-7 SRRI scale. Fund factsheet [P12] states 'The Fund's risk profile is rated as 3 where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk' using HSBC's own internal 1-5 scale. Research report [P16] confirms combined portfolio risk mapping of 5 on the firm's agreed scale. Asset allocation breakdown (equities/bonds/other) not separately evidenced in the extracted model though KIID [P2] states the balanced fund's typical range is 'Equities: 45-70%, Bonds: 20-55%, Other: 0-25%'.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report assigns both funds a risk rating of 5, and the firm's research confirms portfolio risk mapping 5. However the HSBC fund factsheet [P12] states its internal risk profile is rated 3 out of 5 (HSBC's own scale), and the KIID [P3] assigns it SRRI category 4 out of 7. Neither the factsheet nor the KIID confirms a risk rating of 5 for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio on the HSBC scale or the SRRI scale. The rating of 5 applied in the report appears to be the firm's client-agreed scale rating, not the fund's own published risk indicator, but this is not explicitly reconciled in the suitability report or research report.
      - No explicit asset allocation breakdown (e.g. equity/bond/cash split for the combined portfolio) is stated in the suitability report. The extraction confirms /solution/investmentStrategy/assetAllocation[] is absent.
      - ATR questionnaire result was 4; agreed rating used is 5. The uplift is noted in the risk profile document but the suitability report does not contain an explicit explanation of why the rating was moved upward by one notch.
    The core consistency check — agreed risk level 5 mapped to a portfolio rated 5 on the firm's scale — is supported by the research report [P16] which explicitly states the portfolio risk mapping matches the agreed level. The uplift from questionnaire score 4 to agreed rating 5 is documented in the risk profile report [P22] as the result of an interactive discussion and projection process, which is a standard and evidenced process on file; this is not an unexplained override. The guard applies: fund-level risk ratings from a provider (HSBC SRRI 4/7, HSBC internal 3/5) are not on the firm's client risk scale. The research report confirms the portfolio as a whole is rated 5 on the firm's scale, so the discrepancy between the fund's own published indicators and the firm's rating is explained by the scale difference — this is not a finding. However, the suitability report itself does not explain this distinction, which means a reader relying on the report alone would see fund risk ratings of 5 attributed to a fund whose factsheet says 3 or 4 without reconciliation. That is a disclosure shortfall. The absence of an explicit asset allocation breakdown in the suitability report is a minor gap; the KIID provides indicative ranges and the research report includes an asset allocation section [P18], so the information exists in the file even if not reproduced in the report. The questionnaire-to-agreed-rating uplift is evidenced (risk profile report) but not explained in the suitability report itself. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that materially affect this check. The report's claim that 'the funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' [P11] is only partially supported: the combined portfolio rating is supported by [P16], but the individual fund ratings of 5 stated in the report are not confirmed as 5 on the fund's own published scales. This is a disclosure shortfall rather than a material risk mismatch, and the outcome is a Potential Concern at Low severity.

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states the client completed an ATR questionnaire yielding a rating of 4 (Cautious Balanced). The adviser felt this did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite and agreed with the client to increase the rating to 5 (Balanced investor). The justification given is that the client is not reliant on pension funds, wishes for best growth potential over a 10-year period, and feels a balanced approach would achieve this goal. The client is said to have confirmed the increase, acknowledged it is higher than the questionnaire result, and confirmed they are happy to take on this risk with their pension.
    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) from the psychometric ATR questionnaire completed on 18/09/2025, with an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced). The sections for 'Agreed risk level' notes, 'Investment target' notes, 'Capacity for loss for the goal' notes, and 'Other notes' all record 'No additional notes were recorded'. The SIPP research report (category G) independently records the agreed risk level as 5 (Balanced). No narrative explanation for the upward override is captured in any supporting document outside the suitability report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report provides a detailed client-specific justification for the upward override (growth objective, non-reliance on pension funds, 10-year horizon) and records a client acknowledgement, but no corroborating notes for the override discussion appear in the risk profile report (D) or any other file document — the risk profile report's 'Agreed risk level' notes section explicitly states 'No additional notes were recorded'.
      - 	The risk profile report investment period is recorded as 2 years (P6), whereas the suitability report refers to a 10-year investment period as part of the override justification — these are materially different time horizons used in the same override rationale.
    The questionnaire result (4) and agreed rating (5) differ, so the guard — which says this group is not applicable where assessed and agreed ratings match — does not apply. An upward override has occurred and requires justification and client acknowledgement in the evidence. The suitability report does provide both a justification and a client acknowledgement, but these are [I]-category assertions: the report cannot corroborate itself. The supporting documents must independently evidence the override conversation. The risk profile report (D, [P1], [P2], [P6], [P14]) records the natural level as 4 and the agreed level as 5 but contains no notes in the 'Agreed risk level' section (explicitly 'No additional notes were recorded'), and no notes in the capacity for loss or other sections that would corroborate why the rating was raised. The SIPP research report (G, [P15], [P16], [P20]) records the agreed level as 5 but provides no override rationale. No file note or other category-C, D, G or H document records the adviser's reasoning for the override or the client's acknowledgement. The override is therefore asserted entirely within the suitability report with no independent file evidence. Additionally, the risk profile report (P6) states the investment period used in the projections was 2 years, whereas the suitability report cites a 10-year period as part of the override rationale — this is an internal inconsistency between the supporting document and the report's justification that further undermines the evidential basis for the override. The vulnerability extraction note flags that no vulnerability assessment is present for C1, but this does not materially affect this specific finding. The outcome is Potential Concern: the override is asserted but unsupported by independent file evidence, and the investment period used in the risk tool differs from the period cited in the override justification.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client (John Sullivan) completed an ATR questionnaire resulting in a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced), but that the adviser and client agreed to use risk rating 5 (Balanced). The report states the client is happy to proceed at rating 5, is not reliant on pension funds, wishes for best growth potential over a 10-year investment term, and understands that higher risk means potential for larger drops. Capacity for loss is assessed as Medium. The recommended investment strategy targets risk rating 5, invested in CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%) within an Aviva Pension Portfolio SIPP. Existing arrangements carry risk ratings of 9, 4, 2, 7 and 4 (Standard Life rated) on their respective scales.
    File:     [P3] (Risk profile report, category D) confirms the natural ATR questionnaire result is 4 (Cautious Balanced), completed 18/09/2025. [P3] also confirms that, following interactive discussion considering natural ATR, capacity for loss and required return, the agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced). [P2] and [P1] (SIPP research report, category G) both confirm the agreed risk level used for fund selection is 5 (Balanced) and that the recommended funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C, each at 50%. [P7] and [P18] (Pension switch report, category G) confirm the client's agreed risk level is 5 for the Zurich and Standard Life switches respectively. [P5] (suitability report, category I) recites the override rationale — questionnaire gave 4, client agreed to 5, client confirmed awareness of higher risk and non-reliance on pension funds over a 10-year period. [P24] (Risk profile report appendix, category D) records questionnaire responses: client described investment approach as 'Cautious', would feel anxious at a fall of more than £4,000 on £20,000 (20%), associated risk with 'Caution', described themselves as 'More cautious' compared to others. The capacity for loss section in [P24] states 'No additional notes were recorded'. The override from 4 to 5 is documented in the risk profile report but the capacity for loss section records no additional supporting notes.
    Discrepancies:
      - Questionnaire natural result is 4 (Cautious Balanced) but agreed/working rating is 5 (Balanced) — an upward override of one band.
      - The ATR questionnaire responses in [P24] are consistently cautious in character (investment approach: Cautious; risk association: Caution; self-description: More cautious; anxiety threshold: more than £4,000 fall on £20,000), which conflicts with the upward-adjusted rating of 5.
      - The capacity for loss section in the risk profile report ([P24]) records 'No additional notes were recorded', providing no independent rationale for the upward override beyond the narrative in the suitability report itself.
      - The suitability report provides a client acknowledgement and a narrative justification for the override (non-reliance on funds, 10-year growth objective), but the supporting evidence document ([P24]) contains no corroborating notes in the capacity for loss section.
      - Existing arrangement risk rating of 9 is stated but no scale is identified, making it incomparable to the client's agreed rating of 5 on the Defaqto/adviser scale; similarly the rating of 7 and ratings of 4 and 2 carry no scale identification apart from EA5's '4 (Standard Life rated)'.
      - Existing arrangement risk rating '7' (value 7) is not matched in the evidence passages; nearest passage value is 7.12 in [P10] which is a volatility figure, not a risk rating — the rating is unsubstantiated in the file evidence.
      - The report asserts capacity for loss as Medium, but the basis for this assessment relies on income sources and non-reliance narrative in the suitability report without independent corroboration in the risk profile document where the capacity for loss section is blank.
    The core strategy vs agreed rating comparison can be made: agreed rating is 5 and target strategy rating is 5, so no breach of rated strategy against agreed profile exists. The recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced) are confirmed as appropriate for risk level 5 by [P1], [P2] and [P3]. That part of the requirement is met.
    
    However, the upward override from questionnaire result 4 to agreed rating 5 raises a concern. The questionnaire responses recorded in [P24] are uniformly cautious in character, and the capacity for loss section in the same document records no notes at all. The justification for the override exists only in the suitability report itself ([P5]), which is category I and cannot corroborate its own assertion. The risk profile report ([P3]) confirms the agreed level is 5 following 'interactive discussion' but provides no independent recorded rationale for why the override is appropriate given the cautious questionnaire profile. Under the decision logic this is a questionnaire result overridden upward without independently evidenced justification — a High severity concern.
    
    Additionally, the capacity for loss is stated as Medium in the report, but the supporting documentation's capacity for loss section is blank. The report's narrative justification (income sources, non-reliance) is contained only in the suitability report itself and is not independently corroborated in the risk profile or any other category D/B/G/H document. This is a separate concern: capacity for loss asserted without independent evidence.
    
    The existing arrangement risk ratings of 9, 7, 4 and 2 appear on scales that are not identified and are not mapped to the client's agreed 5-point scale. The rating of 7 is not matched in the evidence. The report does not claim alignment of the whole existing portfolio to the client's agreed profile — these are existing holdings being transferred or retained — so this is an incomparability rather than a direct breach, consistent with the guard. However, the absence of scale identification means the ratings cannot be meaningfully reviewed.
    
    No vulnerability information was identified in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment (the extraction notes /vulnerability/C1/assessment as not present in document).

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) ATR questionnaire result was 4 ('Cautious Balanced'), but the adviser and client agreed to use rating 5 ('Balanced') on a 1–10 scale; (2) capacity for loss is 'medium', assessed as consistent with agreed ATR level 5, based on surplus income, savings of ~£6,000, pension funds of ~£106,000, and the client's confirmation that underperformance would not affect day-to-day living; (3) investment term is 10 years; (4) the recommended portfolio comprises two funds at 50% each — HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 5, OCF 0.18%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (risk rating 5, OCF 0.29%) — both rated 5 on the same 1–10 scale as the agreed ATR; (5) the strategy is described as a bespoke blend portfolio within the CIP, targeting risk rating 5 'Balanced', benchmarked against IA Mixed Investment 20–60% Shares TR; (6) the rationale states the funds reflect the risk profile individually and combined, provide diversification and active/passive blend, and are regularly reviewed via a quarterly investment committee.
    File:     [P15] (category D, risk profile report) records the natural/questionnaire risk level as 4 'Cautious Balanced' and the agreed risk level as 5 'Balanced', with the description matching what the report quotes. [P6] and [P12] (category G, SIPP research report) confirm the agreed risk level is 5 'Balanced' and list the two recommended funds at 50%/50%. [P12] shows the Defaqto research was conducted at agreed risk level 5. [P10] shows both HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc appeared on the risk-level-5 shortlist. [P9] (KIID for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio) states the fund is rated 3 on HSBC's own internal 1–5 risk scale (where 1 is lowest and 5 is highest within that range of Global Strategy Portfolios), and also carries a regulatory SRRI of 4 on the 1–7 SRRI scale. [P1] records the SRRI as 4. [P11] shows ongoing costs for HSBC at 0.18% and CT Universal MAP at 0.29%, consistent with the report. [P4] provides performance data for both recommended funds. The case file does not contain any specific capacity for loss questionnaire or standalone assessment tool; the only evidence of CFL assessment is the report's own narrative. The file contains no separate document evidencing the investment term of 10 years beyond the report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report assigns both funds a risk rating of 5 (on the adviser's 1–10 profiler scale). The HSBC KIID (P9) states the fund is rated 3 on HSBC's internal 1–5 scale for Global Strategy Portfolios, and carries a regulatory SRRI of 4 on the 1–7 scale. These are different scales, but the report presents '5' as the fund's risk rating without clarifying which scale it refers to, creating potential for confusion — though the guard confirms SRRI does not map onto the profiler's scale.
      - The report states pension funds total approximately £106,000 in the capacity for loss section, but the sum of the five individual plan values is £116,998.47 (per derived figures) and elsewhere the report cites ~£110,000. No figure across the report agrees with another.
      - The questionnaire result of 4 was adjusted upward to 5 (agreed rating). The report states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' but provides no further specific justification or documented client acknowledgement of this upward override beyond the report's own narrative. The risk profile report (P15) records the agreed level as 5 but the basis for the upward move is described only in the suitability report itself.
      - The capacity for loss is described as 'medium' and stated to be 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5'. This conflates CFL with ATR rather than demonstrating an independent assessment of the client's ability to absorb loss.
      - The investment term of 10 years is stated in the report but no corroborating document in the evidence pack independently evidences this figure.
      - The HSBC KIID (P9) states the fund is 'not appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' — the client's 10-year term exceeds this, so no mismatch on term, but the fund's minimum recommended period is 5 years not 10.
    The core alignment question — whether the recommended funds sit at risk level 5 on the adviser's profiler scale — is supported by P15 (natural level 4, agreed level 5), P6, P12 and P10 (Defaqto research conducted at agreed level 5, both funds on the level-5 shortlist). The strategy risk rating equals the agreed rating on the adviser's stated scale. The funds' names ('Balanced') are marketing labels, but the Defaqto research confirms they were selected and screened at risk level 5 on the profiler's own system, so the alignment claim is substantiated on this limb. The SRRI/internal HSBC rating discrepancy (fund rated 4 on SRRI, 3 on HSBC's own scale, presented as 5 in the report) is explained by the guard: SRRI and proprietary fund-house scales run on different bases and do not map onto the profiler's 1–10 scale. This difference should be noted but does not constitute a suitability failure under this check. The KIID minimum holding period (5 years) is shorter than the client's 10-year term, so no mismatch arises there. The upward override of ATR from 4 to 5 is a genuine concern. The report states only that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' — a generic assertion. P15 (the risk profile report, category D) records the agreed level as 5 and gives the 'Balanced' description, confirming the output, but neither P15 nor any other category-D or category-B document contains the detailed rationale for why 4 was insufficient or records the client's explicit acknowledgement of the upgrade. The report cannot corroborate itself on this point. The lack of documented justification for an upward override is a Weak justification concern. Capacity for loss: the report asserts 'medium capacity for loss, consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5'. The phrase 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level' indicates the CFL conclusion was anchored to the ATR outcome rather than derived independently. No separate CFL tool output or standalone assessment document is in the evidence pack. The financial data cited (income, savings, expenditure) is present in the report's own narrative only; no category-B or category-D document independently corroborates the CFL determination. This is a Weak justification / Data inconsistency concern (CFL as restatement of ATR). The pension fund total cited in the CFL section (£106,000) is inconsistent with other figures in the same report (~£110,000 on p.3, £116,998.47 summed from individual values, £110,185 in the recommended solution table). This internal inconsistency in a figure used to support the CFL assessment is a Data inconsistency. The investment term of 10 years has no corroborating evidence in the file beyond the report itself; this is missing evidence. Vulnerability: the extraction flags that /vulnerability/C1/assessment is absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument). There is no evidence of a formal vulnerability assessment in the pack. The suitability report does not discuss any vulnerability considerations that would affect the risk or strategy assessment. This is noted but not a separate finding under this check — CHK-010 owns vulnerability directly. Overall: multiple concerns subsist — upward ATR override without evidenced justification or client acknowledgement; CFL stated as consistent with ATR rather than independently assessed; pension fund figure used in CFL section inconsistent with other figures in the report; investment term unsupported by external evidence. The strategy risk level itself is corroborated by the Defaqto research, so the core alignment is substantiated, but the surrounding inputs to that alignment (override rationale, independent CFL, consistent fund values) are not adequately evidenced.

        "4  Cautious Balanced"
      · D/Risk profile report.docx [D] (P15)

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The questionnaire produced a result of 4 (Cautious Balanced); the adviser and client agreed to use rating 5 (Balanced) on the stated ground that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite'. The client confirmed awareness that rating 5 is higher than the questionnaire result and accepted the additional risk. Capacity for loss is assessed as medium, based on surplus income, savings of ~£6,000, no liabilities, pension funds 'totalling approximately £106,000', and the client's confirmation that underperformance would not affect daily living. The investment term is 10 years. Both recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 50%, CT Universal MAP Balanced C 50%) carry a risk rating of 5, matching the agreed ATR. The strategy is described as a bespoke CIP blend. The override is documented with a client acknowledgement. Tolerance for loss is addressed only by a general risk warning ('funds may go up as well as down') with no quantified maximum acceptable loss.
    File:     [P1] and [P8] (Risk profile report, category D) confirm the natural/questionnaire risk level as 4 (Cautious Balanced). [P8] and [P2]/[P5] (SIPP research report, category G) confirm the agreed risk level as 5 (Balanced) with the same descriptive text used in the report. [P5] confirms both recommended funds at 50/50 allocation each at risk level 5. [P8] records the investment amount as £106,000 and investment period as 2 years for the risk profile projection — not 10 years. [P15]/[P16] (capacity for loss questionnaire responses, category D) record the client's actual questionnaire answers but no adviser notes on the agreed-level discussion are recorded ([P3], [P4]). The capacity for loss section in the risk profile report ([P4]) notes 'No additional notes were recorded'. The pension switch report ([P13]) and file notes ([P7]) reference existing fund risk levels (Aviva risk 2, Zurich risk 7, Standard Life risk 4). The KIID for CT Universal MAP ([P22]) classifies the fund in risk category 4 ('medium fluctuations historically'), with equity allocation 45–70%. The report states pension funds total ~£106,000 but the derived figures and extraction note three inconsistent totals: ~£110,000 (page 3), ~£106,000 (page 4 CFL section), and £117,998.47 (sum of five individual plan values).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states pension funds total approximately £106,000 in the capacity for loss section, but the sum of the five individual plan values in the canonical model is £116,998.47 (derived figure), page 3 of the report states approximately £110,000, and the recommended solution table on page 9 states £110,185 — none of these three figures agree with each other or with the CFL narrative figure of £106,000.
      - The risk profile projection in [P8] uses an investment period of 2 years and investment of £106,000, whereas the suitability report states the investment term is 10 years. The projection tool was run on a 2-year horizon, not 10 years.
      - The suitability report's override justification is limited to 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' and a reference to the client's growth objective and non-reliance on pension funds. No further factual basis for overriding upward from 4 to 5 is documented in the supporting file; [P3] and [P4] note 'No additional notes were recorded' for both agreed risk level and capacity for loss discussions.
      - The KIID for CT Universal MAP Balanced C ([P22]) places the fund in risk indicator category 4, not 5. The report assigns both funds a risk rating of 5. This is a potential scale/methodology difference but is not explained in the report.
      - Tolerance for loss is not quantified — no maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary amount is stated anywhere in the report or the file.
      - The capacity for loss section restates income and asset facts that substantially overlap with the ATR section, and the conclusion ('medium capacity for loss, consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5') links CFL directly to the ATR rating rather than deriving it independently, which risks CFL being a restatement of ATR rather than a separate assessment.
    Several issues arise under this catch-all requirement. First, the pension fund total used in the CFL narrative (£106,000) is inconsistent with the total on page 3 (~£110,000), the recommended solution table (£110,185) and the derived sum of five individual plan values (£116,998.47). The CFL assessment rests in part on the size of the pension asset base, so an incorrect or inconsistent figure weakens the evidential basis of the assessment. Second, the risk profile report ([P8]) was run with an investment period of 2 years and £106,000, not the 10-year term stated in the suitability report. The projection shown to the client to justify the agreed risk level therefore used a materially different time horizon, which is relevant to whether the override from 4 to 5 was properly explained. Third, the override from questionnaire result 4 to agreed rating 5 is documented with a client acknowledgement and stated on grounds that the questionnaire did not fully reflect risk appetite, supported by the growth objective and non-reliance on pension funds. These are legitimate grounds on their face, but the supporting file records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both the agreed risk level discussion and the capacity for loss discussion ([P3], [P4]), leaving the override with thin evidential support beyond the report's own assertions. The report cannot corroborate itself. Fourth, the CT Universal MAP Balanced C KIID ([P22]) places that fund in risk category 4, while the report assigns it risk rating 5. This discrepancy is unexplained. It may reflect different rating methodologies (the KIID uses the PRIIP/KIID synthetic risk indicator, the report uses a 1–10 adviser scale), but the report does not acknowledge or reconcile the difference. Fifth, tolerance for loss is addressed only by a general risk warning without quantification, meeting the low-severity threshold identified in the guidance. The CFL conclusion being expressed as 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5' rather than derived independently from financial circumstances is a mild concern (moderate per guidance) but is partially mitigated by the factual detail provided in the supporting narrative. The investment period mismatch between the projection tool (2 years) and the stated term (10 years) and the inconsistent pension total used in the CFL section are the most material residual concerns after applying the guards. Standard risk-warning boilerplate is not treated as unsupported. The fund performance and diversification rationale are not assessed here. The override direction and acknowledgement are documented, so this does not reach the 'High' override-without-justification threshold, but the thinness of the supporting file evidence and the projection mismatch mean the justification is weak.

  [G3.11] Funds inside a retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The only arrangement retained on advice is People's Pension (EA1), holding 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' which the report assigns a risk rating of 9 on the existing arrangements table. The agreed client risk rating is 5. The report acknowledges the mismatch, stating 'the funds do not meet your risk profile' and suggests the client 'should consider switching your investments to better align with your risk rating by moving into the Global Investments (up to 60% shares) fund.' The plan is retained solely because of ongoing employer contributions. No formal recommendation to switch the fund is made — only a suggestion to consider it.
    File:     The risk profile report (D) confirms the questionnaire natural result was 4, with an agreed risk level of 5. The SIPP research report (G) confirms the agreed risk level is 5. No evidence in the file confirms that the People's Pension fund has been switched or that any instruction was given to move from the risk-9 fund to a risk-5 aligned fund. The file notes (C) reference the Zurich and other alternatives but do not address the People's Pension fund switch. No KIID or factsheet for the 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' or the proposed 'Global Investments (up to 60% shares)' fund is present in the retrieved passages to evidence the fund risk ratings independently, though the report assigns the current fund a rating of 9.
    Discrepancies:
      - The client's agreed risk rating is 5; the retained People's Pension fund 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' is assigned a risk rating of 9 by the report — a material mismatch of 4 risk levels above the agreed profile.
      - The report does not make a formal recommendation to switch the fund; it only states the client 'should consider switching' — meaning the client remains invested at risk level 9 with no actioned remedy.
      - No independent evidence (KIID, factsheet or provider documentation) for 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' at rating 9 or the proposed replacement 'Global Investments (up to 60% shares)' is present in the file to corroborate the risk ratings assigned by the report.
      - The rationale for retaining the plan (employer contributions) addresses why the plan is kept, not why the fund inside it is left misaligned with the agreed risk profile.
    The People's Pension is the only arrangement retained on advice. The report explicitly acknowledges that the fund held within it — 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)', rated 9 — does not meet the client's agreed risk profile of 5. The guard states that a rationale for retaining the plan (here, employer contributions) explains the retention but does not answer for the fund inside it; where the report gives one rationale and says nothing adequate about the fund's risk level, that is the finding. The report's suggestion to 'consider switching' is not a recommendation: per the guard, where the report stops short of recommending, treat it as no recommendation made. An unactioned suggestion leaves the client invested outside the agreed profile and is not a fix for the mismatch. Accordingly, the client is left with a fund rated 9 inside a retained arrangement where the agreed profile is 5, with no firm recommendation made to correct this. The fund ratings (9 for existing, 5 agreed) are both asserted by the report itself; no independent KIID or factsheet for these People's Pension funds appears in the retrieved passages to corroborate or contradict the ratings, so the report's own assertions stand uncorroborated but also uncontradicted on the rating values. The core finding is the acknowledged mismatch left unresolved by a mere suggestion rather than a recommendation. No vulnerability factors in the file appear to affect this specific assessment materially. The severity is at the highest level per the guidance: strategy risk (fund rating 9) exceeds the agreed profile (5) in a retained arrangement with no binding corrective action.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The suitability report describes the client's knowledge as 'Limited', which is broadly consistent with questionnaire responses of 'No understanding / knowledge' across most investment categories — however 'Limited' is marginally more positive than the 'No understanding / knowledge' returned on pension planning, stocks and shares, and unit trusts. The Balanced investor description used in the report (P15) states suitability for someone with 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters', yet the questionnaire (P1, P10) records 'No understanding / knowledge' against every investment and pension category, and no investment experience in any product type in the last 5 years other than cash savings. The report relies on the Balanced (risk level 5) investor profile to describe the client, whose questionnaire profile aligns far more closely with 'No understanding / knowledge' than with someone having 'some experience' — the Balanced investor description in the risk profile report itself (P5) notes it requires 'limited or moderate experience' at minimum (Cautious Balanced, level 4), and the Balanced level 5 requires 'some experience'. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model records the client's stated knowledge level as 'Limited'. The suitability report (P15) states 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and describes John as a Balanced investor (risk level 5), noting 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.'
    File:     The risk profile report (P1, P10) records the following questionnaire responses for John Sullivan: 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself? — No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work? — No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works? — No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts work? — No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments work? — Very little understanding / knowledge'. Experience questions: no investment in unit trusts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs or pension products in the last 5 years. Only current/savings accounts invested in, by himself. The risk profile report's Balanced investor description (P2, P14) states it may be suited to someone with 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters', which the questionnaire responses do not support.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report describes the client's knowledge as 'Limited', which is broadly consistent with questionnaire responses of 'No understanding / knowledge' across most investment categories — however 'Limited' is marginally more positive than the 'No understanding / knowledge' returned on pension planning, stocks and shares, and unit trusts.
      - The Balanced investor description used in the report (P15) states suitability for someone with 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters', yet the questionnaire (P1, P10) records 'No understanding / knowledge' against every investment and pension category, and no investment experience in any product type in the last 5 years other than cash savings.
      - The report relies on the Balanced (risk level 5) investor profile to describe the client, whose questionnaire profile aligns far more closely with 'No understanding / knowledge' than with someone having 'some experience' — the Balanced investor description in the risk profile report itself (P5) notes it requires 'limited or moderate experience' at minimum (Cautious Balanced, level 4), and the Balanced level 5 requires 'some experience'.
      - No file note or record of an adviser explanation to the client of the product complexity (SIPP with platform investment into two balanced multi-asset funds) relative to the client's recorded nil/very limited knowledge is present in the passages provided — P3 and P4 record 'No additional notes were recorded' in the Investor Experience and agreed risk level sections.
      - The capacity for loss section of P15 acknowledges experience is 'limited to workplace pension arrangements', which is itself contradicted by the questionnaire (P1) answer 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years? — No'.
    The report records the client's knowledge as 'Limited', and the suitability report narrative acknowledges experience is 'limited to workplace pension arrangements'. This is not wholly inconsistent with the questionnaire outputs, which return 'No understanding / knowledge' or 'Very little understanding / knowledge' across all categories. However, a material tension exists: the report deploys the Balanced investor (level 5) framing, whose own description — repeated verbatim in both the risk profile report (P2, P14) and the suitability report (P15) — states suitability for a person with 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. The questionnaire evidence directly contradicts this: every investment and pension knowledge question returns 'No understanding / knowledge', and there is no pension investment experience in the last five years (P1). The report's own capacity for loss section says experience is 'limited to workplace pension arrangements', but the questionnaire says there has been no investment in pension products in the last five years either — a further internal tension. The risk level 5 recommendation is connected to the knowledge narrative because the Balanced investor description is explicitly incorporated into the knowledge/experience framing. The first false-positive guard notes that a meeting explanation could post-date the fact find, but P3 and P4 record 'No additional notes were recorded' in the Investor Experience and agreed risk level sections, so no such explanation is evidenced. The second guard distinguishes self-assessed comfort from assessed knowledge — here the questionnaire responses are assessed answers, not self-assessments, so this guard does not resolve the mismatch. The third guard confirms that 'No understanding / knowledge' recorded against a direct question is an assessed answer: the report describing the same client as suited to a Balanced approach (requiring 'some experience') is a genuine contradiction. The recommended products — a personal pension (SIPP) on an investment platform with two multi-asset balanced funds — are not highly exotic, but they are materially more complex than anything the client has previously used. There is no recorded explanation of the product features or complexity relative to the client's nil knowledge level. On balance, the file evidences a client with no investment knowledge or experience beyond cash savings, while the report frames him within a Balanced investor description that presupposes some experience and understanding. This is a data inconsistency between the questionnaire evidence and the report's knowledge framing, and the absence of any recorded explanation or comprehension check is a further gap.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section) states: (1) John confirmed he has previously taken out cash savings products but has not taken out a stocks & shares investment by himself. (2) He has held group/workplace pensions for some time and feels comfortable with how investments work, with a reasonable understanding of potential risks and rewards. The report therefore characterises John as having cash savings experience, no self-directed equities experience, but meaningful practical familiarity with pensions, and treats that familiarity as sufficient to support the pension consolidation/SIPP recommendation.
    File:     The risk profile report (category D, [P1]) contains John Sullivan's investor-experience questionnaire responses dated 18/09/2025. The directly relevant rows are: (a) 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them?' — 'No understanding / knowledge'. (b) 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' — 'No understanding / knowledge'. (c) 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?' — 'Yes, by myself'. (d) 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' — 'No'. The risk profile report also records no additional notes under 'Investor Experience' [P7]. The agreed risk level narrative [P11] describes a 'Balanced' investor as suited to someone with 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters', but notes this was the agreed level, not the natural level (which was 4 — Cautious Balanced). Existing policy documents confirm John holds/held multiple pension arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, Scottish Widows, Standard Life, People's Pension), but the guard is explicit that plan ownership is not evidence of knowledge or understanding.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states John 'feel[s] comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' derived from holding group pensions for some time. The risk profile questionnaire records his answer to 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' as 'No understanding / knowledge' — a direct contradiction.
      - The suitability report implies pension experience gives John meaningful comprehension of investment risk and reward. The risk profile questionnaire records 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' as 'No', undermining the report's claim of active, ongoing pension experience.
      - The suitability report records no stocks & shares self-investment — consistent with the questionnaire answer 'No' to shares/unit trusts questions. This element is not a discrepancy.
      - The suitability report records cash savings experience — consistent with the questionnaire 'Yes, by myself' for savings/deposit accounts. This element is not a discrepancy.
    The core discrepancy is material. The suitability report characterises John's pension-holding experience as conferring a 'reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' and comfort with 'how investments work'. The fact-find risk questionnaire, completed the day before the report was finalised, records his self-assessed pension understanding as 'No understanding / knowledge' and his recent pension investment activity as 'None'. These two positions are directly opposed and cannot both be correct.
    
    The guard that 'holding a workplace pension is passive experience; a report distinguishing it from self-directed investing is being accurate, not inconsistent' does not resolve this discrepancy — the report does not merely note passive holding; it positively asserts a 'reasonable understanding' and 'comfort' with investment risk derived from that holding, which the questionnaire explicitly contradicts.
    
    The guard that 'plans held do not prove understanding' reinforces the concern: the five existing pension plans evidence passive membership, not comprehension.
    
    The recommended product is a SIPP (Aviva Platform Pension Portfolio — Choice, a self-invested pension with access to thousands of investment choices [P23]) alongside consolidation of multiple arrangements. This is a moderately complex product requiring some understanding of investment selection, fund risk and pension mechanics. Recommending it to a client whose questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning, while the report simultaneously claims he has a 'reasonable understanding', creates a suitability concern.
    
    No category C corroboration of comprehension (questions asked, explanations given, client acknowledgements recorded) appears in the file. The investor experience section of the risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' [P7].
    
    The vulnerability overlay: /vulnerability/C1/assessment is noted as NotPresentInDocument, so no formal vulnerability assessment was recorded. No other passage in the file flags a vulnerability characteristic that would independently affect this finding.
    
    The discrepancy between the report's assertion of 'reasonable understanding' and the questionnaire's 'No understanding / knowledge' is a direct contradiction of the kind the decision logic marks as High severity — the report's description of knowledge contradicts the fact find.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The recommended product is an Aviva Platform Personal Pension (Flexi-Access Drawdown) — a personal pension wrapper with FAD, involving pension consolidation of multiple transfers, a multi-asset fund portfolio with ongoing rebalancing, and IHT implications from April 2027. The extraction infers this as 'Complex'. The report explains features of FAD, lifetime allowance changes, annual allowance, death benefits, and IHT treatment. The client (C1) is the primary advised client. No explicit knowledge/experience grade is assigned to C1 in the canonical model for this check, but the report recommends this product.
    File:     The file contains Aviva Pension Portfolio Key Features [P20], Terms and Conditions [P2, P3, P5, P7, P10, P11, P13, P14, P15, P18], and KIIDs for the CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund and HSBC funds [P21, P23, P24, P8, P9]. These confirm the product is an Aviva Platform Personal Pension with FAD available [P1, P20]. The funds are multi-asset balanced funds rated 4/7 on risk/reward [P23]. The SIPP research report [P16] records the agreed risk level as 5, with questionnaire result of 4. No fact-find document is present in the retrieved passages that records C1's stated knowledge level, investment experience grade, or any comprehension check. No vulnerability assessment for C1 is present (extraction flags /vulnerability/C1/assessment as NotPresentInDocument). The Aviva T&Cs note that direct (non-advised) clients cannot purchase complex financial instruments [P15], but this is an advised case so that restriction does not apply.
    Discrepancies:
      - No explicit knowledge/experience level for C1 is recorded in the canonical model or evidenced in the retrieved passages — the report does not assign a knowledge grade and no fact-find passage corroborates one.
      - The product is inferred as 'Complex' by extraction (FAD, consolidation, IHT) but the report does not itself assign a complexity label, and no corroborating evidence of C1's comprehension of these features is present in the file.
      - The vulnerability assessment path /vulnerability/C1/assessment is flagged as NotPresentInDocument, meaning the vulnerability overlay cannot be applied — it is unknown whether any vulnerability characteristic affects the suitability of a complex product for C1.
    The guard states that a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not complex and complexity should not be escalated on product volume or value alone. However, the recommended product here is not a simple accumulation pension: it involves FAD (variable income withdrawals), pension consolidation across multiple schemes, a multi-asset portfolio with ongoing rebalancing, and IHT planning implications from April 2027. The extraction correctly identifies these as complexity drivers beyond a plain accumulation product. The key question is whether the file evidences that C1's knowledge and experience are commensurate with these features, and whether comprehension is demonstrated. The retrieved passages contain no fact-find extract, no knowledge/experience grade, and no record of client questions or explanations given in relation to FAD mechanics, IHT implications, or the consolidation rationale. The suitability report itself (category I passages [P4, P17]) explains product features generically but this is the report asserting its own adequacy — it cannot corroborate itself. The SIPP research report [P16] records only an agreed risk level of 5 (questionnaire 4), which is attitude to risk and excluded from this check unless relied on to evidence knowledge. No passage from a fact-find, client questionnaire, or adviser note evidences C1's actual investment knowledge level or prior experience with drawdown or pension consolidation. The absence of a vulnerability assessment for C1 is also noted: if any vulnerability characteristic were present it could be material to whether a complex FAD product is suitable, but this cannot be assessed from the file. The finding is that the report recommends a product with genuine complexity drivers (FAD, IHT, consolidation) without the file evidencing the client's knowledge level or comprehension of those features. This is a missing evidence / disclosure shortfall concern at moderate severity: the product has unusual features (FAD income flexibility, IHT from 2027) for which no record of client questions or explanations appears in the file.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited' and the recommended product complexity as 'Complex'. The report states that John's experience is limited to workplace pension arrangements, that he understands returns are not guaranteed, and that he confirmed any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living. The report also states the client wants to keep the investment strategy fairly simple, and recommends an Aviva Personal Pension invested in two balanced multi-asset funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 50% and CT Universal MAP Balanced C 50%), both rated risk 5. The report reproduces the Balanced investor description, stating 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.'
    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records the client's knowledge questionnaire responses as: no understanding/knowledge of stocks and shares; no understanding/knowledge of EIS/VCTs; no understanding/knowledge of pension planning and income in retirement. Investments in pension products within the last 5 years: No. Investments in unit trusts/GIAs within the last 5 years: No. Only savings/deposit/cash accounts invested in by the client themselves. The investor experience section records no additional notes. The natural risk level questionnaire result was 4 (Cautious Balanced), whose description states 'If you have limited or moderate experience of investments'. The agreed risk level was raised to 5 (Balanced), whose description states 'If you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. The SIPP research report (category G) confirms the agreed risk level of 5 Balanced. The capacity for loss section of the suitability report (category I) itself states experience is limited to workplace pension arrangements. No category C record of client comprehension or questions/explanation session appears in the pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records the knowledge level as 'Limited' and simultaneously applies the Balanced (risk 5) investor description, which presupposes 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' — the file's questionnaire responses record no understanding/knowledge of pension planning, stocks and shares, or EIS/VCTs, and no investment experience in any product other than savings/deposit accounts, which is below even the precondition for Cautious Balanced (limited or moderate experience).
      - The recommended product is recorded as 'Complex' but the client's demonstrated knowledge from the fact-find questionnaire is effectively nil for investments and pensions — the report does not reconcile this gap or explain how a complex product is appropriate for a client with no recorded understanding of pension planning.
      - No category C record (client questions, explanation session, or comprehension check) is present in the pack to corroborate that the client understood the complex product recommended, as required where the product has unusual features or complexity.
      - The report states the client 'wants to keep the investment strategy fairly simple', yet the canonical model records the product complexity as 'Complex' — no rationale bridges this stated preference and the complexity classification.
      - The risk profile description reproduced in the report ('some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters') is not corroborated by the file questionnaire responses, which record no investment experience and no understanding of relevant product types.
    The report asserts Limited knowledge but simultaneously deploys the Balanced investor description, which carries a precondition of 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. The file's own questionnaire responses contradict this: the client recorded no understanding of stocks and shares, no understanding of pension planning and income in retirement, and no investment experience in any product class other than savings accounts. The natural risk questionnaire placed the client at 4 (Cautious Balanced), whose own description references only 'limited or moderate experience' — itself a higher bar than the file supports. The risk level was then raised to 5 without any corroborating evidence of the knowledge the level 5 description requires. The guard in the methodology is directly engaged: 'A risk-profile description carries preconditions about the client's experience. Where the report reproduces one whose preconditions the file contradicts, the description is being used as evidence of knowledge it does not establish.' That is precisely what has occurred here. The report labels the product as Complex and the client's knowledge as Limited, but offers no explanation of how a Complex product is suitable for a client whose file records effectively zero investment knowledge or experience. The client's own stated preference for simplicity ('want to keep the investment strategy fairly simple') heightens the concern rather than resolving it. No category C evidence — such as a record of questions asked, explanations given, or a comprehension check — appears in the pack to support a conclusion that the client understood the recommended product. The 'you confirmed you understand' type assertion in the suitability report is an adviser assertion, not independent corroboration. The vulnerability overlay path (/vulnerability/C1/assessment) is noted as absent from the extraction, so no vulnerability factors can be identified as bearing on this finding. The mismatch between the file's knowledge record and the product complexity classification, combined with the absence of any comprehension evidence, constitutes a material concern.
