  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section) that the client 'is comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards.' It further states (page 4, Capacity for Loss section) that 'although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' The report thus characterises the client as having a reasonable but experience-limited understanding, derived exclusively from workplace pension exposure.
    File:     The fact find (category B, [P21]) records the meeting as a video call on 18/09/2025 but contains no recorded knowledge or experience grading for the client. The risk profile report (category D, [P14], [P16]) explicitly states 'No additional notes were recorded' under both 'Investor Experience' and 'Agreed risk level' sections. The file note (category C, [P2]) repeats verbatim the same wording as the suitability report regarding limited experience and understanding of non-guaranteed returns, but this is an [I]-category parallel and not independent corroboration. No category C, B or D document records any client questions asked during the advice process, any explanation provided of complex product features, or any assessment of the client's comprehension of the recommended Centralised Investment Portfolio (CIP) — a multi-fund, actively-monitored construct with layered charges — which represents a product of moderate-to-high complexity relative to a standard pension.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report characterises the client as having a 'reasonable understanding' of risks and rewards, but the risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience — providing no graded or narrative corroboration of the stated understanding level.
      - The report does not record any client questions, nor any explanation of the CIP's unusual features (multiple funds, active oversight, layered charge structure), yet the product is materially more complex than the workplace pension arrangements the client's experience is acknowledged to be limited to.
      - The file note ([P2]) repeats the same knowledge/experience language as the suitability report but is category C (file notes), which may itself have been populated from the report rather than from an independent source; in any event it adds no additional evidence of comprehension beyond what the report already asserts.
      - The risk profile report ([P14]) section on Investor Experience contains no notes, meaning there is no independently recorded baseline of knowledge or experience against which the report's assertion of 'reasonable understanding' can be verified.
    The recommended product is an Aviva Platform pension consolidating three switching arrangements, invested in a CIP comprising multiple funds across asset classes with active oversight — a construct the report itself describes as more complex than a single multi-asset fund ([P4], [P5], [P7]). The client's acknowledged experience is explicitly limited to workplace pension arrangements. Against this backdrop, COBS 9.2.1R and COBS 9A.2.1R require that the client's knowledge and experience be sufficient to evaluate the product's risks and features. The guard acknowledges that absence of recorded questions is not proof of non-comprehension, but it directs that the gap be reported weighted by product complexity. Here the product is complex and the client's baseline is stated to be limited — so the absence of any comprehension evidence is a material gap, not a minor one. The risk profile report ([P14], [P16]) records no investor experience notes at all. The file note ([P2]) merely mirrors the suitability report's own wording and being category C cannot independently corroborate the report's assertion; it is at best internal consistency. No document in category B, C or D records client questions about the CIP structure, explanations of the layered charging, or any adviser assessment of whether the client demonstrated comprehension of features that distinguish a CIP from the simpler arrangements the client has previously held. The vulnerability overlay adds a further dimension: the report notes at page 4 that the client's experience is explicitly 'limited' — this is itself a mild vulnerability indicator for the knowledge dimension. There is no evidence of any tailored explanation or adjusted communication to bridge that gap. The outcome is a Potential Concern: the report asserts reasonable understanding of a complex product but this is unsupported by any independent evidence of comprehension, and the product's complexity relative to the client's admitted limited experience makes the gap material.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

The Aviva plan operates under an automatic Lifestage switching approach, further limiting its value as evidence of active investment decision-making. This is a Potential Concern at moderate severity: the report makes a knowledge claim that the file does not independently evidence, though the existence of multiple pension holdings over time does provide some, limited, corroboration of pension ownership experience.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report classifies the recommended product as Complex, driven by four identified complexity drivers: (1) pension consolidation involving multiple transfers; (2) flexi-access drawdown with variable income withdrawals; (3) multi-asset fund portfolio with ongoing rebalancing; (4) IHT implications from April 2027. The report then provides client-specific rationale for each element of the recommendation: retaining People's Pension because employer contributions continue and transfer would lose that benefit; withdrawing Scottish Widows under small pots rules; transferring Aviva because of lifestyling risk and lower cost in new solution; transferring Zurich and Standard Life with reasons including fund range limitations, lack of flexible access, adviser management unavailability, and cost comparisons. The report further sets out the client's knowledge and experience as limited to workplace pension arrangements, confirms the client understands values can rise and fall and that returns are not guaranteed, and records an agreed ATR of 5 (Balanced investor) with awareness that this exceeds the questionnaire result of 4.
    File:     The file note (P8) confirms the client's experience is described as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and records the ATR discussion, the override from 4 to 5, and the client's understanding of risk. The risk profile report (P13, P14) records no additional notes in the 'Investor Experience' or 'Agreed risk level' sections. The fact find (P20) records retirement planning as the advice area and that the meeting was a recorded video call. The SIPP research report (P3, P4) records platform selection as Aviva Platform from a panel of four. The file notes (P10, P11) record adviser research into alternative options and reasons for discounting them, including fund range limitations at Scottish Widows, Zurich, Standard Life and People's Pension. The HSBC KIID (P1) and fund factsheet (P5) confirm the recommended fund (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio) is a multi-asset fund rated risk 3 on the HSBC internal scale, investing across global markets with a five-year-plus horizon. The Aviva Key Features (P2) confirm the Aviva Pension Portfolio supports flexi-access drawdown. No file document records specific questions asked by the client about the complex features, nor any client comprehension check of IHT implications from April 2027 or drawdown mechanics.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client's knowledge and experience is sufficient to support a complex recommendation involving flexi-access drawdown, IHT planning and multi-asset rebalancing; however the file records client experience as explicitly 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' (P8) and the risk profile report records no additional notes in the Investor Experience section (P14), providing no corroboration of understanding beyond basic workplace scheme familiarity.
      - The risk profile report (P13, P14) records no additional notes in the 'Investor Experience' and 'Agreed risk level' sections, leaving the report's characterisation of the client's understanding of the complex product features uncorroborated by the fact-finding documents.
      - No file document records a client comprehension check, explanation given, or questions raised regarding the IHT implications from April 2027 — a forward-looking regulatory change cited as a complexity driver — despite this being an unusual and technically complex feature.
      - No file document records a client comprehension check or explanation regarding flexi-access drawdown mechanics and variable income withdrawal, which is a named complexity driver.
      - The file note (P8) records the ATR wording as containing an apparent internal inconsistency ('You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6' followed immediately by confirmation of risk rating 5), suggesting a possible data quality issue in the recorded understanding, though this does not directly contradict the report's stated ATR of 5.
    The report correctly identifies the recommendation as complex and enumerates four complexity drivers. It provides client-specific rationale for each transfer and retention decision, which addresses the 'why recommend this product' dimension adequately at a transactional level. However, the adequacy of the rationale for recommending a complex product to this particular client — given their recorded knowledge and experience — is the issue under assessment here.
    
    The file is unambiguous that the client's investment experience is limited to workplace pension arrangements (P8). The risk profile tool records no supplementary notes on investor experience (P14). This means the only corroboration of the client's capacity to understand the complex features is the file note's brief reference to understanding that values can rise and fall. There is no recorded evidence that the IHT implications from April 2027, flexi-access drawdown mechanics, or multi-asset rebalancing were explained to and understood by the client. The report does not itself contain a comprehension check or record of client questions on these features.
    
    The HSBC KIID (P1) and fund factsheet (P5) confirm the fund's nature as a multi-asset portfolio, and the Aviva Key Features (P2) confirm drawdown capability. These are product documents; they do not evidence client comprehension.
    
    The rationale for each individual transfer decision is present and client-specific, which is a positive. However, the absence of any recorded explanation of the complex features — particularly IHT implications and drawdown mechanics — to a client whose file records only limited experience, and the risk profile report's blank investor experience section, means the rationale is incomplete for a complex recommendation. The report asserts suitability of complexity for this client's knowledge level, but the file provides no corroborating evidence of comprehension where the product's complexity warrants it.
    
    The ATR wording anomaly in P8 ('risk rating of 6' / 'risk rating of 5') is noted but does not materially alter the finding; the report consistently states ATR 5.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability flags are recorded in the file that materially affect this assessment.
    
    The finding is a Moderate concern: there is no record of client questions or explanations where the product has unusual features (specifically IHT from April 2027 and drawdown mechanics), and the investor experience section is blank, leaving the report's complexity rationale unsupported by comprehension evidence in the file.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). The report states household expenditure is 'approximately £1,700 per month'; the Fact Find records £1,718.00 per month — a £18 difference, not a rounding to £1,700. The Fact Find shows total monthly disposable income of negative £288.00 (income £1,430 minus expenditure £1,718), meaning the household is running a monthly deficit on the recorded figures; the report asserts income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure. The report's affordability assertion is based on combined income meeting expenditure, but the Fact Find monthly income figure of £1,430 net appears to reflect only a portion of combined income (Kim's £1,100/month alone, or one earner), whereas the cashflow modeller uses John's income of £15,600 + £1,040 p/a and Kim's £13,200 p/a (combined circa £29,840 gross / approximately £2,487 gross monthly). The Fact Find net income figure of £1,430/month is inconsistent with the combined gross income used in the cashflow model — the basis of the net figure is unexplained. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts an initial commitment of £3,305.55 (3% of amount invested, paid from pension transfers), an ongoing annual commitment of circa £1,101.85 (1% per annum ongoing service, based on current values minus initial fee), and that affordability is satisfied because combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, savings of around £6,000, and no outstanding liabilities. No new regular income contributions are being made — the recommendation is a pension consolidation via transfer of existing funds. Capacity for loss is confirmed as not impacting day-to-day living.
    File:     The Fact Find [P1] records total net monthly income of £1,430.00, total monthly expenditure of £1,718.00, and a total monthly disposable income of negative £288.00. The cashflow modelling [P5, P8] records John's income as £15,600 p/a (CDH Recruitment) plus £1,040 p/a (Sea Cadets) and Kim's income as £13,200 p/a (£1,100 monthly), giving a combined gross income of approximately £29,840 p/a. The Fact Find savings figure of £6,000 [P1] is corroborated by the proposed scenario [P5]. The suitability report [P7] confirms the initial fee of £3,305.55 at 3% and [P22] confirms the ongoing fee of £1,101.85 at 1%. No new monthly contribution is being made from income — the investment is funded entirely by pension transfers. The baseline cashflow [P14] shows liquid assets declining rapidly from approximately £6,000 with a deficit position in the first year before pension assets become accessible.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states household expenditure is 'approximately £1,700 per month'; the Fact Find records £1,718.00 per month — a £18 difference, not a rounding to £1,700.
      - The Fact Find shows total monthly disposable income of negative £288.00 (income £1,430 minus expenditure £1,718), meaning the household is running a monthly deficit on the recorded figures; the report asserts income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure.
      - The report's affordability assertion is based on combined income meeting expenditure, but the Fact Find monthly income figure of £1,430 net appears to reflect only a portion of combined income (Kim's £1,100/month alone, or one earner), whereas the cashflow modeller uses John's income of £15,600 + £1,040 p/a and Kim's £13,200 p/a (combined circa £29,840 gross / approximately £2,487 gross monthly). The Fact Find net income figure of £1,430/month is inconsistent with the combined gross income used in the cashflow model — the basis of the net figure is unexplained.
      - The emergency fund of £6,000 savings is stated but never quantified against an explicit emergency fund standard or number of months' expenditure in the report; it represents approximately 3.5 months of expenditure at £1,718/month. No commentary addresses whether this is sufficient given the deficit cash position.
      - The initial fee of £3,305.55 is deducted from the pension transfer value, reducing investment — this is consistent with the guard that fees taken from the fund do not test against monthly surplus — but no explicit confirmation that the clients are aware the investable amount is reduced is provided in the affordability section.
      - The 3% initial charge arithmetic: 3% of £110,185 (total transfer value) = £3,305.55, which corroborates the figure internally, but 3% of the five-arrangement sum of £116,998.47 would be £3,509.95, and 3% of the cashflow model transfer inputs (£3,744 + £103,439 + £3,002 = £110,185) = £3,305.55 — consistent with the three switching plans only, not all five arrangements.
    The central affordability concern is the Fact Find monthly cashflow: income of £1,430.00 net against expenditure of £1,718.00 produces a deficit of £288.00 per month [P1]. The report asserts that combined income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month — this is directly contradicted by the Fact Find data. The expenditure figure in the report (£1,700) also understates the Fact Find figure (£1,718) by £18, which is not a rounding difference that explains the directional claim of comfort. The cashflow modeller [P5, P8] records combined gross incomes of approximately £29,840 p/a, which would yield a materially higher net income than £1,430/month, suggesting the Fact Find net income figure may represent only one earner or an incomplete entry — but the report does not explain the discrepancy and relies on the Fact Find income position to justify affordability. The recommendation involves no new contributions from income (pure pension transfer consolidation), so the guard applies: ongoing adviser charges are deducted from the pension fund, not from the clients' monthly cash surplus. The ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 p/a does not need to be tested against monthly surplus on that basis. However, the report's positive affordability assertion — that income 'comfortably meets' expenditure — is the claim at issue, and that claim is not supported by, and is contradicted by, the Fact Find deficit position. The emergency fund of £6,000 is corroborated by [P1] and [P5], but the report does not quantify it against a months-of-expenditure standard. At £1,718/month expenditure, £6,000 covers approximately 3.5 months, which is a common benchmark minimum. This alone would be a low-severity observation, but in the context of a monthly deficit, the resilience of the £6,000 buffer is more material because there is no evidenced surplus to replenish it. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to raise additional concerns for this specific check beyond those already identified — no specific vulnerability characteristics in the evidence alter the affordability analysis. The initial fee of £3,305.55 is taken from the pension transfer, consistent with [P7], and is arithmetic-consistent with 3% of the three switching plan values (£110,185). The discrepancy between the report's £1,700 expenditure figure and the Fact Find £1,718 is a data quality issue; the more significant concern is the contradiction between the 'comfortably meets' assertion and the evidenced £288/month deficit, which is a data inconsistency constituting a Potential Concern at Moderate severity.

  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Net asset value £6,000 (cash savings), no liabilities. John earns £300/week as HGV driver plus £20/week sea cadets; Kim earns £1,100/month. John receives state pension £11,973 p.a. already; Kim will receive £11,973 p.a. from age 67. Retirement expenditure approximately £1,700/month. Cashflow modelled to age 100 with no shortfall projected. Investments modelled: Aviva transfer £3,744, Zurich transfer £103,439, Standard Life £3,002. Three stress tests (6% inflation, -30% market crash combined with high inflation, and longevity to ONS life expectancy) all show no shortfall. Surplus income percentage saved 0%. Kim stated to retire at 66 (page 3) but cashflow uses state pension from age 67. No explicit emergency fund discussion beyond the £6,000 cash asset. People's Pension (£6,601.55) not listed as a cashflow input.
    File:     Fact Find [P2]: total net monthly income £1,430, total monthly expenditure £1,718, producing a monthly deficit of -£288 on current figures. Fact Find income detail [P22] shows basic income £1,200/month and state pension £230/month for a combined £1,430/month — these are Kim's figures only (John's HGV/sea-cadet income not separately broken out in the same table). Intelliflo proposed scenario [P24]: total assets £122,997; liquid assets £6,000; pensions £116,997. Proposed scenario income statement [P13]: first-year total regular income £41,813 (state pension £11,973, employment combined £29,840); total regular expenditure £24,001 including tax and NI. Proposed scenario cashflow tables [P10, P13] show consistent in-year surpluses throughout with no deficit column other than at ages beyond 100. Baseline scenario tables [P7, P12, P23] similarly show surpluses to age 100 and liquid assets/accessible pensions stable around £122,952–£123,009, with a small deficit only from age 100/96 onwards. Intelliflo proposed scenario [P24] shows Kim's state pension age as 66 in the heads-of-household section. Cashflow report [P6] uses expenditure of £1,700/month in retirement. People's Pension (£6,601) appears in the Intelliflo asset tree [P24, P16] but is not listed as a cashflow transfer input [P6].
    Discrepancies:
      - Fact Find current monthly cashflow shows a deficit of -£288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), yet the suitability report presents the cashflow as sustainable with no shortfall and makes no reference to this pre-retirement deficit or how it is bridged.
      - The suitability report cashflow uses John's income as £300/week HGV (£15,600 p.a.) plus £20/week sea cadets (£1,040 p.a.) = £16,640 p.a. and Kim's income as £1,100/month (£13,200 p.a.), giving combined employment income of £29,840 p.a. The Fact Find records total net monthly income of £1,430 (£17,160 p.a.), a material difference from the cashflow model's £29,840 p.a. employment income. The report does not reconcile this gap.
      - Kim's state pension age: page 3 of the suitability report states she plans to retire at 66 (implying state pension at 66); the cashflow assumptions state state pension from age 67; the Intelliflo proposed scenario heads-of-household section [P24] shows state pension age 66. The cashflow model uses 67, which is inconsistent with two other references to age 66.
      - The People's Pension (£6,601.55) is listed in the asset tree and opening values [P16, P24] but is not included in the cashflow investment inputs listed in the report [P6] ('Aviva transfer £3,744, Zurich transfer £103,439, Standard Life £3,002'). Its treatment in the cashflow is unexplained.
      - No emergency fund is explicitly identified or quantified. The only liquid asset is £6,000 cash savings. The report does not discuss whether this is sufficient as an emergency reserve given that contributions/transfers are being made.
      - The Fact Find income figure of £1,430/month includes state pension of £230/month, suggesting John is already retired or the state pension is already in payment, yet the cashflow treats state pension as a separate ongoing income stream — the components and timing are not reconciled in the report.
    The central financial resilience concern is the material gap between the Fact Find's current monthly cashflow position and the cashflow model inputs. The Fact Find records combined net monthly income of £1,430 against monthly expenditure of £1,718, producing a monthly deficit of -£288. However the cashflow modeller uses combined employment income of £29,840 p.a. (approximately £2,487/month) plus John's state pension of £11,973 p.a., giving total current income substantially higher than the Fact Find figure. The suitability report does not explain this discrepancy. This is a data inconsistency between the fact find and the cashflow inputs that materially affects the reliability of the 'no shortfall' conclusion — if the Fact Find figures are correct, the clients are currently spending more than they earn and the cashflow model overstates their resources. The Kim state pension age inconsistency (age 66 in two places, age 67 in the cashflow) is a further data inconsistency that affects when income enters the model, though the Intelliflo proposed scenario confirms state pension age 66 for Kim [P24], meaning the cashflow assumption of age 67 is at odds with the agreed plan data. The People's Pension omission from the cashflow investment inputs is flagged as an ambiguity in the extraction metadata (possibly intentional as an ongoing workplace scheme), but the report does not explain its absence, creating a weak justification for the completeness of the modelled asset base. The emergency fund is not addressed: £6,000 is the only liquid asset and no commentary confirms whether this is ring-fenced as an emergency reserve or whether it is available to be drawn down. Given the clients are tenants with no property equity, the adequacy of this reserve is a live question the report does not answer. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability flags in the evidence materially alter this specific affordability finding beyond what is already noted. The cashflow output files themselves (baseline [P7, P12, P23] and proposed [P10, P13]) do show consistent surpluses and no red-bar shortfalls to age 100/96, which corroborates the report's 'no shortfall' narrative at the model level — but that corroboration is only as good as the inputs, and the input discrepancy with the Fact Find is unresolved. The guard on modelled main residence does not apply (the clients are renters and no property is modelled). The guard on growth assumptions does not apply here. Overall: the cashflow modelling output supports the no-shortfall conclusion when taken at face value, but the inconsistency between the Fact Find income/expenditure figures and the cashflow model inputs, the Kim state pension age discrepancy, and the absence of emergency fund commentary collectively constitute material concerns about whether the affordability conclusion is reliably grounded.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the clients hold savings of around £6,000, which it asserts is sufficient liquid capital to cover unexpected emergencies. It makes no quantified assessment of what level of emergency fund is required. It also states the clients are comfortable with the pension transfer process because they hold liquid cash for instant access needs, and that pension fund access after transfer will take 10–15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes. No required emergency fund amount is stated.
    File:     The Fact Find (P6, P14) confirms cash savings of £6,000. The Fact Find (P14) records total net monthly income of £1,430 and total monthly expenditure of £1,718, producing a monthly deficit of -£288 — i.e. expenditure exceeds income on a current-month basis before any retirement income flows. The cashflow modelling (P7) shows liquid assets of £6,000 at 2025 declining slowly over time (e.g. £5,956 at 2026, £5,912 at 2027), consistent with modest drawdown of cash savings, with pension assets growing. The baseline scenario (P16) shows comparable liquid asset figures. The proposed scenario cashflow (P7) shows surpluses in-year throughout, and the baseline (P5) similarly shows surpluses once retirement income flows. No document quantifies the required emergency fund. No competing claims on the £6,000 are identified in the evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the emergency fund is 'sufficient' but neither the report nor any supporting document quantifies what level of emergency fund is required, so the sufficiency claim cannot be verified against a defined requirement.
      - The Fact Find records a current monthly cash deficit of -£288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), which the report does not acknowledge or address in the context of emergency fund adequacy or affordability during the pre-retirement period.
      - The report presents no cashflow or affordability analysis reconciling the evidenced monthly deficit against the adequacy of the £6,000 emergency fund reserve, particularly given the deficit would erode the cash buffer at approximately £288/month (roughly 20 months to depletion if no other income changes).
      - No required emergency fund amount is stated anywhere in the report or evidence, meaning the sufficiency assertion rests entirely on the client's own confirmation with no independent validation.
    The report asserts the emergency fund of £6,000 is sufficient, relying solely on the client's own confirmation. The Fact Find corroborates the £6,000 figure. However, the report does not quantify what level of emergency fund is required, and the guard 'an asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be verified — record it as unquantified rather than as a contradiction' applies. That said, the report's claim of sufficiency is further weakened by a material affordability signal in the evidence that the report is silent on: the Fact Find records a monthly deficit of -£288 (net income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718). At that rate the £6,000 would be eroded in approximately 20 months, which is close to the stated retirement horizon (approximately 2 years for Kim, slightly more for John given cashflow modelling from 2027). The report makes no reference to this deficit, does not explain how the shortfall is being met, and does not address how the emergency fund remains intact given ongoing negative cashflow. The cashflow model (proposed scenario, P7) shows in-year surpluses once retirement income flows but the model uses different income inputs (employment income at £15,600 p/a for John and £13,200 p/a for Kim) compared to the Fact Find's figure of net monthly income of £1,430 (approximately £17,160 annualised net), suggesting the cashflow model inputs may reflect gross or different earnings assumptions. The discrepancy between the Fact Find's deficit position and the cashflow model's surplus position is not reconciled in the report. The emergency fund claim is therefore both unquantified (severity Low per guidance) and sits against an unaddressed evidenced monthly deficit that raises an additional affordability concern. No competing claims on the £6,000 were identified. No vulnerability factors in the evidence appear to materially affect this specific finding beyond what is already captured.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts affordability is satisfied. It states combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, savings of around £6,000, and no outstanding liabilities. The initial adviser charge is £3,305.55 (3% of amount invested). The ongoing adviser charge is approximately £1,101.85 per annum for the first year. No monthly surplus figure is stated in the report.
    File:     The Fact Find (P1, P2) records total net monthly income of £1,430 and total monthly expenditure of £1,718, producing a monthly disposable income of -£288 (a deficit). JS's net basic monthly income is £1,200 and a state pension of £230 per month is also recorded (P2, P17). The cashflow modeller baseline scenario (P11) shows annual employment income for JS of £16,640 and combined of £29,840, with total regular expenditure of £24,001 including tax and NI. The proposed scenario cashflow tool (P21) shows liquid assets of £6,000 in savings. The report states household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts combined income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month. The Fact Find records total net monthly income of £1,430 and total monthly expenditure of £1,718, producing a deficit of -£288 per month — not a surplus.
      - The report states household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month. The Fact Find records total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00 — a modest difference but the report rounds down from a deficit position.
      - No monthly surplus figure is stated in the report or captured in the canonical model (/financialPosition/totals/monthlySurplusOrDeficit absent). The Fact Find shows a deficit, not a surplus, meaning the affordability claim is directly contradicted.
      - The initial adviser charge of £3,305.55 and ongoing charge of approximately £1,101.85 per annum are asserted as affordable, but no evidenced surplus from which these could be met is identified — the Fact Find shows a monthly deficit of £288.
      - The report does not explain how a -£288/month cashflow position supports the commitment to an initial lump sum charge of £3,305.55 drawn from the pension transfer proceeds, nor how ongoing charges of £1,101.85 per year are serviced from income.
      - The emergency fund (savings ~£6,000) is referenced but not quantified in the context of the monthly deficit or the initial charge — if the initial charge were met from savings, the £6,000 reserve would be significantly reduced or eliminated.
    The central affordability claim in the report — that combined income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure — is directly contradicted by the Fact Find, which records a monthly deficit of -£288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718). The report states expenditure of 'approximately £1,700 per month', which is a rounding of the Fact Find's £1,718, but critically the income figure used in the Fact Find is only £1,430, producing a deficit, not a surplus. The report does not disclose any monthly surplus figure, and the extraction confirms /financialPosition/totals/monthlySurplusOrDeficit is absent from the report. This is not an extraction failure — the report is simply silent on the surplus, and the file contradicts the affordability assertion. The initial adviser charge of £3,305.55 is taken from the pension fund at the point of transfer, not from income, so it does not in itself create a monthly affordability problem. However, the ongoing charge of approximately £1,101.85 per annum (approximately £91.82/month) would need to be serviced somehow; with a monthly deficit of £288, this is unclear. The cashflow modelling tool (P11) records combined annual employment income of £29,840 and total regular expenditure of £24,001 (including tax and NI), yielding an apparent annual surplus of approximately £5,839. However, this annual cashflow figure is not reconciled with the Fact Find's monthly cashflow showing -£288/month, and the report does not use or reference the annual cashflow figure in its affordability analysis. The savings of £6,000 are cited as evidence of liquidity, but given the ongoing deficit, these reserves risk being eroded over time rather than maintained as an emergency fund. The guard regarding employment income due to cease within the term is relevant: JS intends to retire in approximately 2 years, which would reduce income further. The report does not address how affordability would be maintained post-retirement. No vulnerability factors in the file appear to materially alter this finding beyond what is already identified. The core finding is that the report's affordability assertion is unsupported by and contradicted by the Fact Find cashflow data.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that liquidity and access needs are funded from 'Cash savings'. The canonical model does not record a specific emergency fund amount, a shortfall figure, or any explicit commentary on the adequacy of emergency reserves. The extraction confirms the absence of /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall is due to the field not being present in the document (NotPresentInDocument), meaning the report is silent on emergency fund quantification.
    File:     The cashflow planning files (Baseline and Proposed scenarios) show a single Cash Account / Savings holding of £6,000 belonging to JS. In the Proposed scenario asset statement, liquid assets open at £6,000 in 2025 and decline gradually year on year (e.g. £5,956 in 2026, £5,912 in 2027, £5,869 in 2028, continuing to fall). The Surplus Account is £0 throughout. The liquidation order in the Proposed scenario places Savings as 'LIQUIDATED 2ND' (after the Surplus Account). Total regular expenses are £20,616 p/a. Income sources include employment income for JS (£15,600 + £1,040 Sea Cadets until 2027) and Kim (£13,200 until 2026), plus State Pension of £11,973 each from age 66. The cashflow tables show positive in-year surpluses throughout the plan, and liquid assets are not projected to be exhausted. No separate emergency fund pot is identified in the file beyond the £6,000 savings.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts liquidity needs are funded from 'Cash savings' but does not quantify the emergency fund amount or confirm it is sufficient relative to expenditure commitments (£20,616 p/a regular expenses).
      - The extraction confirms the report contains no emergency fund shortfall figure or adequacy commentary — the report is silent on whether £6,000 is sufficient as an emergency reserve.
      - The Proposed scenario shows liquid assets (£6,000 opening) declining gradually each year, suggesting the £6,000 is being partially drawn upon over time rather than preserved as a ring-fenced emergency reserve, though the cashflow remains positive throughout.
      - No separate emergency fund is identified in the file; the £6,000 savings is the only liquid asset, and it is listed as the second asset to be liquidated in the drawdown order, meaning it would be accessed early if a shortfall arose.
      - The £6,000 savings represents less than four months of total regular expenditure (£20,616 p/a), with no commentary in the report on whether this level is adequate for the clients' needs or circumstances.
    The report asserts that liquidity and access needs are funded from cash savings but provides no quantification of the emergency fund, no adequacy assessment, and no commentary on whether £6,000 is sufficient given the household's expenditure profile of £20,616 p/a. The cashflow modelling files confirm the £6,000 savings is the sole liquid asset and that it is positioned second in the liquidation order. The proposed scenario shows liquid assets declining steadily from £6,000, indicating some erosion over time, though the overall cashflow remains in surplus. The guard noting that a transfer between pension wrappers does not deplete an emergency fund is relevant: the pension consolidation itself does not consume the £6,000. However, the substantive concern is that the report never quantifies the emergency fund as adequate, never states what level of reserve is being maintained, and never addresses whether £6,000 — less than four months of household expenditure — is sufficient for two clients in their late 60s with illness events modelled. The claim that liquidity needs are 'funded from cash savings' is an assertion without supporting analysis. This is not explained away by any guard. The vulnerability overlay is relevant here: the cashflow model includes 'Illness (John)' and 'Illness (Kim)' events, indicating the file contemplates health-related vulnerability for both clients. In that context, the absence of any commentary on whether £6,000 is an adequate emergency buffer — particularly if unplanned care or health costs arise — is a meaningful gap. The emergency fund is asserted as sufficient but never quantified or justified; this meets the Low severity threshold in the guidance.

    File:     The Intelliflo proposed scenario [P19] shows liquid assets of £6,000 (savings) alongside pensions of £116,997 at plan outset. The cashflow model [P5][P12][P24] does not show a separate emergency fund assessment; the £6,000 liquid asset is the only identified liquid reserve.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not state a selected retirement age for the recommended plan (/solution/recommendedPlans[]/selectedRetirementAge absent) — no comparison of access date against wrapper restrictions is possible from the report side alone.
      - The report does not document planned withdrawals (/solution/recommendedPlans[]/plannedWithdrawals[] absent).
      - The report does not document liquidity or access needs with required-from dates (/financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/requiredFrom absent).
      - The emergency fund of £6,000 is recorded in the fact find and cashflow model but is never explicitly assessed for sufficiency against the recommendation in the suitability report narrative.
      - Kim's State Pension age is stated as 66 on page 3 of the report and in the Intelliflo proposed scenario [P19], but the cashflow assumptions state she receives state pension from age 67 — this internal inconsistency affects the cashflow income modelling underpinning affordability.
      - The report asserts combined pension fund value as approximately £110,000 (page 3) and approximately £106,000 (page 4 capacity for loss), while the sum of individual plan values is £116,998.47 and the cashflow baseline [P12] shows pensions of £116,997 — none of these figures agree, creating inconsistency in the financial position data underpinning the affordability assessment.
    The minimum pension age guard is satisfied: John is ~69 and Kim is ~65, both already past the current minimum pension age of 55 and also past the future threshold of 57 applicable from April 2028. Therefore, the PersonalPension wrapper does not restrict access in a way that conflicts with any near-term access need — the lock-up concern does not apply on the facts. However, several affordability and liquidity weaknesses remain. First, the £6,000 cash reserve is the only identified liquid asset outside pension wrappers. The report never explicitly quantifies whether this is an adequate emergency fund relative to the clients' expenditure — it is asserted by implication through the cashflow model but never addressed in narrative. This is a low-severity concern (emergency fund asserted as sufficient but never quantified). Second, Kim's State Pension age is inconsistently recorded as 66 (report page 3, [P19]) and 67 (cashflow assumptions), which means the cashflow income projection may be based on a different retirement income start date than the client's stated intention — this is a moderate-severity data inconsistency affecting the cashflow inputs. Third, the combined pension fund value cited in the report narrative (£110,000 / £106,000) is inconsistent with both the individual plan sum (£116,998.47) and the cashflow model figure (£116,997), weakening confidence in the financial position data underpinning the affordability assessment. Together these constitute material clarification needs: the cashflow inputs contradict the fact find on Kim's State Pension age, and the emergency fund is unquantified in narrative terms. The wrapper itself does not create a liquidity mismatch given client ages, but the affordability evidence base contains inconsistencies that require resolution. No vulnerability overlay concern is evident from the passages provided that bears specifically on this liquidity/affordability requirement beyond what is already captured.

  [G5.7] State Pension entitlement and other anticipated income sources
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model records income categories for the two clients as: Employment, Other, StatePension, Employment, StatePension, StatePension, StatePension.     File:     The Fact Find (P6) records one income line for a State Pension at £230.00 net per month (£2,760 per annum) for one client, alongside an employment income of £1,200 net per month.       - The report records StatePension as an income category but no gross amounts or start dates are present in the extracted model (confirmed NotPresentInDocument), so the quantum of State Pension income relied upon for affordability is unverifiable from the report alone.
      - The Fact Find (P6) records a State Pension of only £230 net per month (£2,760 p.a.) for one client, which is materially below the full new State Pension (approximately £11,500 p.a. at the relevant date), yet no explanation or forecast is provided to reconcile this figure.
      - The intelliflo baseline income statement (P8) shows JS total regular income of £28,613 but only £16,640 employment income is itemised, leaving approximately £11,973 attributable to State Pension/other income — consistent with the cashflow tool figure — but no supporting State Pension forecast document is on file to corroborate this.
      - No BR19 or State Pension forecast document is present in the evidence pack for either client, leaving the State Pension income figures relied upon in the cashflow modelling unsupported by independent evidence.
    The check can be performed to the extent that the available evidence permits, but it reveals several concerns. First, there is a direct internal inconsistency in the report between Kim's State Pension age stated as 66 on page 3 and the cashflow assumption of age 67 on page 14; the intelliflo planning documents (P1, P4) corroborate age 66, making the cashflow input the outlier. This is a data inconsistency within the suitability report itself and affects the affordability modelling. Second, and more fundamentally, the State Pension Entitlement section of the Fact Find (P5) is entirely blank. There is no BR19 projection, no Basic State Pension figure, and no Additional State Pension figure recorded for either JS or KS. The figure of £230/month in P6 appears to be a current or partial State Pension amount for one client only and is not explained. No independent State Pension forecast document is present in the pack. The cashflow modelling relies on State Pension income as a material source of future income (visible in P2 and implied by P8's income totals), but this income stream is not corroborated by any forecast or official statement in the file. This represents an unsupported assertion of a material future income source. The guard regarding weekly/annual conversion does not apply here as no weekly figure is present. The guard regarding qualifying years is relevant: the Fact Find does not record whether the £230/month figure represents a full entitlement, a partial entitlement at current qualifying years, or projected full entitlement — this ambiguity is unresolved. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to add a specific further concern for this particular sub-check beyond what is already identified. Overall, the affordability case depends materially on State Pension income for both clients post-retirement, but the evidence base for the quantum and timing of that income is absent from the file.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts five foreseeable changes: (1) John expects to retire within the next two years (modelled retirement date 2027-10-14), ceasing HGV driver and Sea Cadets income; (2) Kim intends to continue working until age 66 then retire fully; (3) Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week at State Pension age (modelled as age 67 in cashflow); (4) from 6 April 2027 unused DC pensions will be included in the taxable estate for IHT; (5) People's Pension contributions (John £1,193.33 PA, employer £897.13 PA) will cease at John's retirement. Emergency liquidity: £6,000 cash savings held for unexpected emergencies. Planned access: pension funds via Flexi Access Drawdown at retirement, with 10–15 working day access window. No foreseeable changes to expenditure are asserted beyond retirement income cessation.
    File:     Fact Find confirms £6,000 cash savings (JS, Cash Account). Fact Find cashflow shows total net monthly income £1,430, total monthly expenditure £1,718, and a monthly disposable income of -£288, meaning the household is already running a deficit before the recommendation. The Fact Find records no expected changes to income or expenditure in the foreseeable future. Proposed scenario cashflow (intelliflo) records John's State Pension age as 66 and Kim's State Pension age as 66; the proposed scenario timeline shows Kim retiring at age 66 in 2026 and John retiring at age 71 in 2027. The cashflow assumptions passage [P9] states 0% of any in-year surplus will be saved. The fact find records Kim's income as £1,200/month (basic, CDH Recruitment) and £230/month State Pension, and John's income totalling £1,430/month combined. Baseline cashflow opening assets: savings £6,000 and pensions £116,997. No liability is recorded. The Fact Find goal details note a required household income of £1,700 net per month at retirement.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states Kim's State Pension will be received at State Pension age and the cashflow models this at age 67 (page 14 of report); the proposed scenario planning tool (P12) records Kim's State Pension age as 66, not 67 — a direct conflict between the cashflow assumption in the report and the planning tool input.
      - The Fact Find records no expected changes to income in the foreseeable future, yet the report asserts John's retirement income will cease within two years — the Fact Find 'Income Changes' field explicitly says 'No' for both clients, which is internally inconsistent with the retirement plans described.
      - The Fact Find records no expected changes to expenditure in the foreseeable future ('No'), yet retirement is imminent and expenditure may change materially; no forward expenditure modelling is documented in the Fact Find.
      - The current monthly cashflow shows a deficit of -£288/month (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718) before any retirement income reduction occurs, but the report does not address how ongoing affordability is maintained now or after John's retirement income ceases — the report is silent on the pre-retirement deficit.
      - The report models Kim's retirement at age 66 (consistent with P12 timeline: 'Retire (Kim)' age 66 in 2026), but the cashflow assumption in the report uses State Pension age 67 for Kim, creating an internal inconsistency already flagged in the extraction.
      - The planning tool proposed scenario (P12) shows John's retirement event at age 71 in 2027, but the report states John expects to retire within two years from the advice date; the ages are consistent (report date implies John is ~69, retiring at ~71), but the planning tool's State Pension age for John is shown as 66 — already past — which is consistent with him having already reached State Pension age, though not explicitly discussed in the report.
      - The emergency fund of £6,000 is asserted as sufficient for unexpected emergencies, but no quantification of what level of emergency reserve is adequate for this household is provided, particularly given the existing monthly deficit of -£288 and the imminent loss of John's employment income.
      - The 0% surplus savings assumption (P9) means no liquid buffer accumulates from surplus income in the cashflow model, compounding the relevance of the existing deficit, but the report does not comment on this assumption or its implications for financial resilience.
    The central finding is a cluster of related concerns about planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs. First, the Kim State Pension age conflict: the report (page 3) states Kim retires at 66 and the planning tool (P12) records her State Pension age as 66, but the report's own cashflow assumptions (page 14) model State Pension receipt from age 67. This is an internal inconsistency in the report already noted in the extraction, and it is not explained by any guard — it is a genuine conflict between two figures on different sides of the same document, and the planning tool corroborates age 66, not age 67. Second, the Fact Find records 'No' to expected income and expenditure changes despite John's imminent retirement being the primary driver of the advice. This is a data quality issue in the Fact Find, but it also means the report's assertions about foreseeable income cessation are not corroborated by the Fact Find's forward-looking fields — the corroboration comes only from the narrative goal section (P17) and the planning tool, not from the structured income-change fields. Third, the existing monthly deficit of -£288 is evidenced in the Fact Find (P1) and is not addressed by the report in the context of affordability and financial resilience: if the household cannot cover expenditure from current income, the basis for asserting the recommendation is affordable is weakened, particularly as John's income will cease within two years. The report's emergency fund of £6,000 is corroborated (P6, P16), but against a -£288/month deficit and imminent retirement income loss, its adequacy is asserted rather than demonstrated. The 0% surplus savings assumption (P9) means no additional liquid buffer builds. The guard 'a future need recorded without a date is still a need' applies to Kim's retirement — undated in structural terms but reasonably anchored to age 66. The guard 'expenditure already inside the monthly budget must not be counted a second time' means the monthly expenditure items do not need separate treatment as future needs. On balance, the State Pension age inconsistency (66 vs 67) is a data inconsistency affecting cashflow modelling; the unaddressed pre-retirement deficit is a missing component of the affordability analysis; and the emergency fund adequacy is asserted without quantification against the household's actual cashflow position. No vulnerability overlay factors are identified in the evidence that materially alter this assessment beyond what is already captured.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.8, G6.9). Multiple rationale bullets are identical word-for-word across Aviva switch, Zurich switch and Standard Life switch recommendations ('The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options allowing a more tailored investment strategy', 'The new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly, ensuring ongoing financial planning support', 'Moving to the recommended scheme gives the adviser the ability to actively monitor and adjust investments as required', 'The receiving scheme provides more flexible retirement options, including tax-efficient withdrawal strategies') — these are generic boilerplate repeated verbatim, not client-specific language. The report states Standard Life existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8 narrative), but the canonical model's own internal inconsistency note records the switch charges table on page 11 showing the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life — a contradiction in direction of cost benefit. The report states Aviva existing plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution', but the charge arithmetic in derived figures shows EA5 annual charge and EA5 impact rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), making the charge comparisons for Standard Life unreliable. 13 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report provides rationale for six recommendation outcomes: (1) Retain People's Pension — employer contributions ongoing, transferring would lose those contributions; (2) Encash Scottish Widows (small pots rules) — no single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5 available, no adviser management permitted, pension freedoms flexibility restricted; (3) Switch Aviva — lifestyling moves portfolio outside agreed risk range, existing plan 0.06% more expensive, wider investment options, adviser charging, active monitoring; (4) Switch Zurich — no risk level 5 funds available, blended funds underperform, internal switch not recommended because consolidation is client's main priority, flexible retirement options, adviser charging, active monitoring, existing plan 0.08% more expensive; (5) Switch Standard Life — lifestyling, wider investment options, adviser charging, flexible retirement options, active monitoring; (6) New Aviva platform pension — tax-efficient pension building, client understands and prefers Flexi Access Drawdown, Aviva rated A (Stable) by S&P with £407bn AUM, funds reflect risk rating 5 Balanced, CIP regularly reviewed, like-for-like 0.24% less expensive (£264 saving in first year), including adviser fees 4.24% more expensive requiring 4.8% growth to age 75 to match current plans. Rationale items are linked to objectives OBJ1, OBJ2 and OBJ3. Several rationale bullets are flagged isClientSpecific:false (e.g. 'wider range of investment options', 'adviser charges deducted directly', 'flexible retirement options', 'adviser monitoring', 'tax-efficient pension building', 'Aviva S&P rating', 'CIP quarterly review'). Multiple identical bullets are repeated verbatim across the Aviva switch, Zurich switch and Standard Life switch rationales.
    File:     Policy research [P1/P4/P8] confirms: Aviva — recommendation Switch, reasons 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; Zurich — Switch, 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; People's Pension — Retain, 'Active workplace pension'; Scottish Widows — encash under small pots; Standard Life — Switch. The objectives linked (OBJ1–OBJ3) are named in the report but no passage in the evidence pack spells out what OBJ1, OBJ2 and OBJ3 are by label.

      - Multiple rationale bullets are identical word-for-word across Aviva switch, Zurich switch and Standard Life switch recommendations ('The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options allowing a more tailored investment strategy', 'The new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly, ensuring ongoing financial planning support', 'Moving to the recommended scheme gives the adviser the ability to actively monitor and adjust investments as required', 'The receiving scheme provides more flexible retirement options, including tax-efficient withdrawal strategies') — these are generic boilerplate repeated verbatim, not client-specific language.
      - The report states Standard Life existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8 narrative), but the canonical model's own internal inconsistency note records the switch charges table on page 11 showing the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life — a contradiction in direction of cost benefit.
      - The report states Aviva existing plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution', but the charge arithmetic in derived figures shows EA5 annual charge and EA5 impact rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), making the charge comparisons for Standard Life unreliable.

      - Several rationale bullets are flagged isClientSpecific:false in the canonical model itself, including the Aviva S&P rating/AUM, tax-efficient pension, CIP quarterly review, flexible retirement options and adviser monitoring bullets — these appear across multiple recommendations without client-specific anchoring.
      - The report states combined pension value of approximately £110,000 (page 3) and approximately £106,000 (page 4); derived figures show the sum of five individual plans is £116,998.47, and the recommended solution table shows £110,185 — none of these agree, undermining the client-specific financial position underpinning the rationale.

    The report contains rationale for each of the six recommendation outcomes and links them to objectives, which satisfies the structural requirement. However, several material concerns arise. First, at least four rationale bullets are reproduced verbatim across three separate switch recommendations (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) without any client-specific adaptation — this is boilerplate. The canonical model itself flags these as isClientSpecific:false. Second, the cost rationale for the Standard Life switch contains a directional contradiction: the narrative says the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the switch charges table (per the internal inconsistency note) shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive. The derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows are computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically unreliable. This means the cost-benefit rationale for at least one switch recommendation is wrong in direction — a highest-severity indicator under the decision logic. Third, the overall combined fund value cited in support of the client's financial position is inconsistent across three references in the report (£110,000, £106,000, £116,998.47 sum) and none agrees with the recommended solution table (£110,185). These are client-specific figures and their inconsistency undermines the financial basis of the rationale. Fourth, Kim's State Pension age is given as 66 in one place and 67 in the cashflow assumptions — a client-specific fact inconsistency. Fifth, no passage outside the report itself corroborates the Aviva S&P rating, AUM, the 0.06%/0.08% charge differentials, the 0.24% like-for-like saving or the Zurich performance comparison; the research file [P8] leaves the objective column blank for all arrangements. The adviser-charging rationale is partially corroborated by [P1] confirming adviser charging is only available on the recommended Aviva platform. The boilerplate bullets, the directional cost error for Standard Life, and the unsupported charge differentials collectively constitute material concerns. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would separately affect this requirement's assessment — CHK-010 is the appropriate locus for that.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report recommends the Aviva Platform Pension Portfolio (SIPP/PersonalPension wrapper) for C1, with three pension switches (Aviva EA3, Zurich EA4, Standard Life EA5) consolidating £110,185 into the new plan. Provider rationale: Aviva rated A (Stable) by S&P, £407bn AUM (31 Dec 2024), 6,900+ funds, transparent/competitive charges, online convenience. Investment strategy: two funds recommended — HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk 5, 0.18% pa) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk 5, 0.29% pa) — justified on risk alignment, diversification, active/passive blend, simplicity, competitive charges, peer-relative performance and CIP governance. The funds are part of the firm's CIP reviewed by a quarterly investment committee with external research oversight. Investment pathways were considered and discounted as insufficiently tailored. A CIP vs single multi-asset fund comparison was performed and CIP preferred. The provider rationale is not presented as client-specific (isClientSpecific: false for all provider rationale items); two investment rationale items are flagged as client-specific (risk profile alignment, simplicity preference, multi-asset rationale linked to OBJ3).
    File:     SIPP research report [P1/P5/P7/P9]: A Defaqto research exercise was conducted across 4 platform products and 118 SIPP products. No filters were applied to the platform search; shortlist of 4: Aviva Platform, Elevate (Aberdeen), Aegon ARC, Wealthtime. No DNA analysis was applied. For the SIPP search, 1 product matched from 118 after filters: Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice. No additional justification text was entered for the recommended product. Fund research [P10/P12]: Performance data and MiFID II ex-ante cost disclosures are provided for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc and CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc confirming ongoing costs of 0.18% and 0.29% respectively and positive peer-relative performance figures. Policy research [P13]: Adviser notes show Aviva (EA3) recommended for switch ('Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'), Zurich (EA4) recommended for switch ('Expensive, no adviser servicing'), People's Pension retained ('Active workplace pension'), Scottish Widows small pot noted. Standard Life recommendation field visible but truncated in the extract. Key Features [P8]: Confirms Aviva Platform is a portfolio management service; Pension Portfolio is a SIPP. Terms and conditions [P11]: Standard Aviva terms. Zurich policy info [P17]: Confirms no guarantees, bonuses, GMP, safeguarding, lifestyling, MVR or enhanced TFC apply; drawdown not available on existing Zurich plan but transfer available. Suitability report passages [P2/P4]: CIP vs investment pathways comparison present in the report itself (category I — not independent corroboration).
    Discrepancies:
      - Provider rationale is entirely generic (isClientSpecific: false for all five provider rationale items) with no linkage to C1's specific circumstances, objectives or existing arrangements — this is confirmed by the canonical model's own flags.
      - The platform Defaqto search applied no filters and no DNA analysis, so no feature-by-feature comparison of the four shortlisted platforms (Aviva, Elevate, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) is documented in the research file; the report asserts competitive/transparent charges and online convenience but no comparative charge or feature evidence for the alternatives is provided.
      - No additional justification text was entered in the SIPP research report for the recommended product [P5], leaving the selection rationale unsupported by research narrative beyond the filter outcome.
      - The report states the provider was selected partly for 'competitive and transparent charges' but no charge comparison between Aviva and the alternative platforms is present in the research documents.
      - The policy research parateplan notes [P13] show the objective field for each existing arrangement recommendation is blank ('Which objective/goal is this achieving?' — left empty for Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension), indicating the link between each switch recommendation and a named client objective was not completed in the supporting file, and the suitability report does not fill this gap with named objective references for the switch rationale.
      - The report's mid growth rate for the critical yield is stated as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative but 2.95% in the page 18 appendix (internal inconsistency already noted in extraction).
      - The combined pension fund value is stated as approximately £110,000 (page 3), approximately £106,000 (page 4), and the individual plan values sum to £116,998.47 per derived figures (or £117,998.47 per extraction note — the derived figures here show £116,998.47), while the recommended solution table shows £110,185 transfer value — none of these agree, and the rationale for consolidation does not acknowledge or reconcile the discrepancy.
    The report provides a structured provider and investment rationale, and research documents do exist. However, several consistency concerns arise.
    
    1. Provider selection rationale is wholly generic. Every item under /solution/providerSelection/rationale is flagged isClientSpecific: false. The report makes absolute claims (financial strength, competitive charges, online convenience) without linking them to C1's objectives, circumstances or the comparison platforms. The Defaqto platform research [P1] shortlisted four products but applied no filters and no DNA analysis, so there is no documented feature or charge comparison to corroborate the 'competitive charges' assertion. This is a weak justification finding — absolute marketing claims without comparative evidence, exactly what the guard flags as insufficient.
    
    2. SIPP product selection research [P5/P9] shows one product matched from 118 after filters, with no additional justification text entered. The filters that drove the result are visible in the research file but no qualitative rationale was recorded, leaving the selection supported only by the filter outcome.
    
    3. Investment fund rationale is better evidenced. Fund charges [P10], performance data [P12] and the CIP governance claim [P4] are all consistent with what the research file shows. The active/passive blend, diversification and risk-alignment claims are corroborated by the fund data. The client-specific simplicity preference and risk profile alignment are at least internally stated, though they rest on the report alone (category I passages).
    
    4. The per-arrangement switch rationale in the policy research parateplan [P13] leaves the objective fields blank for all arrangements. The suitability report does provide switch rationale text (lifestyle fund, no adviser servicing, cost) for each switching plan, but the supporting parateplan does not cross-reference named objectives, weakening the evidence that the recommendation was assessed against C1's stated goals.
    
    5. The fund performance claim ('performed well in comparison with their peers') is corroborated by the research performance tables [P12], which show positive discrete and cumulative returns for both funds. This claim stands.
    
    6. The CIP vs investment pathways comparison [P2] and CIP vs single multi-asset fund comparison appear only in category I passages and cannot self-corroborate, but the research file does contain fund-level data consistent with the CIP approach.
    
    7. The vulnerability overlay: the extraction notes /vulnerability/C1/assessment as NotPresentInDocument. No vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the canonical model, so no overlay adjustment is required for this check.
    
    8. The guard on firm panel/CIP membership as legitimate justification applies: the report states the funds are part of the CIP reviewed quarterly, which is a documented process justification. This partially offsets the lack of fresh whole-of-market fund comparison.
    
    Overall: the provider selection rationale is generic and the platform comparison is undocumented beyond a no-filter shortlist, which is a Potential Concern at Low-to-Moderate severity. The investment rationale is better supported but still partly generic. The objective-linkage gap in the parateplan is a secondary concern. This does not rise to High severity because research documents do exist and investment rationale has meaningful corroboration; the primary gap is the absence of a documented comparative platform justification.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model records isClientSpecific as true for the majority of rationale entries (21 out of 31), but also records false for 10 entries. The report asserts personalised language linking recommendations to Alan's individual circumstances: consolidation to simplify pension arrangements, retirement in approximately 2 years, target household income of £1,700 net per month, ATR 5, use of the Aviva Platform as the recommended wrapper, and specific treatment of each existing arrangement (switch Aviva, switch Zurich, switch Standard Life, retain People's Pension, and note Scottish Widows as already transferred). The report is presented as client-specific overall.
    File:     The fact find [P5] records Alan's objective as retirement planning: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' The policy research file [P14] records the summary of advice as consolidation where necessary with cashflow planning around retirement, and the alternative strategies note as 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things.' The policy research file [P15] records the recommendation for each arrangement (switch Aviva, switch Zurich, retain People's Pension, switch Standard Life) but the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank for every arrangement. The 'Why/why isn't this scheme suitable?' fields contain only brief labels: Aviva — 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; Zurich — 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; People's Pension — 'Active workplace pension'; Standard Life — blank (truncated). No independent evidence corroborates that the rationale entries marked isClientSpecific=false in the report contain genuinely personalised language; the policy research file shows the per-arrangement justification fields are unpopulated for the objective linkage. An internal inconsistency in the report means that multiple fund values are stated inconsistently (approximately £110,000 on page 3, approximately £106,000 on page 4, sum of individual values £116,998.47, total transfer value £110,185 on page 9), which undermines confidence in how precisely the report has been tailored to the client's actual position. Kim's State Pension age is stated as 66 on page 3 but 67 in cashflow assumptions on page 14 — a further internal inconsistency. No vulnerability assessment is present in the file [extraction confirms /vulnerability/C1/assessment NotPresentInDocument].
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model records isClientSpecific=false for 10 out of 31 rationale entries, meaning the report itself concedes that a material number of its rationale passages are not client-specific.
      - The policy research file's per-arrangement 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank for every arrangement (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Standard Life), meaning there is no file-side evidence connecting any individual recommendation to a named client objective.
      - The policy research file's 'Why/Why isn't this scheme suitable?' field contains only brief labels (e.g. 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing') rather than reasons linked to Alan's specific circumstances, and is blank for Standard Life.
      - The report states combined pension value as approximately £110,000 (page 3) but also approximately £106,000 (page 4); the sum of individual plan values is £116,998.47 and the transfer total on page 9 is £110,185 — four different figures, none consistent.
      - Kim's State Pension age is stated as age 66 (page 3) but the cashflow uses age 67 (page 14) — an inconsistency in a material planning assumption that undermines the personalisation of the cashflow rationale.
      - No vulnerability assessment is present in the file despite the extraction noting /vulnerability/C1/assessment as NotPresentInDocument, meaning any vulnerability overlay on the suitability of the rationale cannot be confirmed.
      - The ATR questionnaire gave rating 4 but the report used rating 5, with ambiguity as to whether a formal override process was applied — the personalisation of risk-based rationale therefore rests on an unsupported rating uplift.
    The report asserts client-specific personalisation across most of its rationale entries, and the fact find [P5] does confirm the core client circumstances that ought to anchor that personalisation (consolidation objective, 2-year retirement horizon, £1,700/month income target, ATR 5). To that extent the high-level framing of the advice is grounded in the file. However, the requirement at G6.3 is that the personalised language is actually present and linked at the level of individual recommendations, and this is where the evidence fails to support the assertion. The policy research file [P15] — the adviser's own working document — leaves the objective-linkage field blank for every arrangement and provides only cursory labels in the suitability fields. This is not corroborating evidence of personalised rationale; it is evidence that the rationale was not elaborated at the per-recommendation level even in the adviser's own notes. The canonical model itself acknowledges 10 rationale entries as isClientSpecific=false, confirming the report does not maintain personalised language throughout. The multiple inconsistent statements of the combined pension fund value (£110,000, £106,000, £116,998.47, £110,185) are not merely arithmetic discrepancies; they signal that the report was not carefully calibrated to the client's actual figures, which is itself a form of weak personalisation. The Kim State Pension age inconsistency (66 vs 67) similarly undermines the cashflow-based retirement rationale. The ATR uplift from 4 to 5 without a documented formal override process means the investment rationale rests on a risk rating that is not fully evidenced. The standard regulatory wording guard applies to boilerplate tax and performance caveats and does not excuse the absence of objective linkage in the per-arrangement rationale. Taken together: the report contains some personalised framing at the case level but fails to sustain personalised, objective-linked rationale at the individual recommendation level, and the file evidence affirmatively shows the per-arrangement justification was not documented. This is a Potential Concern of moderate severity — rationale is present but is partly generic and partly conceded as non-client-specific, and the file does not corroborate per-recommendation objective linkage.
