  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents five options for the consolidated transfer recommendation plus arrangement-specific alternatives: OPT1 (leave untouched), OPT2 (in-situ fund switch), OPT3 (consolidate into existing arrangement), OPT4 (switch into employer's People's Pension — rejected: loss of employer contributions; funds don't meet risk profile, 0.06% more expensive, £4.50 admin charge, no multi-asset funds), OPT5 (new Aviva Platform SIPP — recommended). For People's Pension (EA1): retain (recommended, employer contributions); alternatives of transferring away rejected due to loss of employer contributions. For Scottish Widows (EA2): encash under small pots rules. For Aviva (EA3): switch — lifestyling funds outside risk range, plan more expensive. For Zurich (EA4) and Standard Life (EA5): switch to Aviva Platform. The report also considers: internal fund switch within People's Pension (Global Investments up to 60% shares — rejected as not as strong performance, though less volatile); retain People's Pension and allow pot to grow (noted as possible option but not recommended at this stage). Alternative Solutions section states the recommended plan is more cost-effective than a stakeholder pension, which lacks flexible access and has limited fund ranges. Investment pathways rejected as the client requires a more tailored solution. For the Aviva switch (EA3) the plan is stated as 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution. For Standard Life (EA5) the narrative states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.
    File:     The pension switch report (category G) confirms research was conducted across Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life existing plans. The switch report shows that under mid-growth the overall new solution produces a lower projected maturity value than retaining all existing pensions (e.g. combined existing £127,000 vs new solution overall position tables show decreases). The pension switch report confirms: Aviva only comparison — mid growth existing £4,280, new solution £4,260 (£20 or 0.5% decrease); Zurich only — mid growth existing £125,000, new solution £125,000 (£2,000 or 1.6% decrease); Standard Life only — mid growth existing £3,440, new solution approx lower. The overall comparison table (P5) shows at mid growth existing £127,000, new solution £115,000, effect of switching £12,000 or 9.4% decrease (this is the pre-advice-charge comparison including adviser charge effect). The SIPP research report (P7, P8) shows a shortlist of 4 platforms (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) but records 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out' and 'No additional justification text has been entered' for Aviva Platform selection. The file note (P22) records for Zurich alternative: 'Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used'; for Standard Life: 'Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva'; for Aviva alternative: 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR.' The switch charges table (internal inconsistency flagged): EA5 Standard Life existing charge shown as 0.18% in the charges table (page 11) but 0.52% in existing arrangements (page 5); and derived figures confirm EA5's cost comparison rows are computed against Zurich's fund value (£103,439) not Standard Life's (£3,002), making the EA5 cost comparison unreliable. The volatility/performance chart captions on page 13 swap the letter-to-provider assignments between captions. The suitability report's Alternative Solutions section (P16) and report body (P18) are the sources for stakeholder and investment pathway rejections — these are [I] category passages, so they corroborate nothing from the report side.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. Standard Life (EA5) narrative in the report states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. existing is more expensive). The switch charges table on page 11 shows EA5 impact as +0.26% meaning the NEW solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life — a direct contradiction in direction of cost advantage. The derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows are computed against Zurich's fund value, making the comparison figures for Standard Life unreliable and the report's claim of cost saving for EA5 unsupported.
      - 2. The SIPP research report records 'No additional justification text has been entered' for the Aviva Platform selection and no DNA analysis was carried out. The report asserts the Aviva Platform was selected as the recommended platform but the research file provides no documented comparative justification for choosing Aviva over Elevate, Aegon ARC or Wealthtime.
      - 3. The overall mid-growth projection in the switch report (P5) shows the new solution at £115,000 vs existing £127,000 — a £12,000 / 9.4% decrease. The report does not present or explain this overall reduction in projected value to the client in the alternatives section; it discusses individual plan switches but the combined effect of switching all three is a material reduction not clearly flagged as part of the alternatives rationale.
      - 4. OPT1 and OPT2 and OPT3 in the research optionsConsidered list record empty rejectionReasons arrays — the report lists the options but assigns no individual rejection reasons to OPT1, OPT2 and OPT3 in the structured research section (though OPT4 and the recommendation narrative do contain reasons).
      - 5. The People's Pension alternatives (internal fund switch to Global Investments up to 60% shares) include a rejection reason referencing performance and risk, but the report adds 'this option is not recommended as consolidation into this plan is not recommended' — circular reasoning without a standalone rationale for rejecting the fund switch independent of the consolidation decision.
      - 6. The report states for Aviva (EA3) the existing plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution. The switch report (P2) shows Aviva-only mid-growth existing £4,280 vs new solution £4,260 — the new solution produces a lower projected value, meaning the cost saving does not translate into a better outcome at projection level; this is not explained in the alternatives rationale.
      - 7. Kim's State Pension age is stated as 66 on page 3 but cashflow assumptions use age 67 — this inconsistency in the factual basis underlying the alternatives assessment is not reconciled in the report.
      - 8. Total pension fund value stated inconsistently across the report (£110,000 page 3; £106,000 page 4; sum of individual plans £116,998.47 or £117,998.47 per derived figures) — the factual basis for the alternatives assessment is inconsistent within the report itself.
    The report presents a structured alternatives section with options enumerated and most carrying individual rejection reasons linked to the client's risk profile, employer contributions, fund range limitations and costs. The People's Pension retention and the employer contributions rationale are client-specific and well-evidenced. The Scottish Widows encashment rationale (P18) is detailed and client-specific. The Aviva and Zurich switch rationales reference cost and lifestyling risk alignment. However, several material issues remain after applying the guards.
    
    First and most seriously, the Standard Life (EA5) cost rationale is directly contradicted by the switch report's own charges table: the report claims the existing Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the switch table shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive. The derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows were computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), rendering the cost comparison for EA5 structurally unreliable. This is not a formatting difference or rounding issue — the direction of cost advantage is reversed. The report's rejection rationale for the Standard Life do-nothing alternative rests partly on cost, which is directly contradicted by the evidence.
    
    Second, the platform selection research (P7, P8) records no DNA analysis and no additional justification text for choosing Aviva Platform over the three other shortlisted platforms. The report asserts the Aviva Platform as the recommended wrapper but the research file does not document why it was preferred over Elevate, Aegon ARC or Wealthtime. This is an assertion without comparative research support — the guard notes that 'an alternative rejected in the research file but never mentioned in the report is a report gap'; here the converse applies: the platform alternatives are in the shortlist but the research document records no reasons for selection.
    
    Third, the overall combined projection shows a £12,000 / 9.4% decrease at mid-growth when all three plans are switched (P5). While individual plan comparisons are presented, the client-facing alternatives section does not clearly disclose or explain the aggregate reduction in projected value as part of why switching is still recommended — the report presents only individual plan savings narratives.
    
    Fourth, OPT1, OPT2 and OPT3 in the research optionsConsidered carry empty rejectionReasons arrays. The narrative body of the report does contain some reasons across these (e.g. lifestyling, risk profile) but the structured research section does not attribute individual reasons to these options, which is a partial answer.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: the extraction notes vulnerability assessment (/vulnerability/C1/assessment) was not present in the document, so no recorded vulnerability characteristics require specific consideration here beyond what CHK-010 addresses.
    
    The guard noting that 'a ranked comparison placing the recommendation below the existing arrangement is the finding' applies here: the switch report shows the new solution below the existing pension at every growth rate for the overall comparison (P5), and this is material to whether the alternatives rationale is adequately reasoned. The report does not address this in the alternatives section.
    
    The discrepancy in Kim's State Pension age (66 vs 67) and the inconsistent total fund values (£106,000 / £110,000 / £116,998) affect the factual foundation of the alternatives assessment but are primarily data quality issues rather than alternatives-rationale failures on their own.
    
    Overall: the alternatives section has genuine content and client-specific reasons for most recommendations, but the EA5 cost rationale is directly contradicted by the research, the platform selection lacks documented comparative justification, and the overall projected value reduction from consolidation is not addressed in the alternatives narrative. These are material concerns.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Five existing arrangements are identified: (1) People's Pension – Retain, rationale: active workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions, transferring would forfeit employer contributions; (2) Scottish Widows – Encash under small pots rules, rationale: funds do not meet risk profile, plan 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution, £4.50 annual admin charge, no single multi-asset fund aligning with risk rating 5, limited retirement options (UFPLS only), flexible access not available; (3) Aviva – Switch to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs (0.06% cheaper), lifestyling concern, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging facilitation; (4) Zurich – Switch to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs, lifestyling concern, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging facilitation; (5) Standard Life – Switch to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs (existing plan stated as 0.08% more expensive than recommended solution), lifestyling concern, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging facilitation. Each arrangement has a rationale linked to OBJ3 (and OBJ2 for Scottish Widows). The report also notes adviser will arrange the switches. For Standard Life the report states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.'
    File:     Policy research [P3/P5/P12] confirms five arrangements: Aviva SP50163762 (Stakeholder Pension, value £3,744.36), Zurich P10026-719-001/DL (Personal Pension, value £103,439.24), Standard Life D2301334000 (Personal Pension, value £3,002), People's Pension 6197175/PP (Occupational Pension, current workplace), Scottish Widows ZU4225575 (Occupational Pension). Policy documents [P7] confirm Aviva value £3,744.36; [P6] Standard Life value £2,994.30 (statement date 30 Sep 2025, report uses £3,002). [P2] Scottish Widows confirms no direct annuity options and limited retirement flexibility. [P13] People's Pension confirms Global Investments funds available and investment profile options. [P19] SIPP research confirms agreed risk level 5. [P21] existing arrangements table confirms retirement options: People's Pension FAD/UFPLS; Scottish Widows UFPLS only; Aviva FAD/UFPLS; Zurich UFPLS only; Standard Life FAD only; allows servicing adviser: People's Pension No, Scottish Widows No, Aviva No, Zurich No, Standard Life Yes. The switch charges table (per canonical model internal inconsistency) shows Standard Life EA5 impact as +0.26% more expensive under new solution, contradicting the report narrative that the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution. The derived figures confirm EA5 existing annual charge of 0.18% is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89), not Standard Life's £3,002, making the EA5 charge comparison unreliable. The derived figures also show EA5 impact of 0.26% is computed on Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's.
    Discrepancies:
      - Standard Life (EA5) rationale states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the switch charges table on page 11 shows the EA5 impact as +0.26%, indicating the new solution is more expensive — a direct contradiction in direction of cost benefit.
      - The derived figures confirm the EA5 existing annual charge (£186.19 at 0.18%) and EA5 impact (£268.94 at 0.26%) are both computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439), not Standard Life's own fund value of £3,002, rendering the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically unsound.
      - The report's rationale for switching Standard Life leads with 'Lower Costs' as a primary reason, but the evidence (page 11 table and derived figures) indicates the new solution is actually more expensive for this arrangement, undermining the stated rationale.
      - Scottish Widows rationale includes 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — this figure relates to Aviva (EA3) per the canonical model; the Scottish Widows encashment rationale on page 6 cites the same 0.06% figure that the Aviva switch rationale also uses, creating ambiguity about which plan the cost comparison applies to.
      - The Aviva (EA3) rationale states the plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution; the switch charges table shows EA3 impact as -0.06% (a saving under the new solution), which is consistent in direction with this narrative but the sign convention needs care — this is directionally consistent.
      - Combined pension fund value inconsistencies (£110,000 on page 3, £106,000 on page 4, sum of individual values £116,998.47 or £117,998.47 per canonical model) affect the reliability of the overall financial context underpinning the rationales, though individual arrangement rationales are not solely dependent on this figure.
      - Kim's State Pension age stated as 66 on page 3 but cashflow assumes age 67 — this affects the retirement planning context cited in rationales for OBJ2/OBJ3 but does not directly invalidate individual arrangement outcomes.
    All five existing arrangements have a stated recommended outcome (Retain, Encash, Switch×3) and each has at least one rationale passage linked to a client objective. The rationales for People's Pension (retain for employer contributions), Scottish Widows (encash — risk profile mismatch, limited retirement options, no single suitable fund, small pots tax treatment explained), Aviva (switch — cost, lifestyling, flexibility, adviser management), and Zurich (switch — cost, lifestyling, flexibility, adviser management) are client-specific and corroborated by policy research evidence. The People's Pension retain rationale is supported by [P13] confirming it as an occupational scheme and [P21] confirming current workplace scheme status. Scottish Widows encashment rationale is supported by [P21] confirming UFPLS-only retirement options (no FAD) and no servicing adviser. The Aviva and Zurich switch rationales are supported by [P7] and [P9] confirming plan details and [P21] confirming no FAD for Zurich and no servicing adviser for either.
    
    The material concern is the Standard Life (EA5) switch rationale. The report leads with 'Lower Costs' as the primary justification, asserting 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.' However, the switch charges table on page 11 (per the canonical internal inconsistency note) shows the EA5 impact as +0.26%, meaning the new solution is more expensive for Standard Life — directly contradicting the narrative direction. The derived figures confirm this is not merely a labelling issue: the EA5 charge arithmetic uses Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's £3,002, making the entire cost comparison for EA5 unreliable. The report cannot validly claim a cost saving on Standard Life when the figures are computed on the wrong fund. The 'Lower Costs' rationale for Standard Life is therefore unsupported and contradicted by the table.
    
    The remaining Standard Life rationale points (lifestyling, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging) are present and some are supported: [P21] confirms Standard Life offers FAD only and allows a servicing adviser (Yes), corroborating the adviser management facilitation point. The lifestyling concern is referenced in policy research. However, the primary stated reason — lower costs — is directly contradicted by the table data and arithmetically unreliable per derived figures. This is a genuine finding that survives the guards: it is not a formatting difference or rounding issue, but a contradiction in the direction of cost impact.
    
    On vulnerability: no vulnerability assessment was found in the canonical model (path absent per extraction notes), so no overlay adjustment is required for this check.
    
    The finding is Moderate severity: a rationale is present for Standard Life but the primary justification (cost saving) is contradicted by the report's own table and is arithmetically unsound, constituting a weak and internally inconsistent justification rather than a complete absence of rationale.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   Six recommendations are made: (1) Retain People's Pension (EA1) — client responsible, no transfer; (2) Encash Scottish Widows (EA2) under small pots rules — adviser implements, intended outcome is ~£170 net placed into savings; (3) Switch Aviva (EA3) to new Aviva Platform Personal Pension (NP1) — adviser implements, consolidation rationale; (4) Switch Zurich (EA4) to NP1 — adviser implements, consolidation rationale; (5) Switch Standard Life (EA5) to NP1 — adviser implements, consolidation rationale; (6) Establish NP1 — adviser implements. All six carry adviceStatus 'Recommended' and a stated intended outcome. Implementation timescale is 10–15 working days. The responsible party for EA2 is recorded as 'NotStated' in the canonical model, yet the willAdviserImplement array contains only five entries (no entry for recommendation index 5, the NP1 establishment). The responsibleParty array has six entries but the willAdviserImplement array has only five, creating a structural gap for the sixth recommendation.
    File:     Retrieved passages are predominantly Aviva platform terms and conditions (T&C), a key features document, a risk profile report, a SIPP research report, and policy information documents. None of these passages speak to who will implement the individual switches or encashment, or confirm the adviser's stated role. The SIPP research report (P12) confirms the fund selection (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced) and agreed risk level 5 (Balanced), consistent with the recommendation. No passage corroborates or contradicts implementation responsibility statements. The T&C passages (P2, P7) confirm Flexi-access drawdown and retirement options are available on the Aviva Platform, consistent with the stated intended outcomes.
    Discrepancies:
      - The willAdviserImplement array contains only five entries but there are six recommendations — the sixth entry (NP1 establishment) has no willAdviserImplement value in the canonical model, leaving implementation responsibility for the plan establishment itself structurally unstated.
      - The responsibleParty for EA2 (Scottish Widows encashment) is recorded as 'NotStated' in the canonical model, despite the report asserting the adviser will implement it (willAdviserImplement index 1 = true). There is a conflict between the 'NotStated' responsible party and the 'true' willAdviserImplement flag for this recommendation.
      - No external evidence in the file corroborates who will implement the switches or the encashment — the implementation responsibility rests solely on the report's own assertions.
    The report satisfies the core elements of G6.6 for most recommendations: each change is labelled 'Recommended', a specific intended outcome is stated, and an expected timescale (10–15 working days) is given. However, two structural gaps exist. First, the willAdviserImplement array has only five entries for six recommendations, meaning the NP1 plan establishment (recommendation index 5) lacks an explicit implementation flag — though in context the adviser's role can reasonably be inferred, the report's own data is incomplete. Second, for EA2 (Scottish Widows encashment), the responsibleParty field is 'NotStated' while willAdviserImplement is 'true' — an internal inconsistency within the report itself. The guard noting that provider-executed steps are still stated steps is not triggered here; this is adviser-executed activity. The guard on 'you may wish to consider' wording does not apply as all six are marked 'Recommended'. Because the implementation responsibility for the EA2 encashment is internally contradicted (NotStated vs. true) and the NP1 establishment entry is missing a willAdviserImplement value, there are missing component and process risk concerns, albeit at a low severity level given the overall direction of travel is clear from context. No vulnerability factors in the evidence materially alter this assessment. The claim that the report's assertions are unsupported by external evidence is noted but per the instructions, this requirement is assessed on the report alone.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report identifies six recommended actions: Retain (People's Pension, client responsibility), Encash (Scottish Widows, responsible party not stated), Switch (Aviva, adviser), Switch (Zurich, adviser), Switch (Standard Life, adviser), and New Plan (Aviva Pension Portfolio, adviser). The expected timescale for accessing funds is stated as 10-15 working days. Cancellation rights of 30 days are disclosed. The only next step listed is that the client should complete a nomination of beneficiaries form after the transfer process begins. No per-recommendation timescale is stated in the extracted model.
    File:     The retrieved passages are predominantly Aviva terms and conditions (category H), key features (H), fund factsheet (H), cashflow planning documents (F), Scottish Widows policy info (E), Standard Life policy info (E), and a risk profile report (D). The Aviva T&Cs at P1 note that changes to regular income payments require at least 10 working days notice. No passage in the evidence pack independently corroborates or contradicts the report's stated 10-15 working day timescale for the switch/transfer process, nor the identity of the responsible party for each recommendation. No passage addresses who will arrange the encashment of the Scottish Widows plan.
    Discrepancies:
      - The responsible party for the Scottish Widows encashment (EA2) is recorded as 'NotStated' in the canonical model — the report does not state who will arrange or implement this action, even though encashment is a discrete step material to the overall consolidation.
      - No per-recommendation timescale is stated; the 10-15 working day figure applies to fund access rather than to the transfer/switch process itself, and is not broken down by recommendation.
      - The canonical model records only one next step (nomination of beneficiaries); there is no next step or instruction addressing how or when the Scottish Widows encashment will be arranged or by whom.
    The check is a presence-only assessment. The report clearly states the adviser will arrange the three switches and the new plan, satisfying 'by whom' for those recommendations. The 'retain' recommendation correctly places responsibility with the client. The 10-15 working day timescale is stated in the context of fund access, which the guard acknowledges as a stated timescale; this is adequate for the switch recommendations where the client is temporarily out of the market. However, the encashment of the Scottish Widows plan (EA2) has no responsible party stated. Encashment is a discrete action that requires someone to give an instruction; it is not self-executing, and where the proceeds are presumably to remain in cash or be reinvested, the absence of a stated responsible party is a missing component. The timing guard — 'Timing is material where the client is out of the market or working to a deadline' — does not excuse the absence of 'by whom' for the encashment, because responsibility bears directly on whether the action will actually be taken. The 'by whom' guard states naming the adviser firm satisfies the test; here neither the firm nor the client is named for the encashment. This is a low-severity missing component: the overall implementation picture is largely clear and only one action lacks a responsible party, but that gap is real.

No will review or PoA recommendation is made; the guards confirm that an adviser recommending the client set up a PoA is a different claim from the client already having one, and that a case need not raise estate planning. However, the report positively asserts that Alan has an up-to-date will and that no PoA has been granted. These are factual assertions about the client's estate position that the file should corroborate. The fact find's estate planning section is blank — 'Estate Planning' was not ticked as an advice area and no will or PoA data fields are populated. There is no passage in the evidence pack that records the existence or currency of a will, or the absence of a PoA. The report's assertions about will status and PoA status are therefore unsupported by the file. This is a disclosure/evidence gap rather than a contradiction, but the report states these facts as confirmed when the file records nothing. The vulnerability overlay does not add to this finding: no vulnerability indicators are recorded that would specifically affect estate-planning conclusions.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). The report states the Investment Management charge is 0.22%, but the Aviva illustration [P8] shows a weighted average OCF/TER of 0.23% — a 0.01 percentage point difference that is not explained by the stated calculation-method caveat. The report's total ongoing charge of 0.44% (platform 0.21% + investment management 0.22%) does not add up to 0.44% using the illustration's weighted average of 0.23% (0.21% + 0.23% = 0.44% only if investment management is 0.23%, so the arithmetic is internally consistent at 0.44% only if the 0.22% is used — but that contradicts the illustration's 0.23%). No ongoing adviser charge line appears in the report's charges table or in the illustration's charges section, yet an ongoing service is described in the report. The extraction confirms ongoingAdviceFeeDisclosed = true but no percentage or monetary amount for an ongoing adviser charge appears in the charges table extracted. 22 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report discloses: Platform (Aviva) 0.21% / £231.39 pa; Investment Management 0.22% / £253.43 pa; Total ongoing charge 0.44% / £484.81 pa. Transaction costs are excluded with a stated reason (variable, already reflected in net performance figures). An initial advice fee of 3% / £3,305.55 is disclosed. The report states the personal illustration contains further information on cumulative effect. An aggregatedTotalDisclosed flag is true (0.44% / £484.81). No DFM/discretionary fee is disclosed (flagged false in extraction). No ongoing adviser charge line appears in the charges table. The switch charges table on page 11 compares existing vs recommended recurring charges for each transferred plan. The extraction confirms aggregatedCostIllustration is absent from the document.
    File:     The Aviva illustration [P8] confirms the Aviva platform charge at 0.21% and shows fund OCF/TER charges: CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc 0.29%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 0.18%, weighted average 0.23%. The KIID [P1] shows an ongoing charge of 0.18% for one fund (consistent with HSBC). The SIPP research report [P10] shows total aggregated fund costs of £406.74 (0.37%) including transaction costs of £147.80 (0.13%) and ongoing costs of £258.93 (0.24%). The illustration [P8] weighted average investment charge is 0.23%, not 0.22% as stated in the report. The switch report [P16] inputs an investment charge of 0.18% for the Zurich existing plan; [P23] inputs 0.50% for Aviva existing plan. No ongoing adviser charge appears in any illustration or the suitability report charges table. The Standard Life illustration [P20] shows fund charges of 1.0% with a 0.5% discount = 0.5% net, which is the existing plan charge and not the recommended solution charge. The Key Features document [P6] references illustrations showing all charges but does not itself list them numerically.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the Investment Management charge is 0.22%, but the Aviva illustration [P8] shows a weighted average OCF/TER of 0.23% — a 0.01 percentage point difference that is not explained by the stated calculation-method caveat.
      - The report's total ongoing charge of 0.44% (platform 0.21% + investment management 0.22%) does not add up to 0.44% using the illustration's weighted average of 0.23% (0.21% + 0.23% = 0.44% only if investment management is 0.23%, so the arithmetic is internally consistent at 0.44% only if the 0.22% is used — but that contradicts the illustration's 0.23%).
      - No ongoing adviser charge line appears in the report's charges table or in the illustration's charges section, yet an ongoing service is described in the report. The extraction confirms ongoingAdviceFeeDisclosed = true but no percentage or monetary amount for an ongoing adviser charge appears in the charges table extracted.
      - The aggregated cost illustration path is absent from the document per the extraction (NotPresentInDocument), meaning the report's statement that 'your personal illustration contains further information regarding the cumulative effect the charges will have on your investment returns' cannot be verified against a document in the pack — the illustration [P8] is present but no cumulative cost table or RIY figure is visible in the retrieved passages.
      - The SIPP research report [P10] discloses transaction costs of £147.80 (0.13%) at fund level; the report excludes transaction costs with a stated reason but does not estimate them, and the research report figure is not cross-referenced in the suitability report.
      - The switch charges table internal inconsistencies already flagged in the extraction (Zurich 0.18% on page 5 vs 0.93% on page 11; Standard Life 0.52% on page 5 vs 0.18% on page 11; Standard Life narrative says existing plan 0.08% more expensive but table shows new solution 0.26% more expensive) represent material charge figure contradictions within the report itself.
      - The derived figures note that EA5 existing annual charge and EA5 impact percentage are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's fund value (£3,002), making the Standard Life switch cost comparison arithmetically unreliable.
      - No exit or transfer penalty charges from the existing Zurich plan are disclosed in the report despite the Zurich policy document [P7] showing a 5% bid-offer spread on payments and a ¾% annual management charge — these are materially different from the 0.18% fund charge and 0.93% total charge figures cited for EA4 in the report.
    Several material issues arise. First, the investment management charge: the report states 0.22% but the Aviva illustration [P8] shows a weighted average OCF/TER of 0.23%. This is a direct contradiction between the report and a supporting document. The report's own caveat about calculation method differences does not resolve a discrepancy in the headline percentage rate itself. Second, the ongoing adviser charge: the report describes an ongoing service and the extraction flags ongoingAdviceFeeDisclosed = true, but no ongoing adviser charge percentage or monetary amount appears in the charges table. The charges table shows only platform and investment management lines summing to 0.44%. If the ongoing adviser charge is zero or waived it is not stated; if it exists it is absent from the table — either way the disclosure is incomplete. Third, the switch charges table contains internally contradictory charge figures for Zurich (0.18% vs 0.93%) and Standard Life (0.52% vs 0.18%), and the Standard Life cost direction narrative contradicts the table. The derived figures confirm the EA5 rows use the wrong plan value (Zurich's £103,439 instead of Standard Life's £3,002), making the comparison for that switch arithmetically unsound. Fourth, the aggregated cumulative cost illustration is absent from the document per the extraction, so the report's assertion that the illustration contains cumulative effect information cannot be corroborated within the pack. Fifth, transaction costs: the exclusion is stated with reasons and is therefore a disclosure rather than an omission, but the research report [P10] quantifies them at 0.13% (£147.80) and this figure is not referenced in the suitability report. This is a moderate concern — the guard treats a stated exclusion as a disclosure, but the availability of an estimate in the file and its non-disclosure in the report is a disclosure shortfall. The Zurich policy document [P7] shows a 5% bid-offer spread which is not mentioned anywhere in the report's charge disclosure, representing a potentially material undisclosed cost for that switch. No vulnerability indicator is present in the pack that would affect this cost disclosure assessment. The investment management percentage mismatch and the switch table arithmetic errors are the most serious findings; the absent ongoing adviser charge quantum and missing cumulative illustration corroboration are also material.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report states an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested, equating to £3,305.55 at current transfer values, paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer. An ongoing fee of 1% per annum is stated, equating to circa £1,101.85 for the first year, paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme.     File:     The client agreement document (IMPORT_2.md / TFAS Wealth Client Agreement) confirms a standard initial advice charge of 3% of the value of funds advised on (with a minimum of £1,350), and an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (with a minimum of £100 per annum). The client agreement states fees are set out in the suitability report and confirms the ongoing service. The monetary amount £3,305.55 appears in [P12] (the client agreement document). The monetary amount £1,101.85 appears only in [P5], which is the suitability report itself — an [I] passage. The client agreement does not independently confirm the £1,101.85 monetary figure. The matchesClientAgreement field in the canonical model is null, meaning the report does not expressly confirm alignment.
    Discrepancies:
      - The monetary amount £1,101.85 (first-year ongoing fee) is asserted in the suitability report but does not appear in the client agreement or any independent evidence document — it appears only in [P5], an [I] passage, which cannot corroborate itself.
      - The canonical model field matchesClientAgreement is null for both fee entries, meaning the report does not explicitly state that the fees match the client agreement beyond the generic reference to 'as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement'.
      - The aggregated cost illustration path (/costsAndCharges/aggregatedCostIllustration) is confirmed absent by the extraction, meaning no single all-in annual cost total has been independently verified against the client agreement.
      - The arithmetic check flags that 3305.55 as a bare number is not matched in the evidence (nearest is 2024 in [P8]), though £3,305.55 as a currency figure is matched in [P12]. This is a formatting distinction, not a substantive discrepancy, but noted.
      - The report states the initial fee is 3% 'of the amount invested' while the client agreement in [P13] states 3% 'of the value of the funds that we advise on' — the basis description differs slightly in wording, though both point to the same transfer/investment value concept.
    The percentage rates for both fees (3% initial, 1% ongoing) are confirmed by the client agreement passages [P13] and [P3] respectively, and the 3% rate figure is corroborated in [P12]. The monetary initial fee of £3,305.55 is corroborated in [P12], a category [A] document (client agreement/important information). Arithmetic: 3% of the stated total transfer value of £110,185 yields £3,305.55, which is arithmetically consistent. The ongoing fee of 1% on a post-initial-fee fund of approximately £110,185 minus £3,305.55 = £106,879.45 would give approximately £1,068.79, but the report states £1,101.85 with the basis described as 'current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee'. The precise basis for £1,101.85 is not independently verifiable from the evidence — it appears only in [P5], an [I] passage. No independent document confirms this monetary figure. The guard on monetary amounts moving with transfer value is engaged: the report acknowledges the figure 'may fluctuate depending on transfer values', so a moderate difference in the ongoing amount is explicable. However, the figure is unconfirmed by any external document. The matchesClientAgreement field being null means the report does not make an express confirmatory statement beyond the generic client agreement reference. The overall position is that the percentage rates are corroborated by external evidence, the initial fee monetary amount is corroborated, but the ongoing fee monetary amount rests solely on the report itself. This is a disclosure shortfall / missing evidence concern of low-to-moderate weight: the rates agree, the basis is plausible, but independent corroboration of the first-year ongoing fee quantum is absent. No vulnerability factors in the file are identified as bearing on this specific fee-disclosure comparison. The absence of an aggregated cost illustration (confirmed absent by extraction) is a separate concern addressed in other checks but reinforces that complete independent cost confirmation is not present.

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The critical yield appendix (page 18) shows: Low growth (0.00%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect of switching −£10,400 / −9.6%. Mid growth (2.95%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect of switching −£12,000 / −9.4%. High growth (5.88%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect of switching −£15,000 / −10.0%. The narrative on page 11 states mid-growth rate of 2.94% and critical yield of 4.8%. The recommended plan charges table shows platform 0.21% (£231.39) + investment 0.22% (£253.43) = total 0.44% (£484.81). An aggregated cost illustration is stated as not present in the document. The EA5 (Standard Life) switch charges row states existing annual recurring charge 0.18% (£186.19) and impact +0.26% (£268.94). The report narrative on page 8 states the Standard Life existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.
    File:     [P1] (suitability report itself, category I) reproduces the same critical yield table with identical figures: low RIY existing 0.3% / recommended 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect −£10,400/−9.6%; mid RIY existing 0.3%/recommended 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect −£12,000/−9.4%; high RIY existing 0.3%/recommended 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect −£15,000/−10.0%, mid growth labelled 2.95%. [P10] (provider illustration, category H) shows cumulative charges table at mid growth (5% nominal, 2.9% real): after all charges at age 75 = £115,000, RIY stated as 2.1% (reducing growth from 2.9% to 0.8%). [P11] (suitability report, category I) states mid-growth rate as 2.94% and critical yield 4.8%. [P18] (illustration, category H) shows Aviva platform charge 0.21%; weighted average OCF 0.23% (CT Universal MAP 0.29%, HSBC Global Strategy 0.18%). [P21]/[P22]/[P23] (Defaqto pension switch report, category G) shows consolidated comparison: low growth existing £108,000 / new £97,600 (−£10,400/−9.6%); mid growth existing £127,000 / new £115,000 (−£12,000/−9.4%); high growth existing £150,000 / new £135,000 (−£15,000/−10.0%), with existing RIY 0.3% and new solution RIY varying by growth scenario. The Defaqto report's before-advice-charges comparison shows much lower RIY for the new solution (0.6% at mid growth) versus 2.2% in the suitability report, the difference attributable to inclusion of advice charges. Derived figures flag: EA5 existing annual charge £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value matching Zurich (£103,439), not Standard Life (£3,002); EA5 impact £268.94 at 0.26% similarly implies Zurich's fund value — both rows are computed on the wrong plan. Investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195, not matching any arrangement value or the £110,185 transfer total. Platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies £110,186, consistent with the transfer total. The aggregated cost illustration path is flagged as NotPresentInDocument by the extraction.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. Mid-growth rate label: page 18 appendix states 2.95%; page 11 narrative states 2.94% — 0.01 percentage point discrepancy (internal inconsistency already noted in extraction).
      - 2. Investment management charge arithmetic: £253.43 at 0.22% implies a fund value of £115,195, which does not match the transfer total of £110,185 or any individual arrangement — the percentage and monetary figure are internally inconsistent.
      - 3. EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual recurring charge: £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of £103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's £3,002 — the row appears to have been computed on the wrong plan's fund value.
      - 4. EA5 impact: £268.94 at 0.26% similarly implies Zurich's fund value of £103,439, not Standard Life's £3,002 — again computed on the wrong plan.
      - 5. EA5 narrative (page 8) states the Standard Life existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution; the switch charges table on page 11 shows the impact as +0.26% (i.e. the new solution is more expensive) — the direction of cost advantage is contradicted.
      - 6. Recommended plan investment charge: illustration (P18) shows weighted average OCF of 0.23%; suitability report states investment management charge of 0.22% — minor discrepancy of 0.01 percentage point.
      - 7. Aggregated cumulative cost illustration: flagged as NotPresentInDocument in the extraction; no standalone aggregated all-in cost table is present in the suitability report itself (the illustration in P10 provides this for the recommended plan only but is a provider document, not reproduced in the report).
      - 8. The report's recommended plan RIY of 2.2% (mid) includes adviser charges; the Defaqto before-advice-charges comparison shows 0.6% — while the difference is explainable, the suitability report does not explicitly decompose the RIY into its components to make this transparent to the client.
      - 9. Standard Life existing charge stated as 0.52% on page 5 existing arrangements table versus 0.18% in the switch charges table on page 11 — directly contradictory (noted in extraction as internal inconsistency).
      - 10. Zurich existing charge stated as approximately 0.18% + £2.12/month on page 5 versus 0.93% total in the switch charges table on page 11 — materially inconsistent (noted in extraction).
    The core RIY, critical yield and effect-of-switching figures in the suitability report are internally consistent with [P1] (which is the report itself) and are corroborated by the Defaqto pension switch report ([P21],[P22],[P23]) and the provider illustration ([P10]) in terms of the headline projected values and effect-of-switching amounts. The figures in [P1] are category I and cannot corroborate the report, but the Defaqto report (category G) and the provider illustration (category H) do provide independent corroboration of the low/mid/high projected values and the effect-of-switching amounts.
    
    However, several material charge-level problems remain:
    
    **Investment management charge arithmetic (Discrepancy 2):** £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195, not the transfer total of £110,185. The platform charge (£231.39 at 0.21%) does imply £110,186 — consistent with the transfer total — but the investment charge monetary figure does not reconcile. This is an arithmetically inconsistent presentation of charges in the report.
    
    **EA5 rows computed on wrong plan (Discrepancies 3 & 4):** The derived figures confirm that the EA5 existing annual charge (£186.19 at 0.18%) and the EA5 impact (£268.94 at 0.26%) both imply Zurich's fund value (~£103,439), not Standard Life's £3,002. The switch charges comparison for Standard Life is therefore unreliable — both the monetary figures and the impact are materially misstated. The Standard Life plan's actual ongoing charge at 0.18% on £3,002 would be approximately £5.40/year, not £186.19.
    
    **EA5 narrative contradiction (Discrepancy 5):** The page 8 narrative states the Standard Life existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution. The switch charges table shows +0.26% impact (new solution is more expensive). These are directly contradictory in direction. This is a finding that is not explained by any guard — it is a genuine contradiction between the narrative and the table within the same document, and it concerns the cost comparison for one of the switched plans.
    
    **Existing charge inconsistencies for Zurich and Standard Life (Discrepancies 9 & 10):** The existing arrangements table and the switch charges table give contradictory charge figures for both Zurich (0.18%+fixed vs 0.93%) and Standard Life (0.52% vs 0.18%). Given that the EA5 monetary figures appear to have been computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's, it is plausible that the Standard Life row in the switch charges table is contaminated by Zurich's data. These are material data quality/consistency failures in the cost comparison section.
    
    **Mid-growth rate label (Discrepancy 1):** 2.94% vs 2.95% is a minor rounding difference noted in the extraction. It does not affect any comparison materially.
    
    **Investment OCF discrepancy (Discrepancy 6):** 0.22% vs 0.23% is a minor difference; the illustration's weighted average of 0.23% reflects the actual fund blend, and the guard on rounding differences applies here.
    
    **Aggregated cost illustration (Discrepancy 7):** The extraction flags this path as NotPresentInDocument. The provider illustration (P10) provides a cumulative charges table, but the suitability report does not reproduce an aggregated all-in cost figure in its own body. The check guidance identifies absence of an aggregated all-in cost as a High severity concern. However, the provider illustration is referenced in the report and does contain this information.
    
    **Vulnerability overlay:** No vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this cost-and-charges finding.
    
    The most serious findings are: (a) the EA5 switch charges rows being computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's, rendering the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable; (b) the direct contradiction between the page 8 narrative and the page 11 switch charges table on whether the Standard Life switch represents a cost saving or a cost increase; (c) the investment management charge monetary figure not reconciling with the stated percentage and transfer total; and (d) the contradictory existing charge figures for Zurich and Standard Life across different sections of the report. These are material data inconsistencies and a disclosure shortfall in the cost comparison.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report (page 11, Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment) presents a like-for-like cost comparison table asserting: Aviva (EA3) existing 0.50% / £18.72, impact -0.06% / -£2.25; Zurich (EA4) existing 0.93% / £961.98, impact -0.49% / -£506.85; Standard Life (EA5) existing 0.18% / £186.19, impact +0.26% / +£268.94. Overall the report states 'the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and 'your proposed plan can underperform your current plan by £264 in the first year'. The existing arrangements table on page 5 states: Aviva (EA3) 0.50% fund charge; Zurich (EA4) 0.18% fund charge + £2.12/month admin; Standard Life (EA5) 0.52% fund charge. The narrative on page 8 for Standard Life states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.'
    File:     [P3] (suitability report, category I) reproduces the switch charges table showing Aviva 0.50%/£18.72/-0.06%/-£2.25, Zurich 0.93%/£961.98/-0.49%/-£506.85, Standard Life 0.18%/£186.19/+0.26%/+£268.94, and the £264 underperformance figure — all internal to the report. [P1] (suitability report, category I) shows the existing arrangements table with Zurich charge as '0.18% fund. £2.12 per month admin charge' and Standard Life as '0.52% fund charge'. [P2] (Zurich policy info, category E) shows Zurich's annual management charge as ¾% per year plus a 5% bid-offer spread, not 0.18% or 0.93%. [P8] (Standard Life illustration, category E) shows Standard Life fund charges of 1.0% less a 0.5% discount = net 0.5%, not 0.52% or 0.18%. [P6] (pension switch report, category G) shows an investment charge of 0.18% used in the switch report calculation. The derived figures confirm: EA5 existing charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's value not Standard Life's (£3,002); EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% also implies ~£103,439, again Zurich's value. EA4 0.93%/£961.98 implies ~£103,439, consistent with Zurich. Standard Life's own illustration shows a net fund charge of 0.5%, inconsistent with both 0.52% (page 5) and 0.18% (page 11 table).
    Discrepancies:
      - EA4 (Zurich) existing charge: page 5 states 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin (~0.18% plus fixed element); page 11 switch table states 0.93% total. These cannot both be correct as a like-for-like total and are materially inconsistent.
      - EA5 (Standard Life) existing charge: page 5 states 0.52% fund charge; page 11 switch table states 0.18%. These are directly contradictory in the same report.
      - EA5 (Standard Life) narrative on page 8 states existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than recommended solution; the switch table on page 11 shows impact of +0.26% (new solution is more expensive) — the direction of the cost comparison is contradicted between narrative and table.
      - Derived figures show EA5 monetary charges (£186.19 at 0.18% and £268.94 at 0.26%) each imply a fund value of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's fund value (EA4), not Standard Life's fund value of £3,002. The EA5 rows appear to have been computed on Zurich's fund value, making the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetic unreliable.
      - Standard Life policy illustration [P8] shows net fund charge of 0.5% (1.0% less 0.5% discount), which matches neither 0.52% (page 5) nor 0.18% (page 11), though 0.5% is close to 0.52% and could reflect rounding or a slightly different charge basis.
      - Zurich policy documentation [P2] states annual management charge of 0.75% per year plus 5% bid-offer spread; neither figure matches the 0.18% shown on page 5 or the 0.93% shown on page 11 of the report, though 0.93% may be a composite including the spread amortised — no reconciliation is provided.
      - Overall cost saving direction: the report claims the recommended solution is 0.24% cheaper overall, but EA5 rows show the new solution is 0.26% more expensive for that arrangement; the net saving claim is not arithmetically supported given the EA5 data appears to have been computed on the wrong fund value.
      - Mid-growth rate: page 11 narrative states 2.94%; page 18 critical yield appendix labels mid growth as 2.95% — a 0.01 percentage point internal discrepancy.
    Multiple material internal inconsistencies undermine the reliability of the cost comparison table and the report's overall cost-saving conclusion. The most serious issue is that the Standard Life (EA5) monetary figures — both the existing charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and the impact figure of £268.94 at 0.26% — each imply a fund value of approximately £103,439, which is Zurich's fund value (EA4), not Standard Life's fund value of £3,002. This means the EA5 rows have been calculated on the wrong plan's value. As a result, the stated 0.18% existing charge for Standard Life and the +0.26% / +£268.94 impact figure are arithmetically unreliable, and any aggregate cost comparison built on them is compromised. This is confirmed by the derived figures provided. The guard on 'recompute' is precisely triggered here: the monetary charge is not the stated percentage of the stated plan's value, indicating transposed or erroneous rows. The EA4/EA5 cross-contamination of Zurich's charge figure (0.18% from page 5 EA4 row appearing as EA5's existing charge in page 11) also explains why EA4 shows 0.93% on page 11 while page 5 shows 0.18% for that arrangement — the values appear to have been swapped between EA4 and EA5 in the switch charges table. The Standard Life narrative on page 8 saying the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive directly contradicts the switch table showing the new solution is 0.26% more expensive — not only do the figures differ but the direction of cost advantage is reversed. This is not a rounding difference; it is a qualitative contradiction. Provider documentation partially corroborates concerns: Zurich's policy info [P2] shows 0.75% AMC plus bid-offer spread, not reconciling cleanly with either 0.18% or 0.93% without explanation; Standard Life's illustration [P8] shows 0.5% net, close to but not matching either figure stated in the report. The guard on 'comparisons on different bases' does not resolve the EA4/EA5 swap because the inconsistency is directional and arithmetic, not a matter of adviser fees being included or excluded on one side. The 0.01% mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.94% vs 2.95%) is a minor rounding issue and on its own would be Low severity, but it adds to a pattern of arithmetic unreliability. The overall claim that the recommended solution is 0.24% cheaper is unsupported given the EA5 data is computed on the wrong fund, making the aggregate figure unreliable. No vulnerability factors in the file are identified that would separately affect this cost-comparison check beyond the general need for clarity.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts five generic benefits (lower costs, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, enhanced retirement flexibility) for the Aviva switch. It states that including adviser fees the new plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring outperformance of £4,672 in year one. It claims the critical yield of 4.8% p.a. to age 75 is achievable based on past performance of recommended funds. The 'lower costs' benefit is stated specifically for the Aviva switch (page 7) and a switch charges table on page 11 is referenced. The Standard Life switch narrative states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution. The Aviva switch narrative states the existing plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution.
    File:     The pension switch report [P14] shows: at mid growth rate (2.95%) projected value at age 75 is £127,000 (existing) vs £125,000 (new solution), a decrease of £2,000 or 1.9%; at high growth rate (5.88%) £150,000 (existing) vs £147,000 (new solution), a decrease of £3,000 or 2.0%. The critical yield at mid-growth is 3.2% per [P14], not 4.8% as stated in the report narrative. The switch report [P14] labels the mid growth rate as 2.95%; the suitability report narrative states 2.94%. The derived figures show: EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value matching Zurich (£103,439), not Standard Life (£3,002), meaning the EA5 row in the switch charges table was computed on the wrong plan. The derived figures also show the 4.24% required outperformance figure (£4,672 / ~£110,188) implies a fund value of ~£110,189, which matches neither the total transfer value (£110,185) nor the sum of all five plans. The internal inconsistencies note that the Standard Life switch charges table shows the new solution is 0.26% MORE expensive than the existing Standard Life plan, directly contradicting the report narrative on page 8 which says the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive. The research report [P3] and fund factsheets [P5] provide performance data for the recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced C and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced C), but no independent document corroborates that past performance supports 4.8% p.a. growth being achievable. The aggregated cost illustration path is flagged as NotPresentInDocument by the extraction metadata.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the critical yield is 4.8% p.a.; the pension switch report [P14] shows a critical yield of 3.2% at the mid growth rate — a material difference in direction and magnitude.
      - The report narrative states the mid growth rate is 2.94%; the pension switch report [P14] labels it as 2.95%.
      - The report claims the 'lower costs' benefit applies to the Aviva switch (page 7) and that the existing Aviva plan is 0.06% more expensive; however, the overall cost comparison including adviser fees shows the new plan is 4.24% more expensive and requires £4,672 outperformance — these two statements are in direct tension and the 'lower costs' benefit is unsupported for that switch.
      - The report states the Standard Life existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution (page 8 narrative); the switch charges table on page 11 shows the new solution is 0.26% MORE expensive than Standard Life — a direct directional contradiction.
      - The derived figures show the EA5 existing annual charge of 0.18% / £186.19 is computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439), not Standard Life's own value (£3,002), meaning the EA5 comparison row is based on the wrong plan value.
      - The derived figures show EA4 existing annual charge of 0.93% / £961.98 is computed on Zurich's current value (~£103,439), consistent, but the existing arrangements table states Zurich's charge as 0.18% plus £2.12/month admin — materially inconsistent with the 0.93% used in the switch charges table.
      - The aggregated cost illustration is absent (flagged NotPresentInDocument by extraction metadata); no all-in cumulative cost figure is presented to the client.
      - The benefits listed on page 7 (including 'Lower Costs') are generic and not client-specific, with isClientSpecific=false for four of the six benefit statements.
      - The achievability claim ('Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable') is asserted without an independent document directly linking recommended fund past performance to 4.8% p.a. growth being sufficient — and the critical yield figure itself differs from the switch report.
    Multiple material discrepancies exist between the report's benefit-versus-cost assertions and the supporting evidence. The most serious is the critical yield figure: the report states 4.8% p.a. is required and claims it is achievable, but the pension switch report [P14] shows the critical yield at the mid growth rate is 3.2% — a difference of 160 basis points. This is not a rounding difference; it is a materially different figure that changes the assessment of achievability. The achievability claim in the report cannot be accepted without supporting performance analysis, and the guard explicitly requires supporting performance data before accepting such a claim. The recommended funds' past cumulative performance data is present in [P3] and [P5] but the report does not link these data to the 4.8% threshold — and the threshold itself is contradicted by the switch report. Second, the directional contradiction on Standard Life is unambiguous: the narrative says the existing plan is more expensive but the switch charges table says the new solution is 0.26% more expensive. This is not explained by any guard and is a direct contradiction that undermines the 'lower costs' benefit statement. Third, the EA5 switch charges row is computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002, making the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically unreliable. Fourth, the existing Zurich charge in the existing arrangements table (0.18% + £2.12/month admin) is materially inconsistent with the 0.93% used in the switch charges table, creating uncertainty about which charge basis underpins the comparison. Fifth, the 'Lower Costs' benefit is stated generically without client-specific grounding, and it directly contradicts the 4.24% higher cost figure disclosed elsewhere in the same report. The absence of an aggregated all-in cost illustration (flagged NotPresentInDocument) means the cumulative impact of all charge layers is never shown to the client. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to add a further dimension here beyond these findings, as no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would change the cost/benefit analysis. In aggregate, there are multiple cost and benefit statement contradictions, an unreliable switch charges table, a disputed critical yield, and no aggregated cost illustration — these together constitute material concerns about whether the benefit-versus-cost disclosure fairly represents the position to the client.

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations unverified
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended Aviva solution is 0.24% less expensive than the current plans overall (quote: 'the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans'). A required outperformance figure of 4.24% is stated on the basis that the new arrangement is 'more expensive than current plans including adviser fees'. A pension switch charges table on page 11 is provided. Per-plan comparisons are given: Aviva (EA3) is stated as 0.06% more expensive than recommended; Standard Life (EA5) is stated as 0.08% more expensive than recommended (existing plan more expensive). The aggregated cost illustration path (/costsAndCharges/aggregatedCostIllustration) is marked NotPresentInDocument by the extraction.
    File:     This requirement is assessed from the report alone — no supporting documents were retrieved. The derived figures computed from the report's own extracted values reveal the following: (1) The EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of £103,438.89, which is Zurich's current value (£103,439.24), not Standard Life's current value of £3,002.00 — the EA5 row appears to have been computed on the wrong plan. (2) The EA5 impact figure of £268.94 at 0.26% similarly implies a fund value of £103,438.46, again matching Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002.00. (3) The internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction confirms that the page 11 table shows EA5 impact as +0.26% (new solution more expensive), contradicting the page 8 narrative that says the existing Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than recommended. (4) Similarly, the Zurich (EA4) existing annual charge of £961.98 at 0.93% is internally inconsistent with the page 5 table showing 0.18% fund charge plus £2.12/month admin. (5) The -0.24% overall difference and 4.24% required outperformance appear simultaneously — these are contradictory in direction (one says cheaper, the other says more expensive including adviser fees). (6) No aggregated cost illustration is present in the document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report simultaneously states the new plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans AND states a required outperformance of 4.24% on the basis that the arrangement is 'more expensive than current plans including adviser fees' — these are contradictory in direction without clear reconciliation.
      - The EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% is arithmetically inconsistent with Standard Life's own current value of £3,002.00; the implied fund value of ~£103,439 matches Zurich (EA4), indicating the EA5 row was computed on the wrong plan's fund value.
      - The EA5 impact figure of £268.94 at 0.26% similarly implies a fund value matching Zurich (~£103,439), not Standard Life (£3,002), confirming the EA5 cost comparison row is unreliable.
      - The page 8 narrative states the Standard Life existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the page 11 switch charges table shows the impact as +0.26% — the direction is the same (new solution more expensive) only if the narrative is also read as the existing plan being costlier, but the table value of 0.26% directly contradicts the narrative figure of 0.08%.
      - The Zurich (EA4) page 5 charge of 0.18% fund charge plus £2.12/month admin is materially inconsistent with the 0.93% total recurring charge stated on page 11 — these cannot both be correct as a like-for-like total.
      - No aggregated all-in cost illustration is present in the document (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument).
      - The NP1 total ongoing of £484.81 at 0.44% implies a fund value of £110,184, while the required outperformance of £4,672 at 4.24% implies a fund value of £110,189 — neither matches the stated transfer total nor the sum of individual plan values (£116,998.47 or £110,185.60).
    The report contains several material arithmetic and directional inconsistencies in its costs and charges section that undermine the reliability of the cost comparison. The most serious is the EA5 (Standard Life) row: the derived figures confirm that both the existing annual charge (£186.19 / 0.18%) and the impact figure (£268.94 / 0.26%) are computed on a fund value of approximately £103,439 — Zurich's value — rather than Standard Life's £3,002. This means the EA5 cost comparison is materially wrong and any conclusion drawn from it (including the narrative that Standard Life is 0.08% more expensive) is unreliable. The contradiction between the 0.08% narrative and the 0.26% table figure is a further discrepancy in the same row. The simultaneous assertion that the plan is 0.24% cheaper overall and that outperformance of 4.24% is required because the arrangement is more expensive (including adviser fees) is not clearly reconciled in the report — while in principle these could refer to different bases (charges only vs charges plus adviser fees), the report does not explain this clearly, and the required outperformance percentage of 4.24% implying a fund value of ~£110,189 does not align with any stated plan total. The Zurich charge inconsistency (0.18% + £2.12/month on page 5 vs 0.93% on page 11) is a separate material discrepancy that makes the EA4 existing charge comparison unreliable. The absence of an aggregated cost illustration means the cumulative impact of all charges is never shown. The guard notes that higher cost is not automatically poor value — however, the fundamental issue here is not that costs are high but that the cost comparison figures themselves are arithmetically wrong and internally contradictory, making it impossible to assess whether the recommendation represents value. No vulnerability factors appear in the pack that would materially alter this finding. The outcome is Potential Concern.

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model holds no value at the comparativeCost path. The suitability report (passages [P7], [P10], [P11]) asserts for each switch: Aviva (EA3) — 'Lower Costs — The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure… The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution'; Standard Life (EA5) — 'Lower Costs… The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'; Zurich (EA4) — does not lead with lower costs but cites investment choice, fund blend issues, retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation and ongoing monitoring. The switch charges table ([P8]) shows Aviva existing 0.50%/£18.72 vs new solution impact -0.06% (saving); Standard Life existing 0.18%/£186.19 vs new solution impact +0.26% (more expensive under new); Zurich existing 0.93%/£961.98 vs new solution impact -0.49% (saving). The new solution total ongoing charge is disclosed as 0.44%/£484.81 (platform 0.21% + investment management 0.22%). Transaction costs are excluded but explained as already reflected in net performance figures.
    File:     [P8] (suitability report internal, category I) shows the pension switch charges table: Aviva existing 0.50%, Standard Life existing 0.18%, Zurich existing 0.93%; new solution impact shown as -0.06%, +0.26%, -0.49% respectively. The Defaqto pension switch report [P2] and [P5] show projected maturity value comparisons. For Zurich only ([P2]): existing pension mid-growth projected value £119,000, new solution £117,000, effect of switching -£2,000 or -1.7% decrease at mid-growth. For Standard Life only ([P5]): new solution £117,000 total vs retained scenario £124,000 total (though these include retained plans). [P18] (file notes, category C) states Standard Life alternatives were 'Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' and Zurich discounted because no risk-level-5 multi-asset funds available. The derived figures confirm: EA5 existing annual charge £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value matching Zurich (£103,438.89), not Standard Life (£3,002.00) — so the Standard Life row in the switch charges table is computed on the wrong plan value. The EA5 impact figure of +0.26%/£268.94 is similarly computed on Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's £3,002.00.
    Discrepancies:
      - Standard Life (EA5) narrative ([P10]) states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', but the switch charges table ([P8]) shows the new solution impact as +0.26% — i.e. the new solution is more expensive than Standard Life, directly contradicting the narrative direction.
      - EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of approximately £103,439, which is Zurich's (EA4) fund value, not Standard Life's £3,002 — the Standard Life row in the comparison table is computed on the wrong plan value, making the cost comparison for Standard Life arithmetically unreliable.
      - EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% similarly implies a fund value of approximately £103,438, again Zurich's value rather than Standard Life's £3,002 — the monetary impact figure for Standard Life is therefore computed on the wrong base.
      - The Zurich switch ([P2]) shows new solution projected maturity value of £117,000 vs existing £119,000 at mid-growth (effect of switching: -£2,000 or -1.7% decrease), yet the Zurich switch is recommended primarily on non-cost grounds without explicitly acknowledging the projected value reduction in the main suitability report body.
      - The aggregated cost illustration path is noted as absent (NotPresentInDocument) from the canonical model, meaning no single all-in cumulative cost figure spanning all three switched plans together is disclosed in the report.
      - Zurich existing charge is stated as 0.18% + £2.12/month admin on page 5 of the suitability report, but the switch charges table uses 0.93% as the current annual recurring charge — these are materially inconsistent figures for the same arrangement.
    Three discrepancies are material findings after applying the guard that cheaper options rejected for non-cost reasons are valid. First, the Standard Life narrative directly contradicts the switch charges table in direction: [P10] says the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the new solution, but [P8] shows the new solution impact as +0.26% — i.e. the new solution costs more. This is a Data inconsistency and also a case where the report claims a cost saving that its own comparison table contradicts (High severity per the decision logic). Second, the derived figures confirm the EA5 rows in the charges table are computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), rendering the monetary and percentage comparison for Standard Life arithmetically wrong on the wrong base — this is a Data quality / Data inconsistency issue and means the cost comparison for the Standard Life switch cannot be relied upon. Third, the Zurich switch shows a projected value reduction of £2,000 at mid-growth in the pension switch report [P2], yet the main suitability report does not explicitly acknowledge this projected shortfall; the non-cost benefits cited (fund range, flexibility, adviser charging) are plausible under the guard, but the absence of an explicit acknowledgment that the switch is expected to produce a lower projected outcome at mid-growth is a disclosure gap. The Zurich charge inconsistency (0.18%+admin vs 0.93% in the table) is a pre-existing internal inconsistency flagged in the canonical model's internalInconsistencies and reinforces the unreliability of the cost comparison. The absence of an aggregated all-in cumulative cost illustration is confirmed by the extraction metadata (NotPresentInDocument). The vulnerability overlay: the client agreement [P20] notes reasonable adjustments are available on request; no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the pack that would materially alter this cost/charges assessment beyond what is already identified. On balance, the Standard Life cost direction contradiction and the wrong-base arithmetic for EA5 are clear findings that cannot be explained away. The outcome is Potential Concern.
