  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report's Critical Yield appendix (page 18) and narrative (page 11) assert the following figures for the combined switch (three pensions to Aviva Platform): Low growth (0.00%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect of switching £10,400 / 9.6% decrease. Mid growth (2.95%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect of switching £12,000 / 9.4% decrease. High growth (5.88%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect of switching £15,000 / 10.0% decrease. Page 11 narrative states the mid-growth rate used is 2.94%. Page 18 appendix table labels it 2.95%. The report also asserts that the recommended plan must outperform the current plan by £4,672 in the first year (4.24% of stated fund value).
    File:     Passage [P2] (pension switch report, all-three-pensions section) contains a table that exactly matches the report's appendix figures: Low RIY 0.3%/2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect £10,400/9.6%; Mid RIY 0.3%/2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect £12,000/9.4%; High RIY 0.3%/2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect £15,000/10.0%. The same passage labels the mid growth rate as 2.95%, consistent with the appendix but inconsistent with the page 11 narrative figure of 2.94%. Passage [P13] (Aviva illustration) shows 'All charges could reduce this pension fund from £129,000.00 to £115,000.00 — this is a reduction in the growth rate of 2.1%', which corresponds to the recommended plan RIY at mid growth. The pension switch research report [P2] also confirms that the initial transfer involves an existing pension value of £110,185.60 and a day-one new solution value of £106,880.60, consistent with the £10,400 low-growth effect and £3,305 immediate shortfall. Passage [P1] shows a separate, lower-cost illustration for some pensions (RIY 0.6%, critical yield 0.3% at low, 3.2% at mid) which relates to a different combination (not all three pensions) and does not contradict the main combined figures.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's page 11 narrative states the mid-growth rate is 2.94%, but the report's own page 18 appendix table labels it 2.95%, and the pension switch report [P2] also uses 2.95%. This is a 0.01 percentage point internal inconsistency within the suitability report itself.
      - The derived figures note that EA5 (Standard Life, fund value £3,002) existing annual charge of 0.18% (£186.19) implies a fund value of ~£103,439 — Zurich's value — not Standard Life's value, indicating the EA5 charge row in the switch charges table was computed on the wrong plan's fund value, making any cost comparison for Standard Life arithmetically unreliable.
      - The aggregated cost illustration path (/costsAndCharges/aggregatedCostIllustration) is flagged as NotPresentInDocument by the extraction, meaning no single all-in aggregated cost illustration for all charges combined appears to have been provided in the report.
      - The derived figures show that the charge arithmetic for the platform (0.21% implying £110,185 fund) and investment management (0.22% implying £115,195) base values do not match any single stated arrangement value or the combined transfer value of £110,185.60 exactly in a reconcilable way, though the platform figure is very close to the total transfer value.
      - No corroborating passage from an independent source (category E or H) directly confirms the combined-three-pensions critical yield table figures; [P2] is category G (research/switch report, third-party tool output) which is supporting but the minimum corroborating category requirement calls for at least one corroborating passage — [P2] satisfies this as it is not category I.
    The core RIY, critical yield and effect-of-switching figures in the suitability report's appendix (page 18) are directly corroborated by passage [P2] from the pension switch report. Every figure — RIY 0.3%/2.1%/2.2%/2.3%, critical yields 1.9%/4.8%/7.8%, effects £10,400/£12,000/£15,000 and percentage decreases 9.6%/9.4%/10.0% — matches [P2] exactly. The Aviva illustration [P13] independently confirms a reduction in growth rate of 2.1% for all charges at the mid rate, consistent with the 2.2% RIY quoted in the report (the slight difference is attributable to the illustration using slightly different growth assumptions as noted in the guard). The minimum corroborating category requirement is satisfied by [P2] (category G). There are two residual concerns. First, the mid-growth rate label discrepancy (2.94% on page 11 narrative vs 2.95% in the appendix and in [P2]) is a minor internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction's internalInconsistencies; it does not affect the yield figures themselves but is a data quality issue. Second, and more materially, the derived figures establish that the EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual charge row in the switch charges table was computed on a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value) rather than Standard Life's actual value of £3,002. This means the 0.18% / £186.19 figure for EA5 and the 0.26% / £268.94 impact figure are computed on the wrong fund, making the per-plan cost comparison for Standard Life arithmetically meaningless and the cumulative impact figure unreliable. This is not merely a rounding issue but a structural error in the comparison arithmetic. Third, the aggregated cost illustration is confirmed absent by the extraction — no single document in the pack shows a combined all-in cost illustration. The required outperformance figure of £4,672 at 4.24% implies a fund value of ~£110,189, broadly consistent with the £110,185.60 transfer value, so this is not a significant discrepancy. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the pack that would change this assessment. The EA5 charge computation error and the absence of an aggregated cost illustration are the material findings; the 2.94%/2.95% discrepancy is minor. Taken together, the EA5 arithmetic error undermines confidence in the cost comparison and constitutes a data inconsistency that is a Potential Concern.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report (page 17) states an ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum, estimated at circa £1,101.85 for the first year based on current values, paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme. Five services are listed: (1) Reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss; (2) Reviewing ongoing suitability of the investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time for ad-hoc advice; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning. No review frequency is specified beyond what can be inferred from the service list. The report does not state a minimum annual review meeting nor mention statements, performance reports, cashflow analysis, family benefit, quarterly e-magazine or online access to portfolio valuations.
    File:     The client agreement [P1] confirms an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), automatically taken from funds invested, consistent with the report's stated rate. The 'Important Information' document [P8] describes the ongoing service as including: a review meeting at least once a year (with recommendations potentially covering bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk); statements and performance reports twice per year; cashflow analysis when relevant; family benefit (adviser fee-free advice for direct family members for ISA or pension set up, plus up to three hours advice for children); regular market updates; quarterly e-magazine; online access for portfolio valuations; and access to the adviser as needed. The client agreement [P2] confirms fees are set out in the suitability report and that an ongoing advice and review service will be provided until either party considers it no longer appropriate. The research report [P17] notes the recommended platform charges adviser charging and the charge is calculated against the current balance.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report lists five service components but omits several items described in the firm's own service proposition document [P8]: (a) a review meeting at least once a year, (b) statements and performance reports twice per year, (c) cashflow analysis when relevant, (d) family benefit for direct family members, (e) quarterly e-magazine, (f) online access for portfolio valuations.
      - The suitability report does not specify review frequency (extracted as 'Unspecified'), whereas [P8] states reviews occur at least annually.
      - The report's service list is presented generically and is not tailored or client-specific (isClientSpecific: false for all five items), whereas [P8] describes a more comprehensive and structured service proposition — the report discloses a subset of what the client is paying for.
      - The aggregated cost illustration path (/costsAndCharges/aggregatedCostIllustration) is absent from the report, confirmed by the extraction as NotPresentInDocument — no all-in total cost figure combining adviser, platform, investment and product charges is presented.
      - The ongoing service charge rate of 1% is consistent between the report and the client agreement, so no discrepancy on the rate itself.
    The ongoing adviser charge rate of 1% p.a. is confirmed by both the suitability report and the client agreement [P1] and [P8], so there is no discrepancy on the cost itself. However, the service proposition disclosed in the report is materially incomplete relative to what the firm's own published service description in [P8] sets out. The report lists five broadly-worded services but omits a structured annual review meeting, bi-annual statements and performance reports, cashflow analysis, family benefit, quarterly e-magazine and online portfolio access — all of which form part of what the client is paying 1% p.a. for. This is a disclosure shortfall: the client is not told the full scope of what they receive, which undermines their ability to assess value for money. The absence of a specified review frequency compounds this — 'Unspecified' in the report while [P8] guarantees at least annual reviews. Neither guard (declined service, different label for same service) explains these omissions: the firm's own document lists them as standard inclusions, not optional extras. The missing aggregated cost illustration means the client cannot see the cumulative impact of adviser, platform, investment and product charges combined, which is a separate disclosure gap. The vulnerability overlay does not materially alter this finding — no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded that would change the analysis here, though the absence of clear, complete service disclosure is relevant to any client's ability to make an informed assessment.
      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P15)
        "The costs for this service are 1% per annum. Based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee, this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year. This will vary in line with your fund value. This fee will be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme as you prefer to pay via your provider rather than directly."

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states five generic benefits to justify switching: (1) Lower Costs — the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure; (2) Greater Investment Choice — access to a wider range of options; (3) Adviser Charging Facilitation — charges deducted directly; (4) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments — ability to actively monitor and adjust; (5) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility — more flexible retirement options. It also states (for the Aviva switch covering EA3/EA4/EA5) that 'the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' requiring outperformance of £4,672 in year one, and for the critical yield assessment that 'based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable.' Separately, for the Standard Life switch, page 8 states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the switch charges table on page 11 shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life — a direct internal contradiction. The aggregated cost illustration is absent from the report (noted as NotPresentInDocument).
    File:     The case file provides: (a) The SIPP research report [P10] shows MiFID II pre-sale costs for the two recommended funds totalling 0.37% (ongoing 0.24% + transaction 0.13%), with no platform or adviser charges included in that table. (b) The policy research [P5/P7] confirms the Aviva Pension Portfolio offers FAD and full/partial UFPLS, whereas Zurich offers only full/partial UFPLS and Standard Life offers only FAD — supporting the retirement flexibility benefit for Zurich transfers but not universally across all ceding plans. (c) The Aviva key features [P4/P8] confirm flexi-access drawdown, phased drawdown and annuity options, corroborating the Enhanced Retirement Flexibility claim for the recommended product. (d) The SIPP research report [P3/P12] confirms Aviva Pension Portfolio Choice was the selected product from a panel of 118, with DNA features analysis performed. (e) Zurich policy info [P9] acknowledges higher charges on the new plan are possible and asks whether the client understands their impact. (f) No independent illustration, provider charges schedule or switching analysis document is present in the file that confirms the 0.44% total ongoing charge for NP1 (implied £484.81 on c.£110,184) or reconciles the stated 4.24% cost premium figure. (g) The derived figures show EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value matching Zurich (£103,439), not Standard Life (£3,002) — meaning the Standard Life row in the switch charges table is computed on the wrong fund value. (h) The 'Lower Costs' benefit is directly contradicted by the report's own acknowledgement that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive (inclusive of adviser fees), and by the internal inconsistency where the Standard Life narrative says existing is more expensive while the charges table shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report lists 'Lower Costs' as a benefit of switching, yet separately acknowledges the Aviva plan (inclusive of adviser fees) is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 first-year outperformance — these two statements are irreconcilable as a generic benefit claim.
      - The Standard Life switch narrative (page 8) states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the switch charges table (page 11) shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than the Standard Life existing plan — a direct contradiction in direction of cost differential.
      - EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's value not Standard Life's (£3,002) — the Standard Life row in the switch charges table is computed on the wrong fund value, making any cost comparison for that switch unreliable.
      - The five generic benefits (Lower Costs, Greater Investment Choice, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility) are stated without client-specific tailoring; none is assessed against what the ceding plans already offer, contrary to the guard requiring that a benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify extra cost.
      - The report's critical yield achievability assertion — 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' — is unsupported by any performance evidence in the file specific to the recommended Aviva portfolio in its recommended fund mix.
      - The aggregated all-in cost illustration (path /costsAndCharges/aggregatedCostIllustration) is absent from the report (confirmed NotPresentInDocument), so the client cannot see the cumulative cost impact of the recommendation.
      - The 'Lower Costs' benefit is stated for the Aviva switch covering EA3 (Aviva existing), yet the report acknowledges overall the plan is more expensive — no net saving is evidenced for any individual switch.
      - Standard Life already offers FAD per [P19], so the 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' benefit does not differentiate the recommended product from Standard Life's existing arrangement.
    The central problem is that the report simultaneously claims 'Lower Costs' as a justification for switching and acknowledges the recommended Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive (inclusive of adviser fees) than the current arrangements. These two positions cannot coexist as a coherent justification. The 'Lower Costs' benefit claim is therefore unsupported and contradicted by the report's own data. The guard that consolidation convenience is a real but non-financial benefit applies to some of the other stated benefits (Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring), but it does not rescue the 'Lower Costs' claim, which is a financial assertion. The Standard Life cost comparison is doubly problematic: the narrative direction contradicts the charges table, and the derived figures confirm the table row was computed using Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's £3,002 — meaning the 0.18% existing charge figure for EA5 is arithmetically impossible against Standard Life's own fund value (£3,002 × 0.18% = ~£5.40, not £186.19). Any cost justification resting on that row is unreliable. The 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' benefit has partial support: Zurich (P19) offers only UFPLS and lacks FAD, so moving to Aviva does genuinely add FAD for that ceding plan. However, Standard Life already offers FAD per [P19], so for the Standard Life switch this benefit does not differentiate. The benefits are stated generically and are not assessed against each individual ceding plan's features, as the guard requires. The critical yield achievability assertion relies on past performance of the recommended funds, but no performance data specific to the recommended Aviva portfolio in its HSBC/CT fund mix is produced in the file — the research report contains fund research but no historical performance evidence calibrated to the specific 4.8% per annum growth requirement cited. The absence of an aggregated cost illustration means the client cannot assess the cumulative cost impact. The 0.01% rounding difference in mid-growth rate (2.94% narrative vs 2.95% appendix) is a minor internal inconsistency noted but not material to this check. The vulnerability overlay: no formal vulnerability assessment is recorded in the file (/vulnerability/C1/assessment NotPresentInDocument), but no specific vulnerability driver is identified that would alter this cost and charges finding. Overall: the 'Lower Costs' benefit claim is directly contradicted by the report's own cost data; the Standard Life cost comparison is computed on the wrong fund value; the generic benefit statements are not assessed against existing plan features; and the critical yield achievability assertion is unsupported by evidence. These are material concerns.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts (page 11) that 'the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' (direction: RecommendedCheaper, differencePercentage: -0.24). It also states that the new solution requires outperformance of £4,672 (4.24%) in year one including adviser fees. The critical yield appendix (page 18) shows: Low (0.00%) — new solution projected value £97,600, RIY 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%; Mid (2.95%) — new solution £115,000, RIY 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%; High (5.88%) — new solution £135,000, RIY 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%. Page 11 narrative states mid-growth rate is 2.94%.
    File:     The pension switching report (Defaqto, [P10]) shows the overall combined switch comparison at the same growth rates: Low (0.00%) — existing £108,000, new solution £97,600, RIY new 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect -£10,400 or -9.6%; Mid (2.95%) — existing £127,000, new solution £115,000, RIY new 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect -£12,000 or -9.4%; High (5.88%) — existing £150,000, new solution £135,000, RIY new 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect -£15,000 or -10.0%. The switching report also shows per-plan comparisons ([P3], [P5], [P6]) with their own projected values and critical yields. The switching report identifies the mid growth rate as 2.95% throughout ([P10]). The switching report nowhere states the new solution is 0.24% cheaper overall; the per-plan Aviva-only comparison ([P3]) shows a reduction in yield of 0.6% for both existing and new solution at mid rate with a critical yield of 3.2% and effect of -£2,000. The charge arithmetic from derived figures flags that the EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002, making the EA5 cost comparison row unreliable. The internal inconsistency noted in extraction confirms Standard Life's charge appears as 0.52% on page 5 but 0.18% in the switch charges table on page 11, and Zurich's charge appears as 0.18%+£2.12/month on page 5 but 0.93% in the switch charges table on page 11.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the new solution is 0.24% less expensive than current plans (RecommendedCheaper), but the switching report's overall combined comparison ([P10]) shows the new solution produces lower projected outcomes at every growth rate: -£10,400 (-9.6%) at low, -£12,000 (-9.4%) at mid, -£15,000 (-10.0%) at high. No table in the switching report corroborates a 0.24% saving on an all-in basis.
      - The report's page 11 narrative states the mid growth rate is 2.94%, but the critical yield appendix on page 18 labels it 2.95%, and the switching report ([P10]) consistently uses 2.95%. The 2.94% figure is unsupported by the evidence.
      - The EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual charge is stated as 0.52% on page 5 of the report but as 0.18% in the switch charges table on page 11 — a direct internal contradiction. The derived figures confirm the 0.18% figure is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89), not Standard Life's £3,002, making the EA5 cost row arithmetically wrong.
      - The EA4 (Zurich) existing annual charge is stated as 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin on page 5 of the report but as 0.93% total in the switch charges table on page 11 — materially inconsistent figures that cannot both be correct as a like-for-like total charge.
      - The report states the required outperformance is £4,672 at 4.24% (required outperformance percentage). The derived figures show 4.24% at £4,672 implies a fund value of £110,188.68, which matches no arrangement value and neither total, raising doubt about the basis of this computation.
      - The switching report's per-plan Standard Life comparison ([P3]) shows the new solution is more expensive (effect: -£2,000 at mid, critical yield 3.2%), while the suitability report page 8 narrative states the existing Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive — a contradiction in direction already flagged in the extraction inconsistencies.
      - The aggregated cost illustration path (/costsAndCharges/aggregatedCostIllustration) is confirmed absent from the report (extraction: NotPresentInDocument). No all-in aggregated cost figure reconciling platform, product, fund and adviser charges is present.
    The central assertion — that the new solution is 0.24% cheaper — is the principal concern. The switching report ([P10]) is the primary independent evidence document and it shows the new solution produces lower projected outcomes at every growth rate, with a combined effect of -9.4% to -10.0%. This is not a lower-growth-rate artefact (the guard does not apply: the same growth rates are used for both existing and new solution in the comparison). The 0.24% saving claim therefore contradicts the switching report's own overall comparison table, which the report presents as the 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment'. The -0.24% figure has no corroboration in the evidence: the figures-checked-in-code section confirms it is not matched in any passage. This is the highest-severity concern: the report claims a cost saving that its own comparison table (and the supporting switching report) contradicts. Additionally, the EA5 Standard Life cost comparison row is computed on the wrong fund value (Zurich's £103,438.89 rather than Standard Life's £3,002), making the per-plan charge comparison for that arrangement arithmetically unreliable. The Zurich charge discrepancy (0.18%+£2.12/month vs 0.93% total) means the existing cost baseline for the largest plan is inconsistent within the report, further undermining the 0.24% saving claim. The mid-growth rate label discrepancy (2.94% in narrative vs 2.95% in appendix and switching report) is minor but adds to the pattern of internal inconsistency. The absence of an aggregated all-in cost illustration means the cumulative impact of all charge layers (platform, product, fund, adviser) is never shown holistically. The critical yield figures themselves (1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%) are corroborated by [P10]. The required outperformance of 4.24% (£4,672) to justify the switch including adviser fees is presented alongside a claim of being cheaper on a charges-only basis — these two statements are in tension and not reconciled in the report. No vulnerability overlay factors that would materially alter this assessment are evident in the pack; the vulnerability assessment path is absent from extraction.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The critical yield appendix (page 18) labels the mid-growth rate as 2.95%, but the report narrative (page 11) states 2.94% — a 0.01 percentage-point internal inconsistency noted in the extraction metadata. The report states the Standard Life existing charge is 0.18% (page 11 switch charges table) but the existing arrangements table (page 5) states Standard Life's charge as 0.52% — these are materially inconsistent figures within the report and cannot both be correct. The report narrative for Standard Life (page 8) states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the switch charges table (page 11) shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life — a direct contradiction in direction of the cost comparison, meaning the disadvantage disclosure for this transfer may be reversed. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report discloses multiple material disadvantages across several sections: (1) higher cost including adviser fee — the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one; (2) critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 at mid-growth rate (2.94%); (3) switching effect at low/mid/high growth — £10,400 (9.6%), £12,000 (9.4%), £15,000 (10.0%) decreases respectively at age 75; (4) Scottish Widows limited flexibility on withdrawal; (5) loss of employer contributions if People's Pension is transferred; (6) Standard Life charge 0.18% existing vs 0.44% recommended (0.26% more expensive); (7) no return guarantee; (8) market timing risk during transactions; (9) 10–15 working day access delay; (10) past performance of recommended Aviva funds below existing Zurich funds; (11) Aviva existing plan 0.06% more expensive than recommended plus £4.50 admin charge; (12) limited fund range in Aviva existing plan; (13) opportunity cost of early encashment of Scottish Widows small pot; (14) Standard Life performance data unavailable; (15) transaction costs excluded from charge quotes; (16) cashflow surplus assumption. All risks not specifically listed are delegated to product literature.
    File:     The Defaqto pension switch report (G) quantifies switching effects for each plan individually. For Aviva-only, the low-growth effect is shown as a £10 or 0.3% decrease; for Zurich the figures are materially larger. The product literature (H — Aviva Key Features and Terms & Conditions) discloses investment risks including value going down as well as up, income drawdown risks (fund depletion, reduced provision for dependants, means-tested benefit impacts), currency risk, property liquidity risk, tax treatment changes, and the risk of being disinvested during switching with potential adverse market movements. The policy research file (G — Test Case 1 Policy Research) shows that flexi-access drawdown (FAD) is available on the recommended Aviva platform. The fact find (B) records the client has a monthly cash-flow deficit of -£288. No vulnerability flags appear in the fact find or supporting documents that would materially alter the risk disclosure requirement. The critical yield appendix labels the mid-growth rate as 2.95%, while the report narrative states 2.94%.
    Discrepancies:
      - The critical yield appendix (page 18) labels the mid-growth rate as 2.95%, but the report narrative (page 11) states 2.94% — a 0.01 percentage-point internal inconsistency noted in the extraction metadata.
      - The report states the Standard Life existing charge is 0.18% (page 11 switch charges table) but the existing arrangements table (page 5) states Standard Life's charge as 0.52% — these are materially inconsistent figures within the report and cannot both be correct.
      - The report narrative for Standard Life (page 8) states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the switch charges table (page 11) shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life — a direct contradiction in direction of the cost comparison, meaning the disadvantage disclosure for this transfer may be reversed.
      - The report states the Aviva existing plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution (page 7), but the switch charges table (page 11) shows EA3 impact as -0.06% (a saving under the new solution) — consistent directionally, but the framing in the recommendation section describes this as a disadvantage of the existing plan rather than of the switch.
      - The product literature (Aviva Key Features) discloses income drawdown-specific risks including fund depletion under high income withdrawal, reduced provision for dependants, and potential impact on means-tested benefits — none of these drawdown risks appear in the report's disclosed disadvantages, even though the recommendation includes a drawdown-capable SIPP.
      - The product literature discloses currency risk and property liquidity risk as investment risks — these are not mentioned in the report, though these may be generic and not specifically applicable to the recommended funds.
      - The Defaqto switch report shows the switching effect for the Aviva-only comparison as £10 / 0.3% decrease (low growth), materially smaller than the aggregate figures in the report (£10,400 / 9.6%), which is expected given the aggregate covers all three transfers — no mismatch on its face, but the report does not break down switching effects per plan, making it difficult to verify the aggregate figures plan-by-plan.
      - The derived figures note that the EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of ~£103,439 — Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002 — meaning the Standard Life charge row in the switch comparison table appears to have been computed on the wrong fund value, making the 0.26% impact figure and £268.94 unreliable.
      - The report's total pension fund value is stated inconsistently across the document: approximately £110,000 (page 3), approximately £106,000 (page 4), and the sum of individual plan values is £116,998.47 — none agree, and the switch charges table appears to use Zurich's value as a proxy for the combined transfer value in at least one row.
      - No loss scenarios or disadvantage disclosures are tailored to the client's monthly cash-flow deficit of -£288, which could be relevant to their capacity to absorb the higher ongoing charges.
      - The general statement delegating all other risks to product literature appears on page 8 with no identification of which specific product literature risks are material to this client's circumstances.
    The report makes a substantial and largely client-specific disclosure of disadvantages and risks across multiple sections. It quantifies switching effects at three growth rates, states the critical yield, identifies cost differentials, warns on limited flexibility (Scottish Widows), employer contribution loss (People's Pension), market timing risk, access delays, past performance shortfall (Zurich), and Standard Life performance data gaps. This is a materially richer disclosure than boilerplate-only. However, several concerns arise. First, the most significant finding is the internal contradiction on the Standard Life cost comparison: the page 8 narrative tells the client the existing Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution (framing the switch as advantageous on cost), while the switch charges table on page 11 shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life — a directly opposite conclusion. The derived figures confirm the Standard Life charge row appears to have been computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's £3,002, making the monetary figures (£186.19 existing, £268.94 impact) arithmetically unreliable for Standard Life. This means the disclosed cost disadvantage for the Standard Life transfer (0.26% more expensive under the recommended solution) may in fact be severely understated in monetary terms, or the narrative misrepresents the direction entirely. A client reading page 8 is told the existing plan costs more; page 11 tells them the new plan costs more — they cannot both be correct, and the extraction metadata confirms this as an internal inconsistency. This is a material disclosure failure: the cost consequence of the Standard Life switch is either wrong in direction or computed on the wrong fund value, and whichever reading is correct, the client has received inaccurate information about a cost disadvantage. Second, the income drawdown risks disclosed in the Aviva Key Features — specifically fund depletion under sustained withdrawals, reduced provision for dependants, and means-tested benefit impacts — are absent from the report's own disadvantages section. The report recommends a drawdown-capable SIPP and the cashflow model shows the clients drawing income in retirement; these risks are therefore material to this client. Delegating them entirely to product literature, without any mention in the report itself, falls short of fair and prominent disclosure. Third, the 2.94% vs 2.95% mid-growth rate discrepancy is minor (0.01pp) and unlikely to mislead materially; it is noted but does not alter the outcome. Fourth, the Standard Life charge inconsistency between page 5 (0.52%) and page 11 (0.18%) is a data quality issue that undermines confidence in the accuracy of the existing plan charge comparison. Fifth, the cashflow deficit of -£288/month is recorded in the fact find but no disadvantage disclosure addresses the client's limited capacity to absorb higher ongoing charges. No vulnerability characteristics are flagged in the evidence that would separately heighten the disclosure requirement here. The guards confirm that disadvantages may be spread across sections (satisfied — the report does spread them) and that a quantified reduction in projected value is the most material disclosure (satisfied — three growth scenarios are given). However, the Standard Life cost direction error and the absence of drawdown-specific risks from the report itself are not explained by any guard and constitute genuine disclosure concerns. The delegation of 'all other risks' to product literature without identifying which are material is a Moderate concern under the severity guidance (generic boilerplate with nothing product-specific on drawdown risks).

      · Pension switch report - updated.md [G] (P2)
        table: Effect of switching  |  —  |  £10 or 0.3% decrease
      · Fact Find (Test 1).md [B] (P24)
        table: Total Monthly Disposable Income  |  £ -288.00

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following product-specific and investment risks: (1) AccessTimescale: funds take 10–15 working days to access. (2) CancellationIrreversibility: previous provider not obliged to take the plan back. (3) CancellationMarketLoss: if investment falls before cancellation notice received, the fall is deducted. (4) PensionIHTLegislationChange: from 6 April 2027, unused DC pensions included in taxable estate for IHT. (5) LimitedInvestmentExperience: client has not taken out a stocks & shares investment. (6) RecommendedFundsUnderperformZurich: recommended Aviva funds have not outperformed current Zurich funds. (7) ZurichHigherRiskVolatility: Zurich funds are higher-risk and more susceptible to volatility. (8) MarketRisk (general): unit prices can fall as well as rise, no guarantee of return of original capital. (9) MPAA: flexible access restricts future contributions to £10,000 with no carry forward. The report does not state: counterparty risk, credit/default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, leverage risk, liquidity risk, emerging markets risk, property liquidity delay risk, interest rate risk — all of which appear in the product/fund literature on file. No explicit loss scenario in monetary terms tied to the recommended product is stated in the risk section, though loss figures appear elsewhere in the report (e.g. £10,400 at 9.6%, £12,000 at 9.4%, £15,000 at 10% referenced in derived figures). Income drawdown risks (income depleting fund, annuity purchase impact, means-tested benefits) are present in the Aviva Key Features but are not addressed in the report despite drawdown being a feature of the recommended Pension Portfolio.
    File:     The Aviva Key Features document (H) discloses: market risk (value can go down as well as up), cancellation risk, currency risk (sterling/overseas), property liquidity delay risk, income drawdown risks (fund depletion if income taken, less for dependants or annuity, tax complications), and MPAA-style warnings. The HSBC KIID (H) discloses: counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, and notes the fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw within 5 years. The Columbia Threadneedle KIID (H) discloses: derivative risk, fixed interest securities risk, credit risk, smaller companies risk, emerging markets risk, liquidity risk, currency risk, leverage risk (rated category 4). The Zurich Policy Info (E) discloses: property fund liquidity delay (up to 12 months), high yield bond risk, smaller companies risk, political/economic/market risk in less developed markets, derivative/OEIC cross-liability risk, interest rate risk on fixed-interest securities, and a pension transfer checklist noting features or benefits that may be lost on transfer. The SIPP research report (G) confirms agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) and the two recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc 50%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C 50%). Risk profile report (D) confirms natural ATR of 4 (Cautious Balanced), agreed at 5.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report contains no disclosure of counterparty risk, which is explicitly listed as a material risk in the HSBC KIID and Columbia Threadneedle KIID for the two recommended funds.
      - The report contains no disclosure of derivatives risk, which appears in both the HSBC KIID and the Columbia Threadneedle KIID for the recommended funds.
      - The report contains no disclosure of credit/default risk, which appears in both KIIDs for the recommended funds.
      - The report contains no disclosure of currency risk applicable to the recommended funds, which appears in both KIIDs and the Aviva Key Features.
      - The report contains no disclosure of leverage risk, which is listed in the Columbia Threadneedle KIID.
      - The report contains no disclosure of emerging markets risk, which appears in the Columbia Threadneedle KIID.
      - The report contains no disclosure of liquidity risk relating to the recommended funds, which appears in the Columbia Threadneedle KIID and the fund factsheet.
      - The report contains no disclosure of income drawdown-specific risks (fund depletion from income withdrawal, reduced provision for dependants/annuity, tax complications) that are disclosed in the Aviva Key Features, despite the recommended product being an Aviva Pension Portfolio capable of drawdown.
      - The report contains no disclosure of the property liquidity delay risk (up to 12 months) stated in the Zurich Policy Info, which is relevant to any property fund exposure within the platform.
      - The Zurich pension transfer documentation flags that plans may have features or benefits that could be lost on transfer; the canonical model records featuresOrGuaranteesLost as NotPresentInDocument — no such disclosure appears in the report for EA4 (Zurich) or other transferred plans.
      - The report's market risk warning is a generic capital-at-risk statement; none of the product-specific risks identified in the KIIDs for the two named recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund) are individually addressed in the report.
      - The Columbia Threadneedle KIID rates the fund at risk category 4; the report does not reference this rating or explain its implications for the client whose agreed ATR is 5.
    The report provides a general market risk warning, access timescale, cancellation risks, an IHT legislative change note, a limited investment experience note, a performance comparison disclosure, a Zurich volatility note, and an MPAA warning. These are not trivial — the IHT and MPAA items are client-specific and material. However, the two recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund) each have KIIDs on file that disclose a set of named, product-specific risks — counterparty, derivatives, credit/default, currency, leverage, emerging markets, liquidity — none of which appear in the report. The guard reminds that standard capital-at-risk wording satisfies the general warning but not product-specific risks; accordingly those omissions remain findings. The Aviva Key Features document on file specifically discloses income drawdown risks (fund depletion, reduced provision for dependants and annuity, tax complications). The recommended product is an Aviva Pension Portfolio which includes drawdown capability; these risks are not addressed in the report. The Zurich Policy Info flags that transferred plans may have forgotten features or benefits; the extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost is NotPresentInDocument for all three switched arrangements — the report does not address what features or protections the client may be giving up on transfer of the Zurich plan in particular. The guard states that deferring risk disclosure to product literature is a limitation on the report and should be recorded as such; there is no explicit delegation statement in the report pointing clients to the KIIDs for these risks. On vulnerability: the extraction notes /vulnerability/C1/assessment is NotPresentInDocument, and the client has limited investment experience (no prior stocks and shares investment). This makes the absence of product-specific risk disclosure more material: a client with no prior equity investment experience is less equipped to assess risks not explained in the report, even if product literature is available. The omissions are not individually catastrophic but collectively represent a disclosure shortfall across multiple product-specific risk categories that are present in the file literature and absent from the report. The most significant omission is the income drawdown risk set, given the Aviva Pension Portfolio's drawdown capability and the Aviva Key Features being on file. The missing features/guarantees disclosure on Zurich transfer is also material. Severity is assessed as Moderate: risks are not wholly absent (general market risk and several specific items are stated) but material product-specific risks are covered only in product literature not in the report itself, and income drawdown risks are unaddressed.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report states the following tax consequences: (1) pension tax relief on contributions — £80 net becomes £100 gross; (2) up to 25% tax-free lump sum at crystallisation subject to Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275; (3) FAD income taxed at marginal rate of Income Tax with no upper limit on withdrawals; (4) death before 75 — benefits paid tax-free to nominated beneficiaries; death after 75 — benefits taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate; (5) Scottish Widows small pot encashment: 25% tax-free, 75% taxed as income at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170; (6) alternative for Scottish Widows: leave pot to grow and potentially take within personal allowance tax-free; (7) Annual Allowance £60,000 (2024/25), excess contributions trigger charge up to 45%, tax relief limited to £3,600 or 100% of salary if higher; (8) from 6 April 2027 unused DC pensions included in taxable estate for IHT; (9) MPAA not triggered; current IHT liability £0. All tax disclosures are on the report's own authority (category I).
    File:     Key Features document [P2] states: withdrawals of up to 25% of pension fund normally tax-free; income payments and lump sum payments taxable as income; lump sum allowance and lump sum & death benefit allowance limits apply; tax information based on current UK legislation and practice, subject to change. Key Features document [P4] states: death before 75 — benefits normally paid tax-free to nominated beneficiaries; if value of tax-free benefits exceeds lump sum & death benefit allowance, beneficiary may pay income tax on excess; death after 75 — full value payable to beneficiaries taxed at marginal rate; 45% charge if paid to trust or personal representatives. Terms and conditions [P1] and [P12] contain no tax consequence disclosures relevant to the recommendation. The pension switch research report [P13] and SIPP research report [P8] contain no independent tax consequence statements. No document in the file contradicts the report's tax disclosures. No client-specific tax band or income tax rate is evidenced in the file for the purpose of verifying marginal-rate statements.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states death benefits before age 75 are 'paid free of tax' without qualification; the Key Features [P4] qualifies this — if the value of tax-free benefits taken from pension plans during lifetime and on death exceeds the lump sum & death benefit allowance, the beneficiary may pay income tax on the excess. The report does not mention this qualification.
      - The report's FAD section [P6] states unused pension fund can 'usually be passed down to a wide range of beneficiaries free from Inheritance Tax' but simultaneously the report on page 9 warns that from 6 April 2027 DC pensions will be included in the taxable estate for IHT. These two statements in the same report are in tension, though the 2027 change arguably resolves the 'usually' qualifier. No external evidence contradicts either statement.
      - The mid growth rate is stated as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative and 2.95% in the page 18 appendix table — a minor internal inconsistency already flagged in the extraction metadata; no tax consequence flows from this.
      - No client-specific marginal income tax rate is stated or evidenced, making the marginal-rate tax statements unverifiable in respect of their precise impact on this client — though per the guard this is unverifiable, not necessarily wrong.
      - The lump sum & death benefit allowance (£1,073,100) is mentioned in the FAD/Lifetime Allowance section [P6] but the death benefits tax treatment on page 9 references only the pre-75 and post-75 outcomes without mentioning the lump sum & death benefit allowance threshold that could cause a tax charge even on pre-75 deaths.
    The report contains a reasonably comprehensive set of tax disclosures covering the main consequences of the recommendation: contribution tax relief, tax-free cash at crystallisation, drawdown income taxation, death benefit tax treatment, small pot encashment tax treatment (with a client-specific monetary illustration), the 2027 IHT change, and the Annual Allowance. These are genuine disclosures rather than delegation to product literature, and the small pot calculation is client-specific and numerically illustrated.
    
    The most material discrepancy is the treatment of pre-75 death benefits. The report states beneficiaries receive benefits 'free of tax' without qualification. The Key Features document [P4] makes clear that this is only true up to the lump sum & death benefit allowance (£1,073,100); above that, the beneficiary may pay income tax on the excess. The report does mention the lump sum & death benefit allowance of £268,275 in the context of tax-free cash, and mentions the lump sum & death benefit allowance of £1,073,100 in the Lifetime Allowance section [P6], but does not connect it to the death benefit tax treatment on page 9. Given the combined pension values are well below £1,073,100, this omission is unlikely to be material to this particular client in practice; however, the report's unqualified statement that pre-75 death benefits are 'paid free of tax' is not fully consistent with [P4].
    
    The tension between the FAD section's statement that unused pension can 'usually be passed down free from Inheritance Tax' and the page 9 warning about the 2027 IHT change is present but not a discrepancy — the qualifier 'usually' and the 2027 change are both stated in the report, and the report clearly draws the client's attention to the forthcoming change. The £0 IHT liability statement [P3] is consistent with the assets disclosed.
    
    The absence of the client's specific marginal income tax rate in the file means the marginal-rate statements cannot be verified as to their precise impact, but per the guard this makes them unverifiable rather than wrong.
    
    The internal inconsistency between 2.94% and 2.95% mid growth rate has no tax consequence.
    
    The Standard Life cost direction contradiction (page 8 narrative vs page 11 table) identified in the extraction metadata does not affect tax disclosures.
    
    Overall, the tax disclosure is substantive and covers the main consequences. The one genuine gap is the failure to mention the lump sum & death benefit allowance threshold in the context of pre-75 death benefit tax treatment, which creates a partial inconsistency with [P4]. Given the fund values involved, this is a low-severity disclosure shortfall rather than a material omission.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P3)

If the value of tax-free benefits taken from your pension plan(s) during your lifetime and on death is more than the lump sum & death benefit allowance, the beneficiary may pay income tax on the excess."

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report contains a dedicated section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8 in the main body of the report. This section warns that stock-market movements may occur whilst transactions are pending, that a switch does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement, that past performance is no guarantee of future returns, and that investment values can fall especially in the early years or if income is taken. Risk content also appears in a 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' section on page 11 (charge comparison table) and a Critical Yield appendix on page 18. The report states a mid-growth rate critical yield of 4.8% and a required outperformance of £4,672 in the first year. The Critical Yield appendix contains a three-scenario (low/mid/high) projection table showing loss scenarios of £10,400 (9.6%), £12,000 (9.4%) and £15,000 (10.0%) at each growth rate respectively. The report records that risk warnings and disadvantages are not confined to the appendix alone and do not appear only in an appendix.
    File:     The pension switch report (category G) contains a combined three-plan critical yield table (P11/P13) that exactly matches the figures in the suitability report appendix: low-growth loss £10,400/9.6%, mid-growth loss £12,000/9.4%, high-growth loss £15,000/10.0%, critical yields 1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%. A separate per-plan Zurich-only table (P9) and Aviva/Standard Life table (P10/P12/P13) are also present. The switch report notes an immediate shortfall in death benefit of £3,305 immediately after the switch (P15: existing £110,185.60, new solution day one £106,880.60). The suitability report appendix (P8) also states the initial adviser charge of £3,305 is taken from the new solution reducing the initial investment accordingly, and records an ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00%. The switch report confirms the combined projection figures used in the suitability report. The evidence pack does not contain product literature for Aviva Platform Personal Pension setting out product-specific risks beyond what appears in the suitability report and pension switch report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report Critical Yield appendix labels the mid growth rate as 2.95% (P1) while the main body narrative on page 11 states 2.94% (P2) — a 0.01 percentage point difference already noted as an internal inconsistency.
      - The loss scenarios and critical yield figures appear in the Critical Yield appendix (page 18) and the charges section (page 11), while the benefits of switching (lower costs, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, lifestyling removal) are stated prominently in the per-plan recommendation sections earlier in the body of the report (pages 6–8). The dedicated risk section 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8 does appear in the main body, but the quantified loss scenarios (£10,400–£15,000) are relegated to the appendix on page 18.
      - The death benefit shortfall of £3,305 immediately on switching is disclosed in the pension switch report (P15) but the suitability report does not appear to state this consequence in the main body — it records the initial adviser charge of £3,305 in the appendix (P8) but does not explicitly draw out the immediate death benefit reduction as a named disadvantage in the 'Other Considerations of Switching' section.
      - The Standard Life narrative on page 8 (P6) states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the switch charges table on page 11 (P4) shows the EA5 impact as +0.26% (new solution more expensive). These figures are contradictory in both magnitude and direction — already flagged as an internal inconsistency — and affect whether costs are fairly presented as a disadvantage or benefit for the Standard Life switch.
      - The charge arithmetic flags that the EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of approximately £103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's £3,002, indicating the cost comparison row for Standard Life appears to be computed on the wrong plan. This means the monetary disadvantage/cost disclosure for the Standard Life switch is unreliable.
      - The risk warning section contains generic statements (past performance, values can fall) without product-specific risk content relating to the CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund or HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio that are the actual recommended investments.
    The suitability report does have a dedicated risk/disadvantages section ('Other Considerations of Switching', page 8) in the main body, which satisfies the structural requirement that risk content is not confined to the appendix alone. The section contains warnings about market movements during the switch, no guarantee of outperformance, and general investment risk. This is not mere boilerplate in the sense that it addresses the switching context specifically.
    
    However, three concerns arise on prominence and accuracy:
    
    1. Quantified loss scenarios are appendix-only. The material monetary loss figures — £10,400 to £15,000 potential decrease depending on growth rate — appear only in the Critical Yield appendix at page 18. The benefits of switching (lower costs, lifestyling removal, greater choice) are stated prominently and repeatedly in the per-plan recommendation sections at pages 6–8. The death benefit shortfall of £3,305 on day one (confirmed by P15) is disclosed in the appendix but is not named as a discrete disadvantage in the main body risk section. This is a prominence concern: benefits lead, quantified harms trail in the appendix.
    
    2. The Standard Life cost comparison is internally contradictory and unreliable. The narrative states the existing Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution (a benefit), while the charges table shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive (a cost/disadvantage). The derived figures confirm the Standard Life row appears to be computed on Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's. This means the cost disclosure for that switch is misleading — it appears to present a switch as cost-saving when the charges table (itself likely computed on the wrong base) and the narrative contradict each other. Where a switch involves an increased cost to the client, the regulatory obligation is to explain and justify that cost; here the report neither clearly acknowledges it nor justifies it.
    
    3. Risk content is generic rather than product-specific. The warnings about values falling and past performance are standard. No product-specific risk content relates to the CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc or HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio — the actual funds recommended — such as fund-specific risks, concentration, or liquidity considerations.
    
    The 2.94%/2.95% mid-growth rate discrepancy is minor and does not affect the substance of the disclosure.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment is recorded in the canonical model (path /vulnerability/C1/assessment noted as NotPresentInDocument). The report records that John's experience is limited to workplace pension arrangements. No specific vulnerability characteristics are evidenced that would require adjusted communication of risks beyond standard clear presentation, so this does not materially alter the finding — but the absence of a formal vulnerability assessment means there is no confirmation that the risk presentation was tailored appropriately if any vulnerability existed.
    
    Overall: the report has a risk section in the main body, but quantified loss scenarios are relegated to an appendix while benefits are prominent in the body; the death benefit shortfall is not named as a disadvantage in the main body; and the Standard Life cost comparison is contradictory and unreliable, preventing a fair cost/disadvantage disclosure for that switch. These are Disclosure shortfall and Data inconsistency concerns of moderate-to-high severity.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states for each of the three transferring arrangements (Aviva EA3, Zurich EA4, Standard Life EA5) and the two retained arrangements: 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits.' The report also records, for some arrangements, whether a servicing adviser is permitted and what retirement options are available (Flexi-Access Drawdown, UFPLS, Partial UFPLS). No other features or protections being given up are described in the canonical model for any of the three plans being switched. The extraction metadata confirms that /replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument) for all three replacement analyses.
    File:     Policy literature and research confirm: (1) Standard Life (EA5): No GARs, no GMP, no guaranteed fund, no Reference Scheme Test, no safeguarded benefits. However, Standard Life confirms a Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount: 0.4800%) being applied via creation of units monthly — 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' Standard Life also confirms partial transfers for investment purposes are not permitted and the plan does facilitate drawdown. (2) Aviva (EA3): No life cover, no policy fee, no exit charge, 0.50% annual fund charge, cash transfers only (no in-specie), plan does not allow adviser charging. (3) Zurich (EA4): Policy research confirms no GAR, no GMP, no guaranteed growth rates, no life cover attached. The Zurich policy info references exit charges as a possibility on some plans. (4) People's Pension (EA1, retained): No GAR, No GMP, No guaranteed fund, waiver of premium No, life cover No. (5) Scottish Widows (EA2, retained): No GMP, no guaranteed minimum fund, no reference scheme test, no waiver of premium, no life cover, no fund rebates. Policy research table confirms FAD availability: People's Pension No (unless fund value increases to £10k), Scottish Widows No, Aviva Yes, Zurich Yes, Standard Life Yes. Full and partial UFPLS: People's Pension Yes, Scottish Widows Yes, Aviva No, Zurich No, Standard Life Yes. The report asserts Aviva has FAD+UFPLS+PartialUFPLS but the policy research shows Aviva has FAD=Yes and Full/Partial UFPLS=No. The report asserts Zurich has only UFPLS+PartialUFPLS but the policy research shows Zurich has FAD=Yes and Full/Partial UFPLS=No. The Standard Life policy info identifies a specific Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount 0.4800%) that will be lost on transfer, which is not mentioned anywhere in the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Standard Life policy letter explicitly warns that the Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount: 0.4800%) 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' — this is a material protection being lost on transfer that is entirely absent from the suitability report.
      - The report asserts Aviva (EA3) offers FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS and PartialUFPLS. Policy research shows Aviva has FAD=Yes but Full and Partial UFPLS=No. The report overstates the retirement options available on the Aviva plan.
      - The report asserts Zurich (EA4) offers only UFPLS and PartialUFPLS (no FAD). Policy research shows Zurich has FAD=Yes and Full/Partial UFPLS=No. The report mis-states both the FAD availability and the UFPLS availability for Zurich.
      - The report makes no mention of Aviva's restriction that only cash transfers are possible (no in-specie transfers), which is a feature/limitation of the existing plan relevant to the transfer process.
      - The report makes no mention of Standard Life's restriction that partial transfers for investment purposes are not permitted from this contract.
      - The featuresOrGuaranteesLost field is entirely absent from the report for all three replacement analyses (confirmed NotPresentInDocument by extraction metadata), meaning no structured disclosure of features lost on transfer appears anywhere in the report.
    The central finding is the Standard Life Differential Fund Discount. The policy letter (P10) states a Leaver Discount of 0.4800% is being applied as a creation of units monthly, and explicitly warns that 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a concrete, named financial benefit attached specifically to this member's plan that will be extinguished on transfer. It is a material protection being given up. The suitability report contains no mention of it anywhere — the featuresOrGuaranteesLost path is absent (NotPresentInDocument) and the existing arrangements table for EA5 records only the charge figures without any reference to this discount. This is a material disclosure gap. The guard instructs that a guarantee or discount recorded in provider literature but not mentioned in the report is the finding, and that is precisely the position here. The second set of discrepancies concern the retirement options stated for Aviva and Zurich. The policy research table (P8) shows Aviva has FAD=Yes, UFPLS=No; the report claims FAD+UFPLS+PartialUFPLS. For Zurich, the research shows FAD=Yes, UFPLS=No; the report claims only UFPLS+PartialUFPLS and omits FAD. These are internal inconsistencies between the report's assertions and the policy research, but their materiality to the 'features being given up' limb is moderate — they concern what options exist on existing plans, and the client is switching away from them. The more significant implication is that the report may be understating or mis-stating what the client currently has, which could affect the quality of the comparative disclosure. However, the dominant and most material finding is the undisclosed Standard Life fund discount. The no-safeguarded-benefits statement is corroborated by all five policy documents (P5 Scottish Widows, P10/P17 Standard Life, P2 policy research GARs/GMP all No, P14 Aviva) — that specific assertion is consistent with evidence. But the absence of any disclosure of the Standard Life Differential Fund Discount, which is plan-specific and flagged by the provider as potentially unavailable elsewhere, constitutes a Disclosure Shortfall and Missing Component of material significance.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states that including adviser fees the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring the proposed plan to outperform by £4,672 in the first year. The pension switch charges-only assessment shows the new solution is cheaper than the existing plans for Aviva (0.06% saving) and Zurich (0.06% saving), but more expensive than Standard Life (0.26% more expensive, £268.94). The report narratively justifies the Standard Life switch by stating the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution — which contradicts the table showing the new solution is 0.26% more expensive. Projections at low/mid/high growth rates are given in a critical yield appendix (£97,600 / £115,000 / £135,000 at low/mid/high). The report does not explain why the overall 4.24% cost increase including adviser fees is acceptable beyond the critical yield disclosure. Multiple investment justifications are given (risk alignment, consolidation, fund choice, drawdown flexibility, adviser servicing) but these are benefit statements rather than a direct explanation of why the net cost increase is in the client's interest.
    File:     The pension switch research report (category G) confirms the mid-growth projected value of the new solution is £125,000 versus £127,000 for the existing solution — a £2,000 or 1.6% decrease. At low growth the new solution projects £100,000 versus £102,000 existing (£2,000 or 2.0% decrease). At high growth £147,000 versus £150,000 (£3,000 or 2.0% decrease). The research report's projected maturity values for the existing pension at mid growth are £120,000 under a charges-only view and £127,000 in the full comparison. The critical yield table in the report itself states mid growth at 2.95%, while the narrative on page 11 states 2.94%. The derived figures confirm that the EA5 (Standard Life) charges row is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the 0.26% impact figure and the £268.94 annual charge difference unreliable. The research report shows the new solution ranks 5th of 9 products by projected maturity value at mid growth, below the existing solution, Zurich standalone, a plain Aviva plan, and a Standard Life plan.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report narrative for Standard Life (page 8) states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the pension switch charges table (page 11) and the derived figures show the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life — the direction of the cost comparison is inverted in the narrative.
      - The report states the overall cost increase including adviser fees is 4.24% (£4,672 required first-year outperformance) but provides no narrative explanation of why this net cost increase is justified beyond listing benefits that also apply to cheaper or equally-priced alternatives.
      - The mid-growth rate is stated as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative but 2.95% in the page 18 critical yield appendix — a minor internal inconsistency.
      - The report's critical yield appendix projects £115,000 (mid) for the new solution, while the research report projects £125,000 for the new solution at mid growth — different figures for ostensibly the same projection (likely different fund value bases or charge inputs, but no explanation is given in the report).
      - The EA5 Standard Life annual charge impact figure (0.26%, £268.94) is derived on Zurich's fund value (£103,438) rather than Standard Life's actual value (£3,002), meaning the monetary cost difference stated for Standard Life is materially wrong, yet the report presents it without qualification.
      - The research report shows the new solution ranked 5th out of 9 by projected maturity value, below the existing solution, but the report does not acknowledge this relative ranking or explain why a lower-ranked solution is preferred on a cost-outcome basis.
      - All risks are stated to be 'detailed in the product literature provided' — risk disclosure is partially delegated to product literature for risks not enumerated in the report.
    The central question for G8.7 is whether the report explains to the client why increased costs or reduced projected outcomes are acceptable. The report does disclose the cost increase (4.24% including adviser fees, £4,672 required outperformance) and provides a critical yield comparison. It also discloses that projected values under the new solution are lower than the existing solution across all growth scenarios (this is visible in the critical yield appendix rows showing existing versus new solution values, and corroborated by the research report at P1 and P15). The report lists several benefit justifications (risk alignment, consolidation, fund choice, drawdown flexibility, adviser servicing). These are not empty statements — they connect to specific client needs identified elsewhere in the report — so they constitute a justification, albeit one that repeats benefits without directly addressing why the cost increase is proportionate to those benefits. That is a weak justification rather than an absent one, per the guard. However, two more serious issues remain. First, the Standard Life narrative directly contradicts the charges table: the narrative says the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the new solution, but the table and derived figures show the opposite — the new solution is 0.26% more expensive (and the derived figures confirm the 0.26%/£268.94 figure is itself computed on the wrong fund value, Zurich's rather than Standard Life's). This means the client has been given a materially incorrect cost comparison for the Standard Life switch specifically, which is a disclosure failure rather than mere weakness. Second, the partial delegation of risk disclosure to product literature ('All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you') creates a disclosure shortfall for risks not enumerated in the body of the report. The vulnerability overlay: no formal vulnerability assessment is present in the canonical model (/vulnerability/C1/assessment is listed as NotPresentInDocument), and no vulnerability-related communication adjustments are recorded. The risk profile questionnaire result of 4 was overridden to 5 by the adviser, which itself was not formally documented. Neither of these directly bears on the cost justification disclosure, but the absence of a vulnerability assessment means there is no basis on which to confirm that the complexity of the cost justification disclosure (multiple charges tables, critical yield appendix, narrative contradicting table) was presented at a level appropriate for any identified communication needs. The mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.94% vs 2.95%) is minor and falls within rounding; it is noted but not a primary concern. The different projection figures between the report's critical yield appendix (£115,000) and the research report (£125,000) at mid growth suggest different fund value bases are used in each tool, but the report does not explain this, which could confuse a client comparing the two figures. On balance: the most material finding is the inverted Standard Life cost narrative (a clear misdisclosure of direction of cost impact), combined with that figure itself being computed on the wrong fund value. This is a disclosure shortfall rising to Data inconsistency. The overall cost justification is present but weak. Severity is Moderate: the cost justification exists and the critical yield is disclosed, but a specific switch contains a materially wrong and directionally inverted cost narrative, and the underlying figure is unreliable.
