  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report discloses the following material disadvantages and risks: (1) Including adviser fee, the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one. (2) At mid growth rate (2.94%), the recommended plan needs to grow by 4.8% per year to age 75 to match current plan value. (3) Switching effects: low growth £10,400/9.6% decrease; mid growth £12,000/9.4% decrease; high growth £15,000/10.0% decrease at age 75. (4) Scottish Widows would limit flexible withdrawal. (5) Transferring from People's Pension would lose employer contributions. (6) Standard Life charge is 0.18% existing vs 0.44% recommended, 0.26% more expensive. (7) All other risks in product literature. (8) Transaction costs excluded from charges. (9) Standard Life performance data unavailable. (10) Cashflow surplus assumption. (11) Access timescale 10-15 working days. (12) Cancellation irreversibility and cancellation market loss risk. (13) IHT legislative change from April 2027. The report does not state features or guarantees lost on any of the three switches (paths flagged as NotPresentInDocument by extraction).
    File:     [P10] (Pension switch report) confirms the three switching effect scenarios: low growth £10,400/9.6% decrease, mid growth £12,000/9.4% decrease, high growth £15,000/10.0% decrease at age 75, matching the report. [P10] shows critical yield at mid growth as 4.8%, matching the report's 4.8%. [P2] shows mid growth rate in the pension switch report as 2.95%, while the report narrative states 2.94% (already flagged as an internal inconsistency). [P15] (Standard Life Policy Info) states the plan benefits from a Differential Fund Discount/Leaver Discount of 0.4800% from the TER, explicitly noting 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' [P16] (Aviva Policy Info) confirms Aviva annual fund charge of 0.50% and no transferring-out charge. [P8] (Zurich Policy Info) raises questions about guaranteed annuity rates and life/waiver benefits no longer available on new pension plans, and that completing a medical questionnaire may be required for similar cover. [P22]/[P23] (Standard Life Policy Info) confirm no Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GARs) and no Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP) for Standard Life. [P3] notes 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' in the pension switch report's benefits section. [P11] (Key Features) discloses income drawdown risks including impact on means-tested benefits, fund depletion risk from high withdrawals, and tax risks. [P18]/[P19] (KIIDs) disclose counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, and that the fund may not be appropriate for investors planning to withdraw within 5 years. The Standard Life policy info [P15] explicitly flags that the low charges from the Differential Fund Discount/Leaver Discount may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer — this is a material, plan-specific risk directly relevant to the switch decision.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Standard Life policy document [P15] explicitly states that the plan benefits from a Leaver Discount of 0.4800% which 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' The report does not mention this discount or the risk of losing it when switching. This is a material product-specific feature/discount being surrendered that the report omits entirely.
      - The report states the mid growth rate as 2.94% in the narrative (page 11) but [P2] and [P10] from the pension switch report use 2.95% as the mid growth rate. This is a minor inconsistency but a discrepancy between the report and the underlying research document.
      - The report states 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you' but does not itself reference the income drawdown risks (impact on means-tested benefits, fund depletion risk) or the short-term investment horizon risk disclosed in [P11] and [P18]/[P19], nor does it reference the specific product-specific risks from the key features document by name.
      - The extraction flags featuresOrGuaranteesLost as NotPresentInDocument for all three switches (EA2, EA3, EA4 replacement analyses), meaning the report does not address what features or guarantees are being given up on any of the three switched plans. While [P22]/[P23] confirm no GARs or GMP for Standard Life, and [P8] raises the question of life cover and waiver benefits no longer available on new plans for Zurich, the report does not address whether Zurich's existing plan has or had any such benefits.
      - The Zurich policy information [P8] notes 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' and that 'You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan.' The report does not address whether the Zurich plan carries any such benefits or whether they would be lost on transfer.
    The report provides a substantive risk and disadvantage section: it quantifies switching costs, provides three-scenario loss projections, discloses the Standard Life higher charge, warns about Scottish Widows flexibility limitation, employer contributions loss, IHT legislative change, cancellation risks, access timescales, and delegates residual risks to product literature. This is a reasonably thorough disclosure.
    
    However, two material omissions are identified from the evidence:
    
    1. Standard Life Leaver Discount [P15]: The Standard Life policy document explicitly states the plan benefits from a Differential Fund Discount/Leaver Discount of 0.4800% from the TER, with a direct warning that 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a client-specific, plan-specific advantage being surrendered. The report's charge comparison shows Standard Life at 0.18% existing vs 0.44% recommended (0.26% more expensive), which appears to reflect this discount in the existing charge, but the report never explains that this low existing charge is a special discounted rate that will be permanently lost on transfer. This is a material disadvantage the report fails to explain clearly — it states the cost difference but not that the existing low rate is a protected discount that cannot be replicated.
    
    2. Features/guarantees lost on transfers [extraction flags]: The extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost is NotPresentInDocument for all three switch analyses. The Zurich policy information [P8] raises the issue of life cover and waiver benefits, and while the pension switch report's benefits section [P3] states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products,' the Zurich policy info does not definitively confirm these benefits are absent — it raises the question. The report provides no engagement with this issue for Zurich.
    
    The mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.94% in report narrative vs 2.95% in research tool) is minor (0.01 percentage point) and does not materially affect the switching effect figures, which are corroborated by [P10].
    
    The guard that 'a risk disclosed in a separate document given to the client is disclosed' applies to the income drawdown risks in [P11] and the KIID risks in [P18]/[P19] since the report delegates these to product literature. However, the Leaver Discount issue in [P15] is a material plan-specific advantage being surrendered that goes beyond generic risk content — it is specific research information that undercuts the cost-benefit case and is not adequately explained in the report.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability assessment is present (/vulnerability/C1/assessment flagged as NotPresentInDocument). The file does not identify specific vulnerability characteristics that would heighten the concern here beyond the general population.
    
    On balance: the omission of the Standard Life Leaver Discount as a material feature being lost is a disclosure shortfall of moderate severity — the cost difference is stated but the nature of the existing plan's protected discounted charge, and the permanent loss of that discount on transfer, is not explained.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). The report's performance comparison cites the recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio 5yr cumulative return as 40.02%, but the volatility chart in [P6/P14] shows 42.40% for 'Aviva Risk 5 portfolio TR in GB' over 30/09/2020–30/09/2025. These are different periods (13/10/2020–13/10/2025 vs 30/09/2020–30/09/2025) but the report treats the 40.02% figure as the definitive 5yr comparison figure while the evidence shows 42.40% on a slightly different period — the performance table in [P14] shows 'Aviva Risk 5 portfolio TR in GB' with 5yrs column value that is partially truncated but the volatility chart gives 42.40%. Standard Life existing charge stated as 0.52% in existing arrangements table ([P16]) but 0.18% in the pension switch charges table — these are directly contradictory within the report and both appear in suitability report passages. Zurich existing charge stated as 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin in existing arrangements table ([P16]) but 0.93% in the pension switch charges table — materially inconsistent figures for the same plan within the report. 17 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report compares three ceding plans (Aviva EA3, Zurich EA4, Standard Life EA5) against the recommended Aviva platform solution across multiple dimensions: (1) Charges — Aviva existing 0.50% vs recommended 0.44%, Zurich existing 0.93% vs recommended 0.44%, Standard Life existing 0.18% (switch table p.11) / 0.52% (existing arrangements table p.5) vs recommended 0.44%; (2) Fund range — Aviva 80+ funds (lifestyling) vs 6,900+ on new platform, Zurich 60+ funds (no risk-5 multi-asset) vs 6,900+; (3) Risk alignment — Aviva lifestyling will reduce risk below agreed profile; Zurich requires blend of risk-7 and risk-2 funds; recommended offers risk-5 multi-asset; (4) Adviser charging facilitation — existing plans do not allow it, recommended does; (5) Retirement options — Zurich offers full/partial UFPLS only vs FAD + UFPLS on recommended; (6) Performance — Aviva existing 5yr 15.58%, recommended 5yr 40.02%; Zurich existing fund 44.32% over same period but at higher risk/volatility; Standard Life performance noted as unavailable in system with screenshot reference. The report states no safeguarded benefits exist for any plan. A switch charges table on p.11 and critical yield analysis are included. The report states for Standard Life that 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (p.8 narrative).
    File:     [P16/P23] Existing arrangements table corroborates fund values, plan numbers, safeguarded benefits statement ('This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' for all five plans), retirement options (Aviva: FAD + full/partial UFPLS; Zurich: full/partial UFPLS only; Standard Life: FAD only), fund counts (Aviva 80+, Zurich 60+, Standard Life 300), and adviser charging (all 'No' except Standard Life 'Yes'). [P8] Policy research confirms: Aviva — no adviser charging, no exit penalties; Zurich — exit penalties yes; Standard Life — adviser charging yes, no exit penalties. [P18] Aviva policy document confirms plan value £3,744.36, fund = Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2, auto-switching/lifestage investment approach, no guaranteed rates, no GMP. [P1/P8] Policy research shows Zurich FAD: No (UFPLS only confirmed), Aviva FAD: Yes, Standard Life FAD: Yes. [P14] Performance chart and table corroborate Aviva existing 5yr 15.58%, Zurich Managed AP 5yr 44.32%; recommended Aviva Risk 5 5yr shown in volatility chart as 42.40% (not 40.02% as stated in performance table label). [P6] Volatility chart key shows Aviva Risk 5 portfolio performance 42.40% / volatility 7.56% — different from the 40.02% cited by the report as the recommended fund's 5yr cumulative in the comparison dimension. [P3/P17] Pension switch report (Defaqto) corroborates the overall switch comparison analysis, projected maturity values, critical yields, and confirms 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' (lost benefits). [P9] Defaqto new solution ranking: new solution ranks 5th out of 10 by maturity value at mid growth (£125,000 vs existing solution £127,000), which is acknowledged. [P12] Confirms day-one shortfall of £3,305 due to initial adviser charge. The Defaqto report uses mid growth rate of 2.95% (labelled as such in P17/P20), while the report's narrative on p.11 states 2.94%. Charge inconsistencies from extraction: Zurich existing charge stated as 0.18% + £2.12/month admin in existing arrangements table but 0.93% in switch table; Standard Life stated as 0.52% in existing arrangements table but 0.18% in switch table — these are material internal contradictions in the report itself corroborated by the extraction metadata. Standard Life switch narrative (p.8) says existing plan is 0.08% more expensive, but derived figures show the EA5 charge row is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) not Standard Life's (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable.     Discrepancies:
      - The report's performance comparison cites the recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio 5yr cumulative return as 40.02%, but the volatility chart in [P6/P14] shows 42.40% for 'Aviva Risk 5 portfolio TR in GB' over 30/09/2020–30/09/2025. These are different periods (13/10/2020–13/10/2025 vs 30/09/2020–30/09/2025) but the report treats the 40.02% figure as the definitive 5yr comparison figure while the evidence shows 42.40% on a slightly different period — the performance table in [P14] shows 'Aviva Risk 5 portfolio TR in GB' with 5yrs column value that is partially truncated but the volatility chart gives 42.40%.
      - Standard Life existing charge stated as 0.52% in existing arrangements table ([P16]) but 0.18% in the pension switch charges table — these are directly contradictory within the report and both appear in suitability report passages.
      - Zurich existing charge stated as 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin in existing arrangements table ([P16]) but 0.93% in the pension switch charges table — materially inconsistent figures for the same plan within the report.
      - The derived figures confirm the EA5 (Standard Life) charge row in the switch table is computed on a fund value consistent with Zurich (£103,439) not Standard Life (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically unreliable.
      - The Standard Life switch narrative (p.8) states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the switch charges table shows EA5 impact as +0.26% (new solution more expensive) — a direct contradiction in direction per the extraction metadata.
      - The pension switch Defaqto report mid growth rate is labelled 2.95% ([P17/P20]) while the suitability report narrative states 2.94%.
      - The report claims no existing plan allows adviser charging facilitation as a reason to switch, but [P23] shows Standard Life does allow a servicing adviser ('Yes'), and [P8] confirms this. Standard Life is one of the three ceding plans.
      - Zurich exit penalties: [P8] policy research shows 'Yes' for exit penalties, but the suitability report's existing arrangements comparison table does not appear to address this switching cost dimension explicitly for Zurich.
      - The report states the recommended fund's 5yr performance (40.02%) is superior to Zurich's existing fund (44.32%) — this is acknowledged in the report text — but the comparison dimension labelled 'Performance / Better' in the canonical model does not reflect that Zurich's existing fund outperformed the recommended solution over 5 years.
      - No features or guarantees lost section is populated in the report (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument for all three ceding plans), though the Defaqto report [P17] states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products'.
    The report contains a multi-dimensional comparison of existing versus recommended arrangements and is supported in several key areas by the evidence: the Aviva policy document [P18] confirms the lifestaging approach and absence of safeguarded benefits; the Defaqto pension switch report [P3/P17] corroborates the switch analysis, critical yields, projected maturity values and confirms no benefits will be lost; policy research [P1/P8/P13] supports the fund range, retirement option and adviser charging comparisons. However, several material issues remain after applying the guards.
    
    First, the charge inconsistencies for Standard Life and Zurich are internal report contradictions, not a product of date differences or layout ambiguity — the same plan has two materially different charge figures stated in the same document (Standard Life: 0.52% vs 0.18%; Zurich: 0.18%+£2.12/month vs 0.93%). These are directly relevant to CHK-009 because the cost justification for both switches rests on the switch charges table, which the existing arrangements table contradicts. The derived figures further confirm the Standard Life EA5 rows in the switch table are computed on Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's own £3,002 fund value, making the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically wrong and unreliable as a basis for justifying that switch.
    
    Second, the Standard Life narrative direction contradiction (report p.8 says existing plan 0.08% more expensive; table shows new solution 0.26% more expensive) is a direct conflict within the report on the central cost justification for the Standard Life switch. This is a high-severity issue under the decision logic: a switch justified on cost savings that the report's own comparison contradicts.
    
    Third, the adviser charging facilitation rationale is overstated: the report presents this as an advantage of switching for all three plans, but Standard Life (one of the three ceding plans) already allows adviser charging per [P23] and [P8]. This weakens one stated benefit without acknowledgement.
    
    Fourth, Zurich exit penalties are confirmed by policy research [P8] but are not explicitly addressed in the report's switch cost analysis. This is a missing component in the switching cost disclosure.
    
    Fifth, the performance comparison dimension is labelled 'Better' for both Aviva and the overall comparison, but the Zurich existing fund (44.32% 5yr) outperformed the recommended solution. The report does acknowledge this in the narrative text ([P6]), so this is not a hidden omission, but the canonical model's 'Better' assessment for the Performance dimension across the board is misleading given Zurich's outperformance.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are noted in the extraction that would materially alter this assessment.
    
    The guard regarding date differences in fund-range data does not explain the charge inconsistencies, which are point-in-time figures about the same plan on the same date. The guard about one-sided comparisons is relevant: the Zurich performance comparison is addressed in the report text but labelled 'Better' in the canonical model, which is the report's own inconsistency rather than an extraction artefact.
    
    Overall, the switch justification for Standard Life in particular is materially undermined by contradictory charge figures and an arithmetically unreliable cost comparison, and Zurich's exit penalties are unaddressed. These are Potential Concerns requiring material clarification.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents per-plan cost/benefit analyses for three switching plans (EA3 Aviva, EA4 Zurich, EA5 Standard Life). For Aviva (EA3): existing 0.50%/£18.72 pa, new plan -0.06%/-£2.25 cheaper, so lower cost is claimed as a benefit. For Zurich (EA4): existing 0.93%/£961.98 pa, new plan -0.49%/-£506.85 cheaper. For Standard Life (EA5): existing plan stated to be 0.08% more expensive than recommended on page 8, but the switch charges table on page 11 shows the new solution costs 0.26%/£268.94 MORE than the existing Standard Life plan. Benefits claimed across plans include: lower costs, lifestyling risk avoidance, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset fund alignment (Zurich-specific), enhanced retirement flexibility. Exit penalties stated as £0 for all three. Out-of-market exposure risk disclosed. Critical yield narrative states mid-growth rate of 2.94% and required outperformance of £4,672 at 4.8% pa to age 75. No features or guarantees lost are mentioned (extraction confirmed absent from document).
    File:     The Defaqto pension switch report (category G) provides plan-level comparison tables for Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life vs the new Aviva Portfolio solution. At mid-growth (2.95%), switching all three produces a projected maturity value of £125,000 vs £127,000 retained (£2,000/1.6% decrease, critical yield 3.2%). The switch report's reduction in yield for the new solution across all three plans is 0.6% vs 0.3% for the existing portfolio — confirming the new solution has a higher overall reduction in yield. The Zurich policy document (P9) confirms no transfer value or early retirement charge, and the fund switch charge is £0. The Standard Life policy doc (P16) confirms transfer charge £0.00. The Scottish Widows policy doc (P14) notes that transfer may result in loss of protected tax-free cash or protected pension age. The Peoples Pension policy info (P10) shows ongoing employer contributions into the retained People's Pension. The switch report also shows an immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.60). The mid-growth rate used in the switch report is 2.95%, vs the report's narrative of 2.94%. No documentation from any provider confirms absence of safeguarded benefits for EA3, EA4, or EA5.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. Standard Life (EA5) cost direction contradiction: the suitability report narrative (page 8) states the existing plan is 0.08% MORE expensive than the recommended solution (i.e. switching saves money), but the switch charges table (page 11) shows the new solution costs 0.26%/£268.94 MORE than the existing Standard Life plan — directly opposite directions.
      - 2. EA5 charge arithmetic applied to wrong fund: the derived figures show that EA5's existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and the EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund value of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's (EA4) fund value, not Standard Life's £3,002. The comparison for Standard Life is therefore computed on the wrong base and is unreliable.
      - 3. Mid-growth rate: the suitability report narrative states 2.94% but the Defaqto switch report (P6) uses 2.95%. These differ by 0.01 percentage point.
      - 4. No evidence of safeguarded benefits check for EA3 (Aviva), EA4 (Zurich) or EA5 (Standard Life): the extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the document for all three plans, and no provider document in the pack positively confirms the absence of safeguarded benefits or guarantees for Aviva or Standard Life (Zurich policy doc P9 confirms no transfer/early retirement charge but does not address safeguarded benefits specifically).
      - 5. The switch report (P6/P12) shows the new solution has a higher reduction in yield (0.6%) than the existing portfolio (0.3%) at all growth rates, and produces lower projected maturity values at all growth rates — yet the report claims lower costs as a primary benefit without adequately reconciling this.
      - 6. Immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 disclosed in the switch report (P7/P12) is not referenced in the suitability report's costs of switching section.
      - 7. Zurich existing charge: existing arrangements table states 0.18% fund charge plus £2.12/month admin, but the switch charges table states 0.93% as the current annual recurring charge for Zurich — these are materially inconsistent and cannot both be correct as a total.
      - 8. Standard Life existing charge: existing arrangements table states 0.52% fund charge, but the switch charges table states 0.18% as the current annual recurring charge — directly contradictory.
      - 9. Scottish Widows (EA2, retained) policy note warns that transfer may result in loss of protected tax-free cash or protected pension age — though EA2 is stated as retained, this note highlights the sensitivity around guarantees that is not addressed for the switching plans.
      - 10. The report does not address whether benefits outweigh the higher cost for Standard Life (EA5), given the switch charges table shows it is more expensive after switching.
    Several material problems affect this requirement. The most significant is the Standard Life (EA5) cost direction contradiction: the suitability report on page 8 tells the client the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, implying a saving from switching, while the switch charges table on page 11 — within the same report — shows the new solution costs 0.26%/£268.94 more per year. These are opposite directional claims and cannot both be correct. The canonical model itself flags this as a known internal inconsistency. Compounding this, the derived figures confirm that the charge arithmetic for EA5 is applied to the wrong fund value: £186.19 at 0.18% and £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a base of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's £3,002. This means the entire cost comparison for the Standard Life switch is computed on the wrong plan and is unreliable. No explanation is provided in the report for why a more expensive switch to Standard Life (if the table is correct) is justified by benefits — the report simply asserts it is cheaper, which is contradicted. On safeguarded benefits: the extraction confirms that featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the document for all three switching plans. The Zurich policy document (P9) confirms no transfer or early retirement charge, but this is not the same as confirming no safeguarded benefits. No provider evidence in the pack positively confirms the absence of safeguarded benefits for Aviva (EA3) or Standard Life (EA5). The Scottish Widows note in P14 (retained plan) warns of potential loss of protected TFC or protected pension age on transfer — this heightens the concern about the absence of a similar check for the switching plans. The immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 is evidenced in the switch report (P7, P12) but not disclosed in the suitability report's costs of switching section, which is a disclosure shortfall. The mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.94% in narrative vs 2.95% in the switch report) is a minor inconsistency. The Zurich and Standard Life charge figures in the existing arrangements table are inconsistent with those in the switch charges table (0.18%+admin vs 0.93% for Zurich; 0.52% vs 0.18% for Standard Life), meaning the charge base for the cost comparison is unreliable across multiple plans. The report does present benefits including lifestyling risk, multi-asset fund availability and investment choice, but these do not address the cost contradiction for Standard Life. The 'higher costs justified by benefits' analysis is absent for EA5 given the switch charges table shows it costs more. No guard explains these discrepancies: the cost direction conflict is a genuine contradiction within the report's own data, and the wrong-fund arithmetic is confirmed by the derived figures. Vulnerability: no vulnerability assessment is present in the pack (extraction confirms /vulnerability/C1/assessment absent), so no overlay adjustment is required on that basis.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts, for each of the three ceding arrangements (Aviva SP50163762, Zurich, and Standard Life) and for each existing arrangement overall, that 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits.' The report also asserts that no employer contributions are lost on any of the four replacement analyses. No other features or guarantees are said to be lost. The featuresOrGuaranteesLost paths were marked NotPresentInDocument by the extractor, meaning the report does not separately itemise features lost beyond the safeguarded-benefits assertion.
    File:     Provider documentation confirms the following. Aviva (SP50163762) [P17]: 'This plan doesn't have any guaranteed rates, including annuity rates' and 'The plan doesn't include a Guaranteed Minimum Pension'; no protected tax-free lump sum. Zurich [P15]: 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' Scottish Widows (EA2, being encashed not switched) [P10]: 'I can also confirm that there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan.' [P21] confirms Guaranteed Annuity Rates: No and Guaranteed Minimum Pension: No. Standard Life (EA5) [P4]: 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's)', 'No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)', 'No Guaranteed Fund at retirement', 'No Reference Scheme Test', 'As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan.' However, [P23] notes that Standard Life applies a 'Differential Fund Discount' (a Leaver Discount of 0.4800%) which 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' — this is a feature that may be lost on transfer and is not addressed in the report. The policy research table [P3] confirms No GAR, No GMP and No guaranteed growth rates across all arrangements. No employer contributions are currently being made to any of the ceding plans: Aviva is paid up [P17], Zurich is paid up [P19], and Standard Life is paid up [P19].
    Discrepancies:
      - The Standard Life policy letter [P23] explicitly warns that the Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount of 0.4800%) 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer', yet the report makes no reference to this potentially lost benefit and does not address whether the switch to the Aviva Platform preserves equivalent or better pricing relative to this discount.
      - The report's featuresOrGuaranteesLost field is absent (NotPresentInDocument) for all three replacement analyses — the extractor confirmed this is not present in the document — meaning the report does not contain a dedicated section addressing what features or guarantees are lost, only the bare assertion of no safeguarded benefits.
      - The Standard Life charge shown in the existing arrangements table (0.52% fund charge, page 5) and the switch charges table (0.18% current annual recurring charge, page 11) are materially inconsistent; the comparison resting on EA5 cost figures is therefore unreliable, as confirmed by the derived figures which show the EA5 charge arithmetic is computed against Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's £3,002.
      - No analysis of the Zurich policy's Lifestage/lifestyle investment approach [P17 for Aviva; P1 notes lifestyle questions for Zurich] or any auto-switching feature that would be lost on transfer is addressed in the report.
    For Aviva and Zurich, the provider documentation affirmatively confirms the absence of safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates, guaranteed minimum pensions, and protected tax-free cash, corroborating the report's assertions for those two plans. For Scottish Widows (being encashed), the same is confirmed. For Standard Life, the absence of safeguarded benefits is confirmed by [P4], which is the most important corroboration. However, [P23] introduces a material feature not addressed by the report: Standard Life applies a Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount of 0.4800%) that 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a financially significant feature — it reduces the effective ongoing charge — and the report neither mentions it nor explains why its loss is acceptable or how it is offset. This is a disclosure shortfall and a missing component in the replacement analysis for EA5. The EA5 cost comparison is also arithmetically unreliable (the derived figures confirm the charge rows for EA5 are computed against Zurich's £103,439 fund value rather than Standard Life's £3,002), meaning the cost justification for the Standard Life switch is doubly compromised. The guard requires that an explicit 'no safeguarded benefits' statement must be corroborated by policy documentation — that corroboration exists for all plans. However, the guard also notes that absence of guarantees in a policy summary is not proof of absence of all valuable features; [P23] demonstrates exactly that a valuable feature (the leaver discount) exists that the report ignores. The vulnerability overlay does not add a specific concern here beyond the general requirement to ensure the client is not disadvantaged by undisclosed feature loss. Overall: the safeguarded-benefits claim is corroborated, but the Standard Life leaver discount constitutes an unaddressed lost feature, rendering the replacement analysis for EA5 incomplete. This is a Potential Concern.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report considers four categories of alternative for the switching plans: (1) Leave existing Aviva pension untouched (Do Nothing) — rejected because funds are in lifestyling funds that will automatically reduce risk below the agreed profile and the plan does not allow adviser management. (2) Internal fund switch within existing Aviva plan — rejected because the plan does not allow adviser servicing and lifestyling funds remain available. (3) Internal fund switch within Zurich — rejected because no multi-asset funds are available within risk range 5 at Zurich, meaning a blend of risk level 7 and risk level 2 funds (Managed AP and Interest Deposits) would be required, reducing diversification; the blended funds have not performed as well as the Aviva options; and the client's main priority is consolidation. (4) Stakeholder pension — rejected case-wide because the recommended solution is more cost effective and stakeholder pensions do not allow flexible access and have limited fund ranges. (5) Investment pathway — rejected because the client requires a more tailored solution than the four generic options, it does not reflect individual circumstances, and performance has been inferior. No alternative to switching is considered specifically for Standard Life (EA5). The Aviva and Zurich Do Nothing and In-Situ alternatives are addressed plan-specifically. The stakeholder and investment pathway alternatives are addressed case-wide.
    File:     The Defaqto pension switching report [G] confirms that three plans are being switched: Aviva (EA3), Zurich (EA4) and Standard Life (EA5). The report provides projected maturity value comparisons for each existing plan against the new solution. The policy research file [P14] confirms no guaranteed annuity rates, no GMP and no guaranteed growth rates for any of the switching plans. The Aviva policy info [P4] confirms the plan does not allow adviser charging. The Zurich policy info [P8] confirms fund switching at no charge is available. No evidence document addresses or corroborates consideration of alternatives specific to Standard Life. The case-wide alternatives (stakeholder, investment pathway) are contained only in the suitability report itself (category I passages).
    Discrepancies:
      - No plan-specific alternative analysis is documented for Standard Life (EA5) — neither a Do Nothing option nor an In-Situ switch option is considered for that plan in the report or in any supporting evidence.
      - The stakeholder pension and investment pathway rejections are generic and not plan-specific; they are contained only in the suitability report (category I) with no corroborating evidence from category E or G documents.
      - The Do Nothing and In-Situ alternatives are considered only for Aviva (EA3) and Zurich (EA4), leaving Standard Life (EA5) without any alternative-consideration narrative.
    The report addresses alternatives for two of the three switching plans (Aviva EA3 and Zurich EA4) with plan-specific reasoning grounded in identifiable plan characteristics: lifestyling risk and no adviser servicing for Aviva; fund range limitations and diversification concerns for Zurich. These are material, client-specific reasons and are corroborated in part by the Aviva policy document [P4] confirming no adviser charging. The Zurich in-situ alternative rejection is supported by the Defaqto report's fund analysis. However, Standard Life (EA5) has no plan-specific alternative consideration at all — no Do Nothing narrative, no In-Situ switch narrative — despite being a separate, materially distinct plan (different charges, different fund profile, different provider). The lifestyling and adviser-servicing rationale given for Aviva does not straightforwardly apply to Standard Life, which has 300 funds, allows adviser servicing, and has a different charge structure. The case-wide guard (options assessed once for the whole case may legitimately cover several plans) applies only where the rejection reasoning is plan-specific for materially different plans; Standard Life is materially different from Aviva and Zurich in fund range (300 funds), adviser servicing capability, and charge structure. The case-wide stakeholder and investment pathway rejections are generic and sourced solely from the suitability report (category I), providing no independent corroboration. The Standard Life value (£3,002) is small relative to the overall transfer (£110,185), which limits the materiality of the gap, but the omission remains a missing component of the alternative analysis for that plan. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would specifically bear on this requirement beyond what is addressed by the substantive alternative analysis. The Standard Life omission is a Missing Component concern of Low severity given the relatively small fund value involved, but it is a real gap.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Three pensions are being switched (Aviva EA3, Zurich EA4, Standard Life EA5) into an Aviva platform solution. Exit penalties are nil for all three. The switch charges table (page 11) shows: Aviva EA3 -0.06% / -£2.25 saving; Zurich EA4 -0.49% / -£506.85 saving; Standard Life EA5 +0.26% / +£268.94 more expensive under new solution. Benefits stated include lower costs, lifestyling risk removal, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset fund alignment and enhanced retirement flexibility. Critical yields are 1.9%, 4.8% and 7.8% at low/mid/high growth. The page 8 narrative for Standard Life states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution — directly contradicting the table figure of +0.26% (new solution more expensive). The page 5 existing arrangements table shows Zurich charges as 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin, but the switch charges table records Zurich existing annual recurring charge as 0.93%. Standard Life is shown as 0.52% fund charge on page 5 but 0.18% existing recurring charge on page 11. The EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% arithmetically implies a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not £3,002 (Standard Life's value), meaning the EA5 cost row in the comparison table is computed on the wrong plan's fund value. The EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% likewise implies Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's.
    File:     The Defaqto pension switch report (category G) provides the independent switching analysis. For the overall position (all three switched), the report shows: existing pension RIY 0.3%, new solution RIY 2.1-2.3% across growth scenarios (Zurich-only table [P7]). The Zurich-only comparison shows projected values at mid growth: existing £127,000 vs new solution £115,000 — a £12,000 / 9.4% decrease [P7]. At low growth: existing £108,000 vs new £97,600 — £10,400 / 9.6% decrease [P7]. At high growth: existing £150,000 vs new £135,000 — £15,000 / 10% decrease [P7]. Critical yields: 1.9% (low), 4.8% (mid), 7.8% (high) [P7]. Standard Life only comparison: low growth existing £2,910 vs new £2,900; critical yield 6.0% [P6, P5]. Aviva only: reduction in yield 0.6% both sides; critical yield 5.9% high growth [P8]. Immediate impact: existing pension £110,185.60, new solution day one £106,880.60 — shortfall of £3,305 [P2, P7]. The switch report does not contain the specific charge figures cited in the suitability report (−0.06%, −£2.25, −0.49%, −£506.85, +0.26%, +£268.94, 0.18% Standard Life existing, 0.93% Zurich existing). The Defaqto report confirms independent switch modelling exists but the Zurich comparison shows the new solution underperforms the existing plan at every growth rate, with the new solution RIY materially higher (0.3% existing vs 2.1-2.3% new).
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. Standard Life EA5 narrative (page 8) states existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than recommended; switch charges table (page 11) shows new solution is 0.26% MORE expensive than existing — direction of cost comparison is contradicted within the report itself.
      - 2. EA5 existing annual charge: £186.19 at 0.18% arithmetically implies a fund of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's £3,002 — the EA5 cost row is computed on the wrong plan's fund value, rendering the comparison unreliable.
      - 3. EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% likewise implies Zurich's fund value (~£103,439), not Standard Life's £3,002 — same wrong-fund-value error.
      - 4. Zurich existing charge shown as 0.18% + £2.12/month on page 5 but 0.93% total annual recurring charge in the switch charges table on page 11 — materially inconsistent and unreconciled.
      - 5. Standard Life existing charge shown as 0.52% on page 5 but 0.18% in the switch charges table on page 11 — directly contradictory.
      - 6. The Defaqto switch report shows the new solution underperforms the existing Zurich plan at every growth rate scenario (e.g. mid growth: existing £127,000 vs new £115,000, a £12,000 / 9.4% decrease), with new solution RIY of 2.1-2.3% against existing 0.3% — a material cost increase that the suitability report's charge table claims is a saving of -0.49%.
      - 7. The suitability report's charge figures (−0.06%, −£2.25, −0.49%, −£506.85, +0.26%, +£268.94) are not corroborated by any passage in the evidence file; none of these specific values appear in the retrieved G-category documents.
      - 8. Immediate fund value impact: existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day-one £106,880.60 — a £3,305 shortfall disclosed in the switch report [P2/P7] but not addressed in the suitability report's cost/benefit analysis.
      - 9. The report's own figures show EA5 (Standard Life, £3,002) is being switched to a plan that costs 0.26% more per annum (per the table), yet the narrative states it is cheaper — a direct internal contradiction that is not resolved.
      - 10. Critical yield for the Zurich plan at mid growth is 4.8%, which exceeds the mid growth rate of 2.95% stated in the report, meaning the new solution is projected to underperform the existing Zurich plan under the mid growth scenario — a material finding the report does not adequately address.
    This requirement fails on multiple, compounding grounds. First, there is a direct internal contradiction in the Standard Life (EA5) section: the page 8 narrative claims the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the switch charges table on page 11 shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive. These cannot both be correct and the directional error means the cost justification for switching Standard Life is unreliable. Second, the derived figures confirm that the EA5 cost rows in the switch charges table are arithmetically computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's £3,002 — the comparison is therefore built on the wrong denominator and is not a valid like-for-like analysis for Standard Life. Third, the Zurich charge figures are materially inconsistent between the existing arrangements table (0.18% + £2.12/month) and the switch charges table (0.93%), and Standard Life's charges are contradicted between 0.52% (page 5) and 0.18% (page 11). These inconsistencies mean the charge comparison cannot be relied upon. Fourth, and most materially, the independent Defaqto switch report (G-category evidence) shows that for the Zurich plan — by far the largest ceding plan at £103,439 — the new solution underperforms at every growth rate scenario, with RIY of 2.1–2.3% against the existing 0.3%, producing projected shortfalls of £10,400 to £15,000. The critical yield at mid growth (4.8%) exceeds the assumed mid growth rate (2.95%), meaning outperformance is not projected to be achieved. The suitability report asserts a -0.49% cost saving for the Zurich switch, which directly contradicts the Defaqto evidence showing the new solution is substantially more expensive in terms of reduction in yield. The non-financial benefits stated (investment choice, adviser charging, monitoring, lifestyling risk) are not client-specific for the most part and do not include a quantified or evidenced analysis of why they justify a material cost increase for Zurich. The immediate impact of a £3,305 day-one reduction in fund value is noted in the switch report but not addressed in the suitability report. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would further aggravate or mitigate these findings. The non-financial benefits guard applies to some qualitative benefits but cannot excuse the direct contradiction in cost direction for Standard Life or the absence of corroboration for the Zurich cost saving claim, which is contradicted by the independent evidence. This is a high-severity concern: the largest ceding plan (Zurich) has a cost/benefit analysis that is contradicted by the independent switch report, and the Standard Life analysis contains an internal directional error compounded by a wrong-fund-value arithmetic error.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states that all five existing arrangements have no exit penalties ('No charges for transfer and/or switching'). The report is silent on safeguarded benefits — the extraction confirms /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent from the document, not merely from the extraction.
    File:     Scottish Widows (EA2, the retained plan, ZU4225575): the provider letter at [P13] confirms 'there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan' and that Market Value Adjustment/Reduction are not applicable. The policy research file [P12] shows No MVR for all five plans. The fact find [P14] shows GMP Amount, Enhanced Tax Free Cash, Guaranteed Annuity Rate/Pension, Applicable Penalties, EFI/Loyalty Bonus/Terminal Bonus and Guaranteed Growth Rates all left blank/unpopulated for the plans recorded. The pension switch report [P2] lists the three ceding plans (Aviva EA3, Zurich EA4, Standard Life EA5) and provides a comparison table and alternative analysis but contains no reference to safeguarded benefits or guaranteed annuity rates for any of them. No provider documentation for Aviva (EA3) or Standard Life (EA5) confirming absence of safeguarded benefits is present in the evidence pack. A Zurich policy research entry [P6] shows no MVR but does not specifically address guaranteed annuity rates or protected benefits.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report does not address safeguarded benefits or guarantees at all — it asserts only that there are no exit penalties, which is a different matter.
      - No provider-level confirmation that Aviva (EA3) holds no guaranteed annuity rate, protected tax-free cash, GMP or other safeguarded benefit is present in the evidence pack.
      - No provider-level confirmation that Standard Life (EA5) holds no guaranteed annuity rate, protected tax-free cash, GMP or other safeguarded benefit is present in the evidence pack.
      - The fact find [P14] leaves all safeguarded-benefit fields (GMP, Enhanced TFC, GAR, Guaranteed Growth Rates) blank rather than populated with confirmed nil values, so the absence of guarantees is not evidenced for any of the three ceding plans.
      - Scottish Widows (EA2) is the only ceding/retained plan for which the provider has explicitly confirmed no guarantees — but EA2 is retained, not switched, so this confirmation does not cover the plans actually being transferred.
    The guard states this requirement only applies where G9.3 identified a benefit actually being given up. However, the question here is whether the report has demonstrated that no such benefits are being given up — i.e. whether the claim of no lost guarantees is supported by evidence. The report is entirely silent on safeguarded benefits; it addresses only exit penalties. Silence is not the same as a confirmed nil. For EA2 (Scottish Widows, retained), [P13] provides explicit provider confirmation of no guarantees — but EA2 is not being switched, so this confirmation is beside the point for the replacement analysis. For the three ceding plans (Aviva EA3, Zurich EA4, Standard Life EA5), no provider documentation confirming the absence of guaranteed annuity rates, protected tax-free cash, GMP or other safeguarded benefits appears in the pack. The policy research [P6] records no MVR for Zurich but MVR is not the same as a guaranteed annuity rate or other safeguarded benefit. The fact find [P14] leaves all relevant fields blank rather than confirmed nil. The Defaqto pension switch report [P2] covers charges and projected values but does not address safeguarded benefits. The absence of provider-level confirmation for Aviva and Standard Life means the report's implicit claim that no valuable features are being lost is unsupported by the evidence. This is a disclosure shortfall and a missing evidence concern. Given that two of the three ceding plans (Aviva EA3 at £3,744 and Standard Life EA5 at £3,002) are small-value plans where the risk of material safeguarded benefits is lower, and Zurich (EA4 at £103,439) is the largest — and the policy research for Zurich shows no MVR but no explicit GAR/GMP confirmation — the severity is moderate rather than high, as there is partial (though not complete) evidence. However the report's total silence on the topic, without any provider confirmation for two of the three ceding plans, constitutes a Potential Concern.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report considers five options for the switching pensions: (1) leave existing Aviva/Zurich/Standard Life untouched — rejected because lifestyling funds will reduce risk below agreed profile and no adviser servicing is possible; (2) internal fund switch within existing Aviva — rejected because the plan does not allow adviser servicing; (3) internal fund switch within Zurich — rejected because no multi-asset funds exist at risk level 5, the available blend uses a single-asset fund (Interest Deposits) which reduces diversification, and blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options; (4) switch existing schemes into employer's pension (People's Pension) — rejected because funds do not meet client's risk profile, People's Pension is 0.06% more expensive than recommended, has a £4.50 annual admin charge, and limited fund range with no multi-asset options; employer contributions would be lost on transfer; (5) new plan recommended (Aviva Pension Portfolio) as Option 5. A stakeholder pension alternative is rejected as less cost-effective, lacking flexible access and limited fund range. Investment pathways are rejected as insufficiently tailored for the client's individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile, and because performance has not matched recommended strategy. The report also considers Option 3 — consolidating into an existing pension — which is implicitly addressed through the employer scheme analysis. People's Pension is retained. Scottish Widows (£211.32) is retained and encashed.
    File:     The pension switch report (category G) provides plan-specific alternative comparisons for each of the three switched plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life), each showing projected maturity values at low, mid and high growth rates for both the existing pension and new solution, plus a stakeholder pension comparison table showing the new solution is more cost-effective. The overall comparison table shows the new solution produces lower projected values (£115,000 at mid vs £127,000 existing) when all three are consolidated, with a reduction in yield rising from 0.3% existing to 2.2% new solution at mid-growth when Zurich-only is considered — indicating the switch carries a material cost. The Zurich-only comparison at mid-growth shows new solution projects £125,000 vs existing £127,000 (£2,000 or 1.6% decrease). The Aviva-only comparison shows new solution projects £4,260 vs existing £4,280 (£20 or 0.5% decrease). The Standard Life-only comparison shows new solution projects similar values. The switch report states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' (P14), indicating no safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features are identified as lost. Zurich policy documentation (P9) explicitly states: 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' The existing arrangements table (P19) shows People's Pension has only FAD and partial UFPLS retirement options and no adviser servicing, Zurich has only UFPLS and no adviser servicing, Standard Life has FAD and no details of UFPLS. Research data (P18) provides flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS availability across providers. The switch report rankings are visible from the alternative comparisons sections in P5, P8, P3 — showing the new solution consistently projects lower maturity values than the individual existing pensions in isolation, with the Zurich comparison being the most material (£2,000–£3,000 lower at mid/high growth). The report does not identify any alternative that produces a better outcome by projected value; the benefits of switching are presented as qualitative (adviser servicing, fund flexibility, consolidation, drawdown access). The internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction — that the Standard Life switch charges table shows the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life (contradicting the narrative that Standard Life is 0.08% more expensive) — is directly relevant to whether the cost justification for switching Standard Life holds.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (lower cost is a stated reason for switching), but the pension switch charges table shows the impact as +0.26% — meaning the new solution is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life, contradicting the direction of the cost argument for this plan.
      - The derived figures show EA5 annual charge is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89 implied by 0.18% producing £186.19) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002.00), making the Standard Life charge comparison in the switch table unreliable — the 0.26% impact figure is computed on the wrong base.
      - The report claims the recommended solution is 'more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension' but no external evidence corroborates this — it is supported only by the switch report (category G) which was produced as part of the same advice process.
      - The overall three-plan comparison at mid-growth shows new solution projects £115,000 vs existing £127,000 (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease), meaning the switch produces materially lower projected values; the report does not address this shortfall in the alternatives analysis.
      - The report's alternatives analysis addresses 'Option 3 — switch into another existing pension arrangement' only generically through the employer scheme (People's Pension) rejection, without analysing whether any of the three existing pensions (e.g. Zurich, the largest by value) could serve as a consolidation vehicle.
      - Mid-growth rate label: switch report appendix uses 2.95% (P5, P16) while suitability report narrative states 2.94% — minor but a documented internal inconsistency.
      - Performance chart captions in the suitability report (P15) assign letters A=Aviva and D=Zurich for performance, but the volatility chart caption swaps these assignments — creating ambiguity about which fund's volatility data is being presented to the client.
    The requirement is to assess whether alternatives to the recommended switches were considered and properly justified, and whether those justifications are supported by evidence.
    
    The report considers a range of alternatives — do nothing, in-situ fund switch (per ceding plan), consolidate into employer scheme, stakeholder pension, investment pathways — and provides plan-specific reasons for rejection. This is a materially complete alternatives framework.
    
    However, there are two substantive concerns:
    
    1. Standard Life cost justification is contradicted and computed on the wrong base. The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', which is offered as a reason to switch. The pension switch charges table at page 11 shows the impact as +0.26% — in the opposite direction (new solution is more expensive). The extraction flags this as a direct contradiction. Furthermore, the derived figures confirm that the EA5 charge row is calculated using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the entire Standard Life cost comparison in the switch table arithmetically unreliable. This means the cost-saving rationale for switching Standard Life (the smallest plan at £3,002) is unsupported and internally contradicted. The other reasons given (lifestyling, adviser servicing, investment choice) are client-specific and plausible, but the lead justification — lower cost — is contradicted by the report's own data.
    
    2. The overall switch produces materially lower projected values. The consolidated comparison at mid-growth shows £115,000 (new) vs £127,000 (existing), a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. The Zurich-only comparison shows £125,000 vs £127,000. The report does address that the switch results in higher charges (the critical yield analysis and effect-of-switching rows make this plain), and the qualitative benefits — adviser servicing, fund consolidation, drawdown access, diversification — are genuine and client-specific. The Zurich policy document (P9) confirms no safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates, or GMP apply, so no valuable features are being lost on the Zurich transfer. The switch report states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' for all three plans. These qualitative justifications are supported by category E and G evidence. The consolidation rationale is a valid client objective (the client wanted to consolidate pensions — see CHK-002 guard). So the projected value shortfall, while material, is addressed by the qualitative case.
    
    The dominant concern is the Standard Life cost contradiction. The plan is small (£3,002) so the practical impact on the client is limited, but the report's cost rationale for switching it is directly contradicted by the switch report's own charges table, and the underlying arithmetic is wrong because the charge was computed on Zurich's fund value. This is a Data inconsistency that also weakens the justification for that element of the recommendation.
    
    The volatility chart label swap (P15) and the 2.94%/2.95% discrepancy are minor presentation issues and do not affect the substance of the alternatives analysis.
    
    Option 3 (consolidate into an existing pension) is addressed only via the employer scheme rejection. No analysis is presented of whether the Zurich plan (by far the largest at £103,439) could have served as a consolidation vehicle for the other two. This is a missing component in the alternatives analysis, though a moderate one given that the Zurich plan itself does not allow adviser servicing and has limited fund options, reasons which implicitly apply.
    
    Overall: the alternatives framework is substantive and mostly supported by evidence, but the Standard Life cost argument is materially contradicted by the report's own data, and the Option 3 in-situ consolidation analysis is incomplete. These are Potential Concerns at moderate severity — the Standard Life plan is small and other reasons for switching it exist, but the contradiction is genuine and the missing Option 3 analysis for consolidation into an existing plan is a gap.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report makes plan-specific cost/benefit justifications for three switches. For Aviva (EA3): lower costs (existing plan 0.06% more expensive than recommended), lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. Net benefit conclusion: lower costs, greater investment flexibility, continued professional oversight. For Zurich (EA4): greater investment choice, multi-asset fund alignment (Zurich cannot offer risk level 5 in a multi-asset fund; blend of risk 7 and risk 2 used), enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. Net benefit conclusion: greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, continued professional oversight. For Standard Life (EA5): lower costs (plan itself 0.08% more expensive than recommended), lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. Net benefit conclusion: lower costs, greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, continued professional oversight. The switch charges table (page 11) shows: Aviva -0.06% / -£2.25 (saving); Zurich -0.49% / -£506.85 (saving); Standard Life +0.26% / +£268.94 (new plan MORE expensive). The critical yield narrative (page 11/18) states mid-growth rate of 2.94%/2.95% and that the plan needs to grow at 4.8% p.a. to match the existing plan. The report asserts this is achievable based on past fund performance.
    File:     [P13] (Pension switch report summary) confirms the three plans being switched (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) to an Aviva SIPP. [P14] (suitability report, switch charges table) shows Standard Life existing annual recurring charge 0.18%, impact of recommended alternative +0.26%, +£268.94 — the new plan is more expensive for Standard Life. [P15] (suitability report, Standard Life recommendation section) states 'Lower Costs — The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', which directionally contradicts the switch charges table. [P4] (Pension switch report, Zurich-only alternative comparison) shows that switching Zurich alone produces a negative effect at all growth rates (e.g. mid growth: existing £127,000 vs new solution £115,000; effect of switching -£12,000 or -9.4% decrease; critical yield 4.8%). [P8] (Pension switch report, new solution comparison ranking) shows the new solution ranks 5th out of top 10 (£125,000 projected maturity value at mid growth), behind the existing solution (2nd, £127,000), Zurich alone (1st, £128,000), Aviva alone (3rd, £126,000) and Standard Life alone (4th, £126,000). [P3] (Pension switch report, alternative comparisons) shows that retaining Zurich and switching only Aviva/Standard Life produces lower projected maturity than retaining all. [P12] (Pension switch report appendix) notes the client's agreed risk level is 5 and that Zurich fund data shows as N/A. [P5] (Zurich policy info) confirms fund switch charge £0.00 and no transfer value deduction. [P9] (Zurich policy info, transfer considerations checklist) flags questions about guaranteed annuity rates, fund choices and charge comparisons but the completed checkboxes are not shown. [P2] (Pension switch report) confirms immediate day-one shortfall of £3,305 (existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day-one £106,880.60). [P18] (suitability report, critical yield narrative) states mid-growth rate 2.94% and required growth of 4.8%. [P1] (Pension switch report) documents the overall switching analysis framework.
    Discrepancies:
      - Standard Life (EA5) cost direction contradiction: the recommendation narrative (page 8/[P15]) states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution (i.e. a saving), but the switch charges table (page 11/[P14]) shows the impact of the recommended alternative as +0.26% / +£268.94 — meaning the new plan is MORE expensive than Standard Life by £268.94 per year. The report's stated justification of 'lower costs' for Standard Life is directly contradicted by the report's own switch charges table.
      - EA5 charge arithmetic confirms wrong-plan calculation: derived figures show that EA5's existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and the £268.94 impact at 0.26% both imply a fund value of ~£103,438 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's value of £3,002. The Standard Life cost comparison in the switch charges table appears to have been computed on Zurich's fund value, making it unreliable.
      - The new solution ranks 5th in the Defaqto comparison (mid-growth £125,000), below the existing solution (2nd, £127,000) and all three individual existing plans retained (Aviva 3rd £126,000, Standard Life 4th £126,000, Zurich 1st £128,000), yet the report does not address why a solution delivering lower projected maturity is in the client's best interests.
      - For Zurich (EA4), the pension switch report shows the effect of switching at mid growth is -£12,000 / -9.4% decrease with a critical yield of 4.8%. The report asserts this is achievable based on past performance but provides no specific fund performance data or evidence that 4.8% growth is reliably achievable, particularly given Zurich's fund data in the switch report shows as N/A.
      - The report claims 'Lower Costs' as the first justification for the Standard Life switch in the net benefit conclusion, but the switch charges table shows a cost increase of £268.94 p.a. The net benefit conclusion for Standard Life says 'lower costs' — a claim that is false per the report's own table.
      - The report claims lifestyling as a reason for switching Standard Life but no evidence in the file (category E or G) confirms Standard Life's fund is a lifestyling fund.
      - The report claims 'Greater Investment Choice' as a benefit for all three switches but no fund count comparison between existing and recommended plans is provided in the file to quantify this claim.
      - Mid-growth rate stated as 2.94% in narrative [P18] but appendix table [P17] labels it 2.95% — a minor internal inconsistency.
      - Zurich existing arrangement charge stated as 0.18% fund + £2.12/month on page 5 of the suitability report, but the switch charges table (page 11/[P14]) states 0.93% as the current annual recurring charge — these are materially inconsistent, undermining the reliability of the Zurich cost-saving claim of -0.49%.
    This requirement asks whether claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits are supported by evidence. The guard specifies that where the recommended plan is cheaper, this requirement does not bite — but it bites squarely where it is more expensive.
    
    Standard Life (EA5) is the clearest and most serious problem. The report's own switch charges table ([P14]) shows the new plan is +0.26% / +£268.94 per year MORE expensive than Standard Life. Yet the recommendation narrative ([P15]) claims 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' — a directly contradictory directional claim. The net benefit conclusion also states 'lower costs' as a benefit of switching Standard Life. The derived figures confirm that the EA5 charge rows in the switch charges table were computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,438), not Standard Life's fund value (£3,002), so the entire cost comparison for EA5 is unreliable. The report does not address or justify the cost increase for Standard Life with any specific benefit analysis beyond generic claims (greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, lifestyling) that are not individually evidenced for this plan. No category E or G document provides plan-specific evidence supporting these claims for Standard Life.
    
    For Zurich (EA4), the switch charges table shows a saving of -0.49% / -£506.85 at the plan level, so the 'cheaper' guard partially applies. However, the independent Defaqto pension switch report ([P4], [P8]) shows the new solution produces lower projected maturity values than retaining Zurich alone at every growth rate (mid: -£12,000 / -9.4% decrease), and the new solution ranks 5th overall while Zurich alone ranks 1st. The report addresses this through the critical yield narrative, asserting 4.8% growth is achievable based on past fund performance, but provides no specific performance evidence — the Zurich fund data in the switch report ([P12]) shows as N/A. The justification that multi-asset fund alignment and better fund performance outweigh the projected maturity shortfall is made by assertion, without corroborating evidence from categories E or G.
    
    For Aviva (EA3), the switch shows a saving of -0.06% / -£2.25, so cost is lower and this limb does not bite materially for EA3.
    
    The inconsistency between the Zurich charges on page 5 (0.18% + £2.12/month) and the switch charges table (0.93%) is flagged as an internal inconsistency that undermines the reliability of the Zurich cost-saving figure.
    
    On vulnerability: the extraction notes no vulnerability assessment was present in the document (/vulnerability/C1/assessment NotPresentInDocument). No vulnerability factors are identified in the retrieved passages that would materially alter this finding.
    
    In summary: the Standard Life switch is justified on 'lower costs' when the report's own table shows higher costs, the cost calculation for Standard Life appears to have used the wrong fund value, and the benefits claimed are not corroborated by any category E or G evidence. The Zurich switch relies on a critical yield of 4.8% being achievable with no evidential support. These are material concerns.
