  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report recommends switching Aviva (EA3), Zurich (EA4) and Standard Life (EA5) into an Aviva Platform SIPP, encashing Scottish Widows (EA2) under small pots rules, and retaining People's Pension (EA1). The stated rationale for switching EA3 (Aviva) is lifestyling moving the portfolio outside the agreed risk range, EA3 being 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution, and lack of adviser management. For EA4 (Zurich) the rationale is higher cost (0.93% existing vs 0.44% new, saving 0.49%), limited fund range for risk rating 5, and lack of adviser management. For EA5 (Standard Life) the rationale is that the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, limited retirement options (FAD only), and lack of adviser management. For EA2 (Scottish Widows) the rationale is that it does not support ongoing adviser charging, lacks flexible retirement options, and the fund qualifies under small pots rules (estimated £170 net). EA1 (People's Pension) is retained because employer contributions are ongoing. Net benefit conclusions include lower costs, greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, and continued professional oversight.
    File:     [P8] Aviva policy info confirms plan SP50163762 is invested in 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2', has a Lifestage Investment Approach (auto-switching), no guaranteed rates and no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, plan value and transfer value both £3,744.36, retirement age 70. [P14/P10] Scottish Widows confirms no guarantees, discounts or bonuses, Market Value Adjustment not applicable, and that Money4Life Plans do not support ongoing advisor charging or transferring of Servicing Rights. [P3] Zurich confirms that 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan' and that 'Drawdown options are not available on this plan; however, it can be transferred to another provider to do this.' [P9] Standard Life confirms retirement options including annuity and transfer, and that no guaranteed rates apply. [P21] The existing arrangements comparison table in the suitability report itself shows Scottish Widows retirement options as 'Full and partial UFPLS' and Standard Life as 'FAD' only. [P5/P20/P6] The Defaqto pension switch report confirms analysis was performed, compares existing plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) to the new solution, and shows the new solution ranks 5th of 10 by projected maturity value at the medium growth rate (£125,000 vs £127,000 for existing solution and £128,000 for Zurich alone). The stakeholder pension comparison shows the new solution outperforms the generic stakeholder pension. [P16] File notes confirm alternatives were considered and discounted: Aviva internal switch discounted as only mixed asset fund available is risk rated 4; Zurich internal switch discounted as no risk level 5 funds available, blend of funds has not performed as well; Standard Life discounted as all funds within same range more expensive than Aviva offer. [P17] File notes confirm current fund risk ratings: Aviva risk 2, Zurich risk 7, Standard Life risk 4. The report's charge comparison table (P22/I) shows: Aviva existing 0.50%/£18.72, new impact -0.06%; Zurich existing 0.93%/£961.98, new impact -0.49%/saving £504.66; Standard Life existing 0.18%/£186.19, new impact +0.26%/£268.94 more expensive. The derived figures confirm EA5 existing annual charge at 0.18% implies a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002), so the Standard Life charge row is computed on the wrong fund value.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. Standard Life switch cost direction contradiction: the report narrative (page 8) states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. existing Standard Life is more expensive), but the switch charges table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative as +0.26% (£268.94), meaning the new solution is 0.26% MORE expensive than the Standard Life existing plan — the direction of the cost comparison is directly contradicted within the report itself.
      - 2. Standard Life charge basis error: the derived figures confirm that the EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's current value of £3,002. The charge arithmetic is computed on the wrong fund, making the entire Standard Life cost comparison unreliable.
      - 3. Zurich existing charge inconsistency: page 5 of the report states Zurich's charge as '0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin', implying approximately 0.18% plus fixed admin, but the switch charges table on page 11 states Zurich's current annual recurring charge as 0.93%. These are materially inconsistent and cannot both be correct as a like-for-like total charge, though the derived figures confirm 0.93% at £961.98 implies Zurich's fund value, suggesting 0.93% may be the correct total.
      - 4. New solution ranks lower than existing in projected maturity value: the Defaqto switch report (P6) shows the existing solution projects to £127,000 at medium growth and the new solution to £125,000 — the new solution produces a lower projected maturity value. The report's net benefit claim of 'lower costs' for Standard Life is contradicted by the switch table showing the new solution is more expensive for EA5.
      - 5. Scottish Widows retirement options: the report's comparison table (P21) lists Scottish Widows retirement options as 'Full and partial UFPLS', but the report also states that transferring all pensions into Scottish Widows 'would limit how you can withdraw funds' and that the client 'would be unable to access this pension flexibly' — this may be consistent if the concern is about consolidating everything there, but the encashment rationale relies on flexible access not being available, whereas UFPLS is a form of flexible access.
      - 6. Critical yield mid growth rate: page 11 narrative states 2.94% and page 18 appendix states 2.95% — minor 0.01% discrepancy noted internally.
      - 7. The report states lower costs as a benefit of switching Standard Life, but the switch charges table shows the new solution costs 0.26% more per annum for that plan — the claimed cost advantage is directly contradicted by the report's own table.
    The central concern for G9.9 is whether the evidence in the file supports or contradicts the replacement rationale. Several material contradictions exist.
    
    First, and most seriously, the Standard Life (EA5) cost rationale is contradicted on multiple levels. The report narrative states the existing Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, which is stated as a reason to switch. However the report's own switch charges table shows the recommended alternative has an impact of +0.26% (£268.94), meaning the new solution is 0.26% MORE expensive than the Standard Life existing plan. The direction is reversed. Furthermore, the derived figures confirm that the £186.19 charge at 0.18% used for EA5 in the switch table implies a fund value of ~£103,439 — Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's £3,002. The entire Standard Life charge row is arithmetically computed on the wrong fund. The cost justification for the Standard Life switch is therefore both internally contradicted (narrative vs table) and arithmetically wrong. The alternative justifications for the EA5 switch (FAD-only retirement options, lack of adviser management) are present, and P9 supports that Standard Life's options are limited, but the primary cost rationale is unsupported and contradicted.
    
    Second, for Zurich (EA4), the cost rationale is supported — P3 confirms no drawdown, the switch table shows a 0.49% saving (£504.66), and the derived figures confirm the 0.93% existing charge is computed on Zurich's fund value correctly. However, the charge stated on page 5 (0.18% + £2.12/month admin) is inconsistent with the 0.93% total used in the switch table, creating ambiguity about what the correct existing charge is. The 0.93% figure appears arithmetically correct given Zurich's fund value, so the page 5 figure appears to be an error or partial disclosure.
    
    Third, for Aviva (EA3), P8 confirms lifestyling ('Yes, Lifestage Investment Approach'), which supports the rationale. P8 also confirms no guarantees or GMP. The -0.06% cost advantage is minor but directionally correct.
    
    Fourth, for Scottish Widows (EA2), P14 and P10 confirm no ongoing adviser charging and no guarantees, supporting the rationale. However the comparison table in P21 shows Scottish Widows offers 'Full and partial UFPLS', which is a form of flexible access. The report's rationale that the plan 'would limit how you can withdraw funds' applies to the scenario of consolidating everything into Scottish Widows (which only offers UFPLS, not FAD), and the small pots encashment rationale is separately stated. This is internally consistent.
    
    Fifth, P6 (Defaqto) shows the new solution ranks 5th with a projected maturity value of £125,000 vs £127,000 for the existing solution at medium growth. The new solution therefore produces a lower projected maturity value. The report claims benefits of lower costs and greater flexibility, but the net benefit for Standard Life switching is contradicted by the charges table.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: P18 notes an ATR discrepancy (questionnaire result 4, agreed rating 5, and one note references rating 6). This does not directly affect the replacement rationale analysis beyond what is assessed under other checks.
    
    In summary: the Standard Life switch rationale is directly contradicted by the report's own switch charges table (direction of cost comparison is reversed) and the charges are computed on the wrong fund value. This is a material Data inconsistency going to the heart of the cost justification for EA5. The Zurich charge inconsistency between page 5 and page 11 is also a Data inconsistency. The Aviva and Scottish Widows rationales are broadly supported by evidence. The overall net benefit claim of 'lower costs' is undermined by the Standard Life analysis.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts for all three ceding plans (Aviva EA3, Zurich EA4, Standard Life EA5): (1) no safeguarded benefits exist ('This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits'); (2) no exit penalties apply ('No charges for transfer and/or switching'); (3) no employer contributions will be lost (employerContributionsLost = false for all). The extraction notes that featuresOrGuaranteesLost paths for all three plans are absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument), confirming the report makes no disclosure of any features or guarantees that would be lost beyond the generic 'no safeguarded benefits' statement.
    File:     [P11] Zurich policy info discloses that if the plan has capital units an exit charge applies on transfer, reducing by ~0.3–0.4% per month until SRA, capped at 1% from age 55. [P13] Zurich confirms 'There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred or taken. The initial set up charges have already been paid' — but this applies only where there are no capital units; the capital-units caveat in [P11] is unresolved. [P11] also discloses that Zurich does not offer partial UFPLS (only a full UFPLS if one has been taken previously) and cannot take just the 25% tax-free lump sum leaving the remainder for later. [P16] Standard Life confirms no bid-offer spread, 100% allocation, no exit charge, no protected tax-free cash (standard 25% only). [P9] confirms Standard Life value £3,002.02. [P1]–[P8] and [P10] show the People's Pension (EA1, retained) has ongoing employer and employee contributions — the retained plan is not switched, so employer contributions on EA1 are not at risk from this transaction. The Zurich policy ([P11]) shows no current regular payments (£0.00 employer, £0.00 employee), confirming the scheme is not live/active; the historic payments were all protected-rights transfers/former protected rights. [P20] policy research file shows no MVR for any plan. [P22] critical yield report (category G) shows the effect of switching is negative across all growth scenarios (e.g. mid-rate: £12,000 / 9.4% decrease) but is presented as a comparison tool rather than contradicting the switch. [P19] KIID shows an ongoing charge of 0.18% for the recommended fund. No provider documentation in the file positively confirms 'no safeguarded benefits' for Zurich or Aviva.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts no exit penalty for Zurich (EA4) ('No charges for transfer and/or switching'), but [P11] discloses a capital-units exit charge that reduces by ~0.3–0.4% per month and is capped at 1% from age 55 — whether this applies to EA4 is unresolved by the file, and the report makes no reference to it.
      - The report makes no disclosure of any features or guarantees lost beyond the generic 'no safeguarded benefits' statement; [P11] discloses that Zurich cannot offer partial UFPLS or a standalone 25% tax-free lump sum, which are retirement-flexibility features that would be lost on transfer and are not mentioned in the report.
      - The extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost is NotPresentInDocument for all three ceding plans, meaning the report contains no plan-specific disclosure of retirement-option features foregone on switching Zurich.
      - The report asserts no safeguarded benefits for all ceding plans, but no provider documentation corroborates this for Zurich or Aviva — the Standard Life file [P16] is the only one that positively addresses protected tax-free cash ('no Protected Tax Free Cash and the standard 25% will be available').
      - Internal inconsistency in the charges used for Standard Life (EA5): the existing arrangements table states 0.52% fund charge, but the switch charges table states 0.18% current annual recurring charge, and the derived figures show the EA5 annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% is computed on a fund value consistent with Zurich (£103,439) not Standard Life (£3,002) — meaning the cost/benefit comparison for EA5 rests on a materially wrong base value.
      - Internal inconsistency in the charges used for Zurich (EA4): existing arrangements table states 0.18% + £2.12/month admin, but the switch charges table states 0.93% current annual recurring charge — these cannot both be correct as a like-for-like total.
      - The critical yield report [P22] shows the effect of switching is negative at every growth rate (mid: −£12,000/−9.4%; high: −£15,000/−10.0%), yet the report does not prominently address why switching is justified given this outcome — though this is partly addressed by the consolidation rationale.
    Several concerns arise from this requirement. First and most material: the Zurich policy information [P11] discloses a capital-units exit charge (reducing ~0.3–0.4%/month, capped at 1% from age 55) that may apply on transfer. The report flatly states 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' for EA4 (Zurich). [P13] does contain a transfer value and early retirement charge table stating 'There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred' — but this is qualified by [P11]'s capital-units caveat. Whether EA4 holds capital units is not confirmed in the file, so the capital-units exit charge cannot be ruled out. The report makes no acknowledgement of this contingency whatsoever. This is a Disclosure shortfall and a potential Missing component. Second: [P11] discloses that Zurich does not offer partial UFPLS and cannot isolate the 25% tax-free lump sum. These are valuable retirement-flexibility features that are specific to the Zurich contract. The report records 'NotPresentInDocument' for featuresOrGuaranteesLost, confirming the report makes no disclosure of these foregone features. This is a further Disclosure shortfall. Third: the report asserts no safeguarded benefits for all three plans but no category-E or category-G document corroborates this for Zurich (EA4) or Aviva (EA3). Standard Life [P16] does confirm no protected tax-free cash. Absence of corroborating provider documentation for Zurich and Aviva means these assertions are unsupported, not confirmed. Fourth: the charge arithmetic identified in the derived figures shows that the EA5 (Standard Life) cost comparison is computed using a fund value matching Zurich (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's actual value (~£3,002), making the switch charges table internally unreliable for EA5. Combined with the contradictory charge figures for both Zurich and Standard Life already flagged in the extraction's internalInconsistencies, the cost/benefit analysis for these plans contains material data quality issues. The employer contributions point: Zurich [P11] shows £0.00 current employer payments and no regular payments, confirming no active employer contributions are at risk from the Zurich transfer. The People's Pension is retained, so its employer contributions (evidenced in [P1]–[P8]) are not affected. The employer contributions assertions (false) are therefore consistent with the file evidence. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment is present in the canonical model (NotPresentInDocument for /vulnerability/C1/assessment) so no vulnerability-specific overlay adjustment can be made. Overall, the most significant issues are: (a) the capital-units exit charge for Zurich is potentially applicable but completely unaddressed; (b) Zurich-specific retirement-flexibility features (no partial UFPLS, no standalone 25% drawdown) that would be lost on transfer are not disclosed; (c) the safeguarded-benefits assertions for Zurich and Aviva are unsupported by any provider documentation; and (d) the Standard Life and Zurich charge comparisons contain material internal inconsistencies that undermine the reliability of the cost/benefit analysis. These collectively constitute a Potential Concern at High severity, primarily because the largest ceding plan (Zurich, £103,439) has an unresolved exit charge risk and undisclosed retirement-option losses, and the charge comparison table for both EA4 and EA5 is internally inconsistent.

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Discrepancies:
      - The report is entirely silent on vulnerability assessment; the extraction confirms /vulnerability/C1/assessment is NotPresentInDocument — no assessment appears anywhere in the report or file.
      - The risk profile records that John Sullivan has no understanding or knowledge of pension planning or investment products, which is a FG21/1 low-capability driver, but this indicator is not reflected in any vulnerability or support-needs section of the suitability report.
      - No communication preferences, accessible format requirements or literacy considerations are referenced in the suitability report despite the fact find recording a preferred delivery method field (answered 'No Preference').
      - No third-party support, trusted contact, or representative arrangement is recorded or discussed, though no positive evidence of need exists; absence of assessment means this cannot be confirmed as deliberate.
    The canonical model extraction explicitly confirms that /vulnerability/C1/assessment is NotPresentInDocument — this is report silence, not extraction failure. The check applies to all cases. The file contains a clear FG21/1 driver: John Sullivan recorded no understanding or knowledge of pension planning and income in retirement products, and no experience of pension products in the last five years. Under FG21/1 low financial capability is a recognised vulnerability characteristic that should prompt an adviser to consider whether additional support or communication adjustments are needed. No vulnerability screening, no support-needs assessment, no record of communication adjustments and no consideration of third-party support appears in either the suitability report or the supporting file. The fact find records nobody else was present at the meeting and preferred delivery method as 'No Preference', but contains no vulnerability or support-needs section completed with substantive content. The risk profile discussion notes sections for investor experience, capacity for loss, investment target and agreed risk level all state 'No additional notes were recorded', suggesting the process was completed without recording any qualitative discussion that might have surfaced vulnerability indicators. The guard that a spouse present is not automatically third-party support is not triggered here as no spouse was present. The guard distinguishing a future PoA recommendation from current third-party support is noted; the report records PoA not granted and makes no recommendation either way — this is consistent with no current need, but the absence of any assessment means it cannot be confirmed as a considered conclusion. The low-capability indicators in the risk profile are the primary concern: a client with no knowledge of pension products and no investment experience is precisely the profile FG21/1 identifies as potentially requiring additional support or communication adjustments, and the absence of any documented assessment of this is a process and disclosure gap.

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The canonical model holds no value at any vulnerability path. The extraction confirmed /vulnerability/C1/assessment is NotPresentInDocument. The suitability report contains no recorded vulnerability assessment, no identified vulnerability characteristics, no description of impact on the recommendation, and no foreseeable harms analysis. The report does mention Power of Attorney in passing ('It would also be prudent to set up and register Power of Attorney for your financial affairs / health and welfare, to ensure that in the event of your incapacity, your spouse or relative could act on your behalf') but frames this as a general recommendation rather than a response to any identified vulnerability. The report notes John's investment knowledge is limited to workplace pension arrangements and that the questionnaire result of 4 was overridden to 5 by adviser agreement.
    File:     The risk profile report (category D) shows John responded 'No understanding / knowledge' to questions about stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning and income in retirement, and 'No' to having invested in unit trusts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension products in the last five years. This is a low-capability indicator under FG21/1. The fact find (category B) records no vulnerability indicators, no support needs, no accessible format requirements, no third-party authority, no Power of Attorney in place, and no preferred communication method beyond 'No Preference' for delivery method. The fact find declaration page references sensitive personal data including health details but records nothing under that heading. No vulnerability assessment document exists anywhere in the pack. The intelliflo planning scenarios (category F) record illness events for both John and Kim at age 100 as planning assumptions but carry no clinical or vulnerability content. The file notes (category C) contain no vulnerability or support-needs content.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report contains no vulnerability assessment and no impact-on-recommendation analysis; no document in the case file contains one either — the overlay was never applied.
      - The risk profile report records John as having no understanding or knowledge of stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension planning — a low-capability FG21/1 driver — but this characteristic is not reflected anywhere in the suitability report's reasoning or adjustments.
      - The adviser overrode the questionnaire ATR result from 4 to 5 (upward) in a case where the client has stated no understanding of pensions, stocks or investment products; the report does not address this combination in the context of vulnerability or client capability.
      - The Power of Attorney mention in the report is a forward-looking general recommendation, not a record of any existing authority or vulnerability-driven adjustment; no PoA is on file and no trusted contact is recorded.
      - No communication preference, accessible format requirement or literacy consideration is recorded or addressed in the report.
    The extraction confirms /vulnerability/C1/assessment is NotPresentInDocument, and no vulnerability assessment document exists in the case file. This is not an extraction failure — the overlay was simply never applied. The check runs on every advised case regardless of trigger. The case carries at least one clear FG21/1 low-capability driver: the risk profile questionnaire records John as having no understanding of stocks and shares, pension planning, or associated risks, and no relevant investment experience in the last five years. Under FG21/1 low capability is a vulnerability characteristic that requires consideration. The adviser then overrode the client's questionnaire ATR upward (4 to 5) in circumstances where the client acknowledged no understanding of the products involved; the report does not address this combination through a vulnerability or capability lens. The age guard states age alone is not vulnerability and should be combined with reliance, health or capability indicators before concluding — here the capability indicator is present independently of age, so the guard does not suppress the finding. The report's Power of Attorney paragraph is a standard forward-looking recommendation and does not constitute a vulnerability assessment or support arrangement. Because no vulnerability assessment appears anywhere in the case, and a clear FG21/1 low-capability driver is evidenced in the risk profile document, this meets the 'High' severity threshold: no vulnerability assessment in a case with clear FG21/1 drivers.

  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model records hasVulnerability as 'No' for both clients (C1 and C2). The paths for vulnerability categories, characteristics and assessment date are all absent from the extracted model, and the extraction metadata confirms /vulnerability/C1/assessment was 'NotPresentInDocument' — meaning the report is silent on any vulnerability assessment having been conducted.
    File:     The fact find (B) records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement' for the client. The fact find also records the meeting type as 'Video Call - Recorded' with 'No' to anybody else present. The risk profile report (D) notes no additional observations under investor experience or capacity for loss. No vulnerability screening section, health questionnaire or FG21/1 driver checklist appears in any retrieved passage. However, one relevant indicator is present: C1 (Alan) has a date of birth of 07/06/1956 and an age recorded as 70 in the fact find, placing him in an age band commonly associated with FG21/1 vulnerability drivers (older age, proximity to or past retirement). The file notes (C) record that he is an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net and continues working, but also that he is 69–70 years old and consolidating pensions ahead of retirement. No health conditions, cognitive impairment, life events, financial distress or capability concerns are recorded anywhere in the file. The cashflow modeller (F) models illness events for both clients at age 100, which is a default placeholder rather than a recorded health indicator. No Power of Attorney, trusted contact, or third-party involvement is recorded anywhere.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts hasVulnerability = No for both clients but no vulnerability assessment record (date, method, outcome) appears anywhere in the report or the file — the extraction confirms /vulnerability/C1/assessment is NotPresentInDocument.
      - C1 is aged 70 at the time of advice (fact find records DOB 07/06/1956, age 70), an age that is a recognised FG21/1 driver warranting at minimum a documented consideration; no such consideration appears in the report or file notes.
      - The report is silent on how the 'No vulnerability' conclusion was reached — there is no questionnaire result, adviser note or screening tool referenced.
    The report asserts no vulnerability for either client. The file does not record any positive vulnerability indicators such as health conditions, cognitive impairment, recent bereavement, financial distress or low financial capability. In that narrow sense the 'No' conclusion is not contradicted by positive evidence. However, FG21/1 requires firms to consider vulnerability proactively and to document that consideration. C1 is aged 70, past the standard retirement age, and is consolidating multiple pension arrangements — circumstances that at minimum engage the age-related and life-event drivers under FG21/1. The extraction confirms that /vulnerability/C1/assessment is NotPresentInDocument, meaning the report contains no record of a vulnerability assessment having been conducted at all. The file notes (C) and the risk profile (D) are equally silent: the risk profile notes 'No additional notes were recorded' under both investor experience and capacity for loss. The fact find records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement' which shows the question was reached but does not constitute a vulnerability assessment. The absence of any documented assessment process — not merely the absence of a positive finding — is the concern. Where the report asserts hasVulnerability = No without any supporting assessment record, and the file contains an age-related FG21/1 driver that warranted at least documented consideration, the claim is unsupported. The guard noting that age alone is not a vulnerability characteristic is acknowledged: the finding here is not that Alan is vulnerable, but that no assessment was documented despite the driver being present. This is a process and disclosure gap rather than a contradicted data point. Severity is assessed as Moderate: there is no recorded positive vulnerability, but the absence of any documented assessment in a case with a clear FG21/1 age driver, and a report that simply asserts 'No' without evidencing how that was determined, represents a material gap in duty-of-care documentation.
